# Farik Specification

Version 0.2 (draft for review). Owner: project founder. Status: not yet implemented; this document is the contract the first milestone is built against. Revision 0.3 also carries the rules the phase 1 reviews pinned down as the harness was built, each marked where it appears. Revision 0.2 (2026-09-14) adds sections 5.11 to 5.15, requirements F14 to F17, the `locked` and `references` contract fields, and the amendments marked "added in 0.2", all from the planning review recorded in `docs/plans/project-plan.md`. Revision 0.3 (2026-09-15) records the founder's decisions of that day and the move of the backend to Rust (section 8, ADR 0005). Revision 0.4 (2026-09-17) names in section 8.5 the six event kinds phase 2 emits that earlier revisions left unlisted, and records in 8.4 where task ids come from and where they stop. Revision 0.5 (2026-09-17) says in 5.14 what the repository's default branch is when there is no remote to record one, and that one task integrates at a time, both from the phase 2 step 04 reviews. Revision 0.6 (2026-09-17) names in 5.5 and 8.3 the two files phase 2 step 06 gave a place -- the price override and this machine's sandbox choice -- neither of which earlier revisions put anywhere. Revision 0.7 (2026-09-18) says in section 3 what `farik init` writes, the starter team included, which phase 2 step 08 had to decide because no team editor exists until phase 5. It also says what the reading commands of step 09 show, and adds `lock_mismatch` and `contract_unreadable` to the `drift.detected` event in 8.5, which step 07 had added to the store and left the event unable to report. Revision 0.8 (2026-09-22) corrects 8.3, which put git operations inside the container while ADR 0004 runs them on the host through `farik_git`, and says in 5.5 that a day is a UTC day, that a cost is priced when it is recorded, and that a model no price table knows is refused rather than costed at zero. It also says in 8.4 that a task id is never one a contract file in the repository already holds, and that a new contract is refused rather than written over a file already there, from the phase 2 landing review, which found a fresh clone filing its first request over a committed contract. It also names in 8.5 the two event kinds phase 3 step 04 emits that earlier revisions left unlisted: `transition.refused`, which records a move the governor refused with every reason, and `contract.evaluated`, which records each Definition of Ready or Done evaluation. It also says in 8.3 that no-sandbox mode leaves the user's credential files readable, and a push through a wrapped git possible, from any command, and that its warning says so. It also says in section 6 what a role's `role.yaml` holds, and that a role's permission tiers and session limits are not in it but are the governor's defaults, overridden per agent in the team file. It also names in 8.5 the two event kinds phase 3 step 05 emits that earlier revisions left unlisted: `criterion.recorded`, an exit criterion's result as the agent that ran it recorded it, and `note.written`, an assignee's or reviewer's note about the work. It also says in 5.6 that Farik's coordination tools need only `read` and are governed by the rule each names, and in 5.6, 8.2, and 8.3 that git is four tools, one per tier-checked action, rather than one `farik_git`. It also says in 8.2 the order of the sections of a session's system prompt, which of them are marked as untrusted, and how much of each is kept (ADR 0011). It also says in 8.2 what the daemon's `PreToolUse` hook denies, a path outside the session's worktree among it, and that the hook commands fail closed, in 5.6 that no MCP server but Farik's own reaches a session in this release and what the hook cannot keep a search from reading, and in 8.5 what the three `tool.` events record, which phase 3 step 07 emits. It also says in 5.5 how much of the budget table this release enforces, which phase 3 step 11's orchestrator does: costs recorded, a session a budget ran out for aborted, and no session started for a task or a day with no room, while the table's blocks and escalations wait for phase 4. It also says in 5.4 how phase 3 step 12's orchestrator verifies a task: Farik runs the mechanical criteria as the reviewer, the reviewer may neither record them nor commit, no agent records a `human` criterion, Farik files a failed review's rejection with the review note, a task with a `human` criterion or of risk `high` waits for the human, and the reviewer's session has the read-only built-in tools alone. It also says in 5.4 that Farik runs those criteria in a sandbox made afresh for them, what becomes of one it could not run, and that a refused request from a verification's own sessions also waits for the human, and in 5.2 that the governor escalates a task over such a criterion. It also renames the integration policy `local_merge` to `auto_merge`, which a new project now gets and which pushes to `origin`, says in 5.14 how each of the three policies integrates, that a failed integration escalates a task that stays `accepted`, and that one lock serialises integration across processes, and says in 5.15 what recovery records and removes (ADR 0012). It also names in 8.5 `agent.updated`, which phase 3 step 14 emits, and what `question.answered`, `human.accepted`, and `escalation.resolved` carry. It also says in 5.7 that a resolution moves the task to the status the user names with a message, that approving a contract is not a resolution, that the user moves a task with a reason, and how answers and messages reach the next session. It also says in 5.2 that the user's stop aborts the session, and in F1 how a pause blocks an agent's tasks and a return unblocks them. It also says in 5.1 and 5.4 that Farik runs an epic's mechanical criteria for the human who reviews it (ADR 0013), and in 5.16 the triage model, the move from draft to refining, the judgement of a written contract, and how an epic is assigned, broken down, closed out, and accepted. It also says in section 3 which commands drive the team and which reach a process driving it, in 5.7 that the user answers from any terminal and what a driving process says waits on the user when it ends, in 5.13 what `farik contract new` files and how it asks its questions, in F3 what `farik task show` says of a task and that the human files a task under an epic, in 8.2 how `farik run` and `farik plan` start, name their credential, hold the run lock, take commands on the daemon, and stop on Ctrl-C (ADR 0014), and in 8.3 the warning's words. It also defines in F17 each of the five harness metrics exactly, and names `farik metrics` among the reading commands of section 3. Revision 0.9 (2026-09-23) makes every dollar limit optional, with no default (ADR 0015), in sections 3, 4.1, 5.2, 5.3, 5.5, 5.12, and 10: the daily budget, a sprint's budget, and the team's cap on a task's budget are each the user's to set, and one left out is no limit of that kind. A team file that never wrote `max_task_budget_usd` no longer has the five-dollar cap it used to get by default; a team that wants it writes `max_task_budget_usd: 5`. It also says in 5.5 that a usage report for a model no price table prices is recorded at no cost and marked `unpriced`, rather than refused as 0.8 said, and in sections 3 and 5.5 that `farik doctor` and every start of `farik run` name such a model. It also says, from phase 3 step 15's landing review, in 5.13 that `farik contract new` starts driving before it files anything and takes a contract with `--lock` only once the Product Manager has written one, in 8.2 that Ctrl-C does not cut short a tick busy outside a session, in 8.3 that the no-sandbox warning names `daemon.json`, whose token lets an agent's command act as the user, and in 8.6 that the command line escapes the control characters agents write. It also corrects 5.5, from phase 3's final review, which said a session that exhausts a budget is aborted: token and dollar budgets are checked when a session reports its usage, which Claude Code does at the session's end, so a live session is bounded by its wall clock and its tool-call limit, and a budget crossed during a session stops the next one from starting. It also says in 5.6, from the same review, that `farik_git_commit` reads each path it names as a path and refuses a directory. It also says in 5.4 and 5.6 that the Definition of Done refuses a diff that changes a protected path, even one inside the allowed paths. It also says in 5.4 that the fresh sandbox of Farik's criterion runs mounts the task's worktree, gitignored build output and all. It also raises in 5.5 the default of a contract's `max_sessions` from 5 to 12, from phase 3's live testing, in which a small request's triage, refinement, plan, implementation, and review took the five sessions and left none for its acceptance: 12 is the most a task needs before its iteration limit escalates it (triage, refinement, plan, and acceptance, and an implementation and a review for each of `max_iterations` 3 passes and the one after them), so that `max_sessions` bounds only the loops the iteration limit cannot see, such as implement sessions that keep ending early. It also says in 5.4, from the same testing, in which a reviewer called `farik_exec` and was told its session had no sandbox, that a `verify` session is not offered `farik_exec` or the git writes. Revision 0.10 (2026-09-24) records what phase 4 step 01 built, which gives a team the Scrum Master, the Architect, and the Marketing Specialist. It says in 5.3 that the Scrum Master's judgment is recorded as `contract.judged` by `farik_record_judgment` in a Scrum Master session of its own, that the governor asks for it only once every other rule of the Definition of Ready passes, that a contract written after its judgment needs a new one, and that a failed judgment goes back to the Product Manager as a failed Definition of Ready. It also specifies in 5.3, on the founder's decision of that day, the configurable judgment, a team policy `judgment` with `required`, `questions`, and `judge`, which the team editor builds (phase 5) and whose defaults are what this release does, and adds to section 10 that configuring the harness is user-friendly and foolproof, which F1 and F15 say the team editor meets. It also says in 5.1 and 5.4 that Farik runs an epic's mechanical criteria for whichever reviewer it has, that the human accepts any epic's result only once those runs passed, not only one the human reviews, and how the Product Manager reviews an epic the Scrum Master broke down, asked again for any criterion its review left unanswered; and in 5.16 that an epic's reviewer, once assigned, is read from its row rather than from the team as it is now. It also gives in 5.6, 6.3, and 6.5 the Architect `git_local` and the Marketing Specialist `write_workspace` and `git_local`, because a task reaches `verifying` only with a commit on its branch. It also says in 5.16 and 6.2 that triage is the triager's session, the Scrum Master's when the team has one, on the cheaper model whatever the triager's own, and in 5.16 that an approved epic is assigned to the Scrum Master when the team has one, with the Product Manager as its reviewer, and that a rejected epic's close-out carries the rejection until the tasks that fix it are filed. It also drops the Architect's spikes from 6.3, since an Architect's task is a document and only the Developer writes application code (the founder, 2026-09-24), says in 8.2 which Farik tools a session is registered with, the judgment session's `farik_record_judgment` alone among them, and names `contract.judged` in 8.5. Revision 0.11 (2026-09-24) records what phase 4 step 02 built, on the founder's decision of that day that only the Software Developer changes code and that a task's branch says what it changes. It adds to 5.3 the rule that holds a task for any role but the Software Developer to the team's document paths, and says when an allowed path counts as inside them; to 5.12 the team rule `document_paths`, its default, and what an empty list means; and to section 3 that `farik init` writes that default into the starter team. It names in 5.11 the contract's new `change` field, `feature` or `fix`, among the content, and says in 5.14 that a task works on `feature/FRK-<n>`, `fix/FRK-<n>`, or `docs/FRK-<n>` where it worked on `farik/FRK-<n>`, with no migration of a branch made before, and that git refuses the worktree where the repository has a branch named `feature`, `fix`, or `docs`. It also says in 6.1 to 6.5 that only the Software Developer writes application code and that every other role's tasks are documents under `document_paths`. Revision 0.12 (2026-09-24) records what phase 4 step 03 built, on the founder's decision of that day that sprints are optional. It says in section 3 that the human starts a sprint, with a budget or none, that the assigner plans it from the ready backlog, and that it ends by itself once every task in it is accepted or cancelled or when the human ends it, and names `farik sprint start`, `end`, and `show` and `farik metrics --sprint` among the commands. It adds to 5.2 that while a sprint is open the governor assigns only its tasks, and those within what is left of its budget; to 5.3 that the remaining sprint budget a contract is held to is its own sprint's, and none for a contract in no sprint; to 5.5 that the sprint budget is read from the sprint, that spending it stops new assignments into the sprint and aborts no session, and that a cost stays with the sprint it was spent in; and to 5.11 that the governor writes `sprint` when a task is planned into a sprint and clears it when the task leaves one. It says in 6.2 that the Scrum Master plans the sprint, the Product Manager when the team has none; names `.farik/sprints/` in 8.4 and `sprint.started`, `sprint.planned`, and `sprint.ended` in 8.5; and computes F17's metrics per sprint. From the step's landing review, it says in section 3 that no task is left in an ended sprint, and that `farik doctor` reports a contract whose `sprint` disagrees with the log, recorded as a `drift.detected` of `sprint_mismatch`. Revision 0.13 (2026-09-24) records what phase 4 step 04 built. It changes 5.5's first row, so that a session that runs out of its tokens, its wall clock, or its tool calls leaves a note from Farik for the next session rather than blocking its task, and gives `budget.exhausted` the consequence `end_session_with_note`, which reads `end_session_and_block_task` in logs recorded before it as the same consequence; rewrites 5.5's account of what this release enforces: the note, the escalation of a task whose dollars or sessions are spent, the daily budget unchanged, and the sprint budget as 0.12 left it; and adds to 5.5 how Farik reads a model provider's usage limit from a session and the residual of that reading, that the session's agent then sleeps until the limit resets, and that a run waits for it. It says in 5.2 that Farik asks for the `any -> escalated` row for a task whose budget is spent, and in 5.7 that such an escalation resolved without more room is raised again; names in 8.2 the session end `provider_limit`, what the stream parser reads to find it, and that a driving process waits for a sleeping agent and says so, and that a command of the user's ends the wait; names `agent.slept` in 8.5; and says in F1 that a sleeping agent stays active and is not paused. Revision 0.14 (2026-09-24) records what phase 4 step 05 built: the team channel. It says in 5.9 what a message is and who writes each of its six kinds; that an agent's reaction to a move comes from the session that made it, on that session's model, which replaces "ambient and reaction messages use a cheaper model"; that Farik posts a system line, with no model, for a move by the governor or the human, for a rejection it files in a reviewer's name, for a spent daily or sprint budget, and for an agent asleep at its provider's limit; the ambient allowance, counted per sprint or, with no sprint open, per UTC day; that a mention gets its agent one short conversation session on Claude Sonnet 5 at low effort, and that replies are one deep; and how the channel summary is made. It says in 5.1 that chat reaches work only through `farik_create_task` without a parent; names `farik say` and `farik channel` in section 3; says in 8.2 which tools a conversation session has and on which model it runs; describes `message.posted` in 8.5; says in F7 what the command line gives of the channel; and adds the channel's message counts to F17. It also corrects 8.4, which listed a channel projection this release does not build. Revision 0.15 (2026-09-24) records what phase 4 step 06 built: the ceremonies and escalation hygiene. It says in 5.9 that planning, standup, review, and retro are each one session of the Scrum Master, the Product Manager's when the team has none, on Claude Sonnet 5 at `medium` effort, posting at most three messages in the ceremony's thread; when each runs and what each is given; and that a ceremony's messages start no conversation. It says in 5.7 that the planning ceremony posts a digest of every open escalation and every budget spent since the previous planning, which replaces the digest "at the start of each sprint", and that an escalation left waiting past the team policy `escalation_age_hours` is recorded as `escalation.aged` and said in the channel as a system line, while something ticks, which replaces the ping through the app's notification channel until phase 6. It says in 5.8 that the retro ceremony appends to `team/retro.md` with `farik_append_retro` and that the planning ceremony is given its end; in 6.2 that the Scrum Master runs the four ceremonies, the Product Manager when the team has none, and plans the sprint in the planning ceremony rather than a `plan` session; in section 3 that a sprint is planned in that ceremony; names `retro.appended` and `escalation.aged` in 8.5 and `session.started`'s `thread`; and says in F7 that the command line shows a ceremony's messages with their thread. Revision 0.16 (2026-09-24) records what phase 4 step 07 built: memory and decisions. It says in 5.8 that an agent writes its notebook only through `farik_write_memory`, which replaces it whole and is recorded as `memory.written`, within the team policy `memory_cap_tokens` (500 to 16,000, default 8,000), a text's tokens being a quarter of its characters rounded up; that every session's prompt says how full the notebook is and, past 80 percent of the cap, asks the agent to prune it; that a decision is immutable once written, not once accepted, written only by the Architect and the Product Manager through `farik_write_decision`, numbered, in a fixed format, never half-written or written over, recorded as `decision.written`, and read by every agent through `farik_read_decisions`; and that the project scan is refreshed after an integration when what it reads has changed, which replaces "whenever the tree changes materially". The team policies are not listed in 5.12, so `memory_cap_tokens` is described in 5.8 alone. It says in 5.9 and 8.2 that ceremony and conversation sessions are also given `farik_write_memory` and `farik_read_decisions`; in 6.1 and 6.3 that the Product Manager and the Architect write decisions with `farik_write_decision`; in 8.2 that the memory section opens with a line saying how full the notebook is and that the notebook is cut at its cap rather than at 32 KiB; and names the bodies of `memory.written` and `decision.written` in 8.5. Revision 0.17 (2026-09-24) records the fixes of phase 4's final review. It raises in 5.5 the default of a contract's `max_sessions` from 12 to 14, because a task's normal path now holds the Scrum Master's judgment and a spent session budget escalates whatever the status, so that under 12 a task that passed its last allowed review was escalated before its acceptance. It says in 5.9 that `farik_plan_sprint` is refused outside the planning ceremony. It says in 5.3, 5.4, and 5.8 that files under `.farik/` change only through Farik's tools: the Definition of Ready refuses a contract whose allowed paths reach under `.farik/`, and the Definition of Done a diff that changes a path there, whatever the role. It says in 5.12 that a path with a `..` segment anywhere, before or after a wildcard, is within no ceiling, and in 5.8 that the retro is appended by whoever runs the retro ceremony. Revision 0.18 (2026-09-24) records the founder's decisions of that day (ADR 0016): Farik is for non-technical users, ships as a web app and a desktop app with native iOS and Android apps after launch, and has a brand the founder supplies before implementation goes further. It says in the opening paragraphs that Farik is a web and desktop application with phone apps to follow, and that the pixel office is the desktop app's; adds to section 1 the goal that a non-technical user sets up and runs a team with no terminal, and phone apps at the first launch to the non-goals; and adds to section 2 the non-technical founder or creator as the primary persona. It says in section 3 that the web and desktop apps drive the team with the command line's commands, and in 4.1 that the first run is a wizard, one question per screen, in the browser or the desktop app. It adds to 5.4 what a human gate shows, a contract's approval, a `high` risk task's acceptance, and an epic's acceptance: a plain-language summary written by the agent that asks, the Product Manager in its contract and the assignee and the reviewer at the opening of their notes, the diff, and Farik's check results, required from the web UI on (phase 6), and says so in 5.7 and 5.16 item 2; it changes no other rule of section 5. It says in 8.1 that `apps/web` is served by the local daemon on 127.0.0.1 and later by the hosted tier, that `apps/desktop` wraps it, and adds `apps/ios`, `apps/android`, and `packages/brand`; in 8.6 how the web UI gets its token and that the daemon refuses a foreign `Origin`; in section 10 WCAG 2.2 AA, a web app responsive from 360 px wide, and clients built from the brand's tokens; in section 11 that Milestone 1's test runs in the browser first with at least three non-technical users, that Milestone 2 launches the web and desktop apps, and a mobile milestone before premium; in section 12 the brand and how a phone reaches a running Farik as questions 7 and 8; and a new section 14, the brand, which is not yet supplied. It also moves the forward pointers to the new phases in 5.3, 5.6, 5.7, 8.4, F7, and F12: the team editor to phase 6 step 04, MCP and the apps' notifications to phase 8, and the channel's view to the web app in phase 6. Revision 0.19 (2026-09-26) records the founder's choice of AWS for everything Farik hosts (ADR 0017): it says in 8.1 that the public website on the project's domain runs on AWS from phase 8, and the hosted tier from phase 10, while the working web app stays served by the local daemon; and it adds `apps/site` and `infra` to the tree. Revision 0.20 (2026-09-27) ships Farik on the web first (ADR 0018), in sections 1, 5.6, 5.7, 8.1, 11 and 12: the launch releases the web app alone; the desktop app becomes Milestone 3 and starts only after the web release has been tested with real users; mobile becomes Milestone 4 and premium Milestone 5. Revision 0.21 (the same day) adds a sixth role, the Finance Specialist (6.6; ADR 0019): optional, with private spreadsheet books in `.farik/local/finance/`, the team's AI spending and Stripe read-only before the web launch, and receipts from a dedicated mailbox over IMAP after it; 1, 5.2, 5.3, 5.4, 5.6, 5.8, 5.14, 8.2, 8.4, 8.6, 9, F17 and 11 each take what a role with no branch and no worktree needs. Revision 0.22 (2026-09-28) adds role kits (6.7; ADR 0020), in 5.4, 5.6, 6.7, 8.1, 8.2, 9 and 11: each role's skills and connectors, pre-tagged and pinned, with allowances for connectors that spend the user's credits; the launch becomes phase 9 and the phases after it renumber. Revision 0.23 (2026-09-28) records the founder's decisions for the web UI (ADR 0021), in sections 3, 4.1, 5.4, 5.9, 8.1, 8.2, 8.5, and 8.6: `farik serve` is the always-on driver, started in any folder, with `farik pause` and `farik resume` (`team.paused`, `team.resumed`); the first run picks or creates the project in a folder browser, checks the computer, and connects the user's AI account, whose credential is kept in the OS keychain; and the browser holds a session it traded a one-time code for, not the daemon's token, how the built web app is served and how a browser disconnects, in 8.1 and 8.6 (the page headers, `GET /session`, `POST /disconnect`, and the event stream's close on failed reads), and in 14 that the brand is recorded, its tokens have no elevation, and no two jobs share a colour. Revision 0.23 is extended (2026-09-29) by phase 6 step 05, with the founder's approval of the first-run order, computer, then AI account, then project: sections 4.1, 8.3 and 8.6 describe the setup screens as built, and the credential falls back to a private file where the computer has no keychain (ADR 0022). Revision 0.24 (2026-09-29) records the founder's decision of that day (ADR 0023), in sections 1, 5.6, 5.7, 6.6, 8.1, 8.2, 11, and 12: a user can connect an AI account from any provider and choose any agent engine, open-source ones included, with the same tools, skills, connectors and governor enforcement; this comes in a new phase 7, Engines and providers, after the web UI and before the ecosystem, so the phases from the ecosystem on renumber by one. Revision 0.25 (2026-09-29) records the runtime phase 6 step 07 built for the web UI's gates (ADR 0024), in sections 5.1, 5.2, 5.4, 5.5, 5.7, 5.11, and 8.5: the human's send-back, which waits for the review; more tries, granted on an `iterations` escalation; the summary a human gate leads with, required in a contract the human decides on and at the head of a completion or review note. Revision 0.25 is extended (2026-09-29) by step 07's landing review, in sections 5.1, 5.7 and 5.11: the criteria a human's send-back names are checked as a reviewer's are, more tries come only with a move back to `in_progress`, a task's result is listed as waiting on the human once its review has passed, and a save from the web UI never changes `locked` nor a frozen contract the team is working to. Revision 0.26 (2026-09-29) records the founder's rule of that day for who judges a plan, and what phase 6 step 06 built for team setup: the plan check is the team's own policy, built (5.3, 8.5); its judge is the Architect, else the Scrum Master, else the Product Manager (5.1, 5.3); the wizard's steps 4 to 8 and its Start the team are described (4.1); a change to what an agent may do waits for its next session by the session's own record (4.4); and every change shows its effects before it is saved (10). Revision 0.26 is extended (2026-09-29) by step 06's landing review, in F1: a retired agent is not one of the seven; in 5.6: the team's permission answers; in 4.1: where the user's words are kept, the marker's name, and only setup's start resumes the team; in 8.6: the AI account's row and Disconnect; in 10: refusals by code, the defaults a setting is put back to, and the daemon, not the page, works out each agent's model, tiers and who checks plans; in 4.4: a replacement is one change, and the last agent of a needed role is not paused or retired; and in 8.2: an agent's effort may be set without its model. Revision 0.27 (2026-09-30) says in 4.4 and 10 that the answers setup asks for are changed from Settings after setup, each change shown before it is saved, from phase 6 step 09's Task 4b. Revision 0.28 (2026-09-30) says in F3 what the web app's board, task page and sprint page show, and in F17 what its Costs page shows, from phase 6 step 09; its landing fixes keep the last sprint's figures on the Costs page once it ends, and leave a session's tool calls out of a task's history. Revision 0.29 (2026-09-30) says in F7 what the web app's channel shows, from phase 6 step 10. Revision 0.30 (2026-09-30) records the founder's ruling of that day on a key the model provider refuses, found by testing the web UI with a key that did not work: in 5.5 such a session is not its task's, and the team pauses at once; in 5.7 Today's one row for it; in 8.5 `credential_refused` and a `team.paused` by `farik`; and in 8.6 the account row says so and connecting the account again resumes the team. It also records, from the same test, that the web app words a move's channel line in plain words (8.6). Revision 0.31 (2026-09-30) records the UI/UX Designer, from phase 6 step 11 (ADR 0026): in 1, 3, 4.1 and F1 the suggested team of six, which it joins; in 3, 5.3, 5.12, 5.14 and 6.1 to 6.5 that only the Developer and the UI/UX Designer change code; in 5.1 and 5.4 its reviewer; in 5.2 and 5.6 its plan gate and its tiers; a new 6.8 for the role, its explore, plan, approval and implement flow, and what the web app shows of it; in 8.2 the `explore` session and the Designer's model; in 8.5 the three `design_plan.*` events; in F17 the `explore` purpose; and in section 10 the first-day figure, now an estimate for the suggested six. From the step's landing review, it also says in 4.1 that a take-on that fails goes back to the wizard on the same port, and in 6.8 what a Designer's activity line says while it explores and while its plan waits, and that each Team card shows its agent's line; in 6.8 the plan card's words, as the approved mockup has them; in 4.1 that each setup row says its role's job; in 6.8 that an explore session's calls are held to the read tier; and in 10 that the first-day figure is "about" twenty-five dollars, an estimate, not a bound. It also says in 8.2 that the shipped defaults are the newest priced model of each family, Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Sonnet 5.5, at the efforts they had. Revision 0.32 (2026-09-30) adds an eighth, optional role, the DevOps Engineer, in 6.9 (ADR 0027): it deploys integrated work as sprint tasks, watches production with a tick that uses no model, and restores a failed deploy by one restart, then one rollback to the last healthy deployment, then a reviewed fix; it reaches production only through three Farik tools whose arguments Farik chooses. Nothing of it is built before phase 9 step 06. Farik is a production-grade harness system for multi-agent systems, whose agents are its own team rather than other agent systems run under it. It is a web application, with a desktop app to follow once the web release has been tested and native iOS and Android apps after that (changed in 0.20), that lets a person, technical or not, assemble a small team of AI agents, each with a named role, a face, its own tools, and its own skills, and put that team to work on a software product. The team runs a lightweight Scrum process: a product manager writes task contracts with explicit exit criteria, a scrum master keeps the board moving, and specialists do the work. A deterministic governor enforces the rules the agents cannot be trusted to enforce on themselves. The web app shows the team's work on a board, where the user can open any task and any agent and talk to them one-on-one or in the team channel; the desktop app wraps the same interface and adds a pixel-art office where the user can watch the team and open any agent's desk (changed in 0.18).

The governance layer is the product. The pixel office is how people fall in love with it. The roles are the first content shipped on top of both.

## 1. Goals and non-goals

Goals for the initial launch:

1. A user can create a team of two to seven agents (at least one Product Manager and one Software Developer; the team builder suggests six, changed in 0.31), name them, pick avatars, and assign each one of seven launch roles: Product Manager, Scrum Master, Architect, Software Developer, UI/UX Designer (added in 0.31), Marketing Specialist, and Finance Specialist, which the team builder offers but does not suggest (added in 0.21; on the Team page from 0.54, and in setup once the list of more roles of phase 7 step 10b lands); the DevOps Engineer and the Procurement Specialist are offered the same way (6.9, 6.10; added in 0.32 and 0.49). From phase 12, after the launch, a team needs at least one Product Manager and no Software Developer unless it builds software (ADR 0040; changed in 0.50).
2. A user can point the team at an existing git repository or start a new project from a one-paragraph brief. From phase 12, a project is a business workspace, a folder of its own on the user's computer, which a software business's repository already is (ADR 0040; changed in 0.50).
3. Work flows through a task lifecycle that the user can inspect at every step, where every task has a written contract with exit criteria before anyone starts on it, and where nobody accepts their own work.
4. Every agent action is logged to an append-only event stream, and every expense (tokens, wall clock, tool calls) is attributable to a task and an agent.
5. The user can talk to any agent directly and can read and join the team's group channel, where the agents talk to each other in plain, conversational language.
6. Each agent can be given its own MCP servers and its own skills, independent of the others.
7. The core is open source and runnable with the user's own API key. A hosted premium tier exists in the design but is not required for the first release.
8. A non-technical user can set up and run a team with no terminal, in the web app (changed in 0.20): the first run is a wizard that asks one question per screen (4.1), and every human gate explains itself in plain language, with the diff there for whoever reads code (5.4) (added in 0.18).

Non-goals for the initial launch:

- The desktop app and the phone apps. The first launch ships the web app alone. The desktop app follows once the web release has been tested with real users, and native iOS and Android apps follow the desktop app (section 11; added in 0.18, changed in 0.20).
- Non-software businesses in the first launch (changed in 0.50). Farik is for every business, software or not (ADR 0040, the founder's decision of 2026-10-05), but the first launch is built and measured around shipping software; business workspaces, where a team with no repository and no Software Developer runs any business, are phase 12, after the launch, and cloud-hosted workspaces for paying customers are phase 15. The Procurement Specialist (6.10) ships at the launch and already serves any business.
- Teams larger than seven. The coordination cost grows faster than the output. Seven is a hard cap in the first release.
- Multi-user collaboration on one team. One human, one team, for now. Team templates are reuse, not several live teams: a saved team is copied into a project's one team, and the project's team is what runs (3, 4.4; added in 0.35).
- Fully autonomous operation with no human at all. The harness assumes a human is reachable for escalations, even if hours pass before they answer.
- A model-agnostic runtime before the web UI's Milestone 1 test, which runs on Claude alone (changed in 0.24, from "on day one"). See section 8.2 for the provider decision. Other providers and agent engines come in phase 8, after the role kits, which are built on Claude first, and before the rest of the ecosystem and the launch (renumbered by ADR 0029), with the same tools, skills, connectors and governor enforcement on every engine (8.2; added in 0.24, ADR 0023).

## 2. Who it is for

Three people matter for the first release, and the first of them most (changed in 0.18).

The non-technical founder or creator (added in 0.18). Someone with a product in mind who can say what they want but cannot read code. Farik is built for them first. They must be able to set up a team, approve its contracts, and accept its work through plain-language summaries alone, with the diff there for whoever can read it (5.4), and never be asked for a glob, a regular expression, or a file format unless they open a setting's advanced view (section 10).

The solo builder. Someone with an idea and a repository, technical enough to read a diff, who wants a product to keep moving while they are at their day job. They are price sensitive and will run on their own key. They care that the agents do not wreck the repository while unsupervised.

The small-team lead. A founder or engineering lead with a two-to-five person company who wants an always-on "extra squad" that takes the backlog grooming, the boring implementation tickets, and the release notes. They will pay for hosted execution if it saves them an afternoon a week and if the audit trail is good enough to trust.

The small-business owner (added in 0.50; ADR 0040). Someone who runs a business that is not software, flipping cars, reselling goods online, renting cars, and wants a team that researches, buys through purchase orders they approve, keeps the books and markets the business. Served from phase 12; until then, the Procurement Specialist works for them inside a software team's workspace.

A third persona, the enterprise platform team, is deliberately deferred. Their asks (SSO, SOC 2, private model endpoints) are real but would bend the first release out of shape.

## 3. Core concepts

**Team.** A named group of two to seven agents attached to exactly one project at a time. A team has a shared channel, a one-to-one chat between the user and each agent (4.3; added in 0.34), a board, optional spending limits, and a memory (ADR 0015).

**Team template (added in 0.35).** A team the user saved to start other projects from, kept on this computer outside every project (8.4) and held to `docs/schemas/team-template.schema.json`. It has a name of 1 to 60 characters with no space at either end, when it was saved, two to seven agents, and the four answers setup asks. Each agent keeps its id, name, role, persona, picture and model with its effort; the answers are what agents may do (`run_commands` and `push`), the plan check, how finished work is added, and the daily spending limit, none when there is none. It also holds whether the team plans its work in sprints, `policy.plan_in_sprints`, the team's value written out when it is saved, `false` among them, and a template saved before has none (added in 0.36). It leaves out everything that belongs to one project: the team's rules, the criterion library, how to open the app, the integration branch, the team's name, and each agent's status, grants, revokes, preauthorized tools and connectors; a retired agent is not saved, a paused one is, and an uploaded picture is left out, the agent taking its role's shipped picture where the template is used, since nothing copies a picture between projects yet. A template is held to the team's own rules (F1): a team made of its agents, all active, must be one `validate_team` accepts, and a hand-edited file that is not gets the team's sentences at the template's own paths. Its file is named by its slug: the name's ASCII letters and digits lower-cased, every run of anything else one `-`, trimmed, at most 64 characters; a name that leaves nothing is refused. A template is never a second live team: using one changes the project's one team (4.4).

**Agent.** A persistent identity: name, avatar, role, persona, model configuration, tool permissions, MCP servers, skills, and a private memory. An agent is not a running process. When work is assigned, the runtime starts a session for that agent, seeded with its identity and the task. Sessions end; the agent persists.

**Role.** A template that an agent is instantiated from. A role defines a mandate (what this agent is for), the artifacts it produces, the gates it owns, its default tools, and, importantly, what it is forbidden from doing. Roles are shipped as files and are editable. Two roles change code, the Software Developer and the UI/UX Designer; every other role's work is documents (6; added in 0.31).

**Project.** A git repository plus a `.farik/` directory inside it that holds the backlog, contracts, decisions, and memories as plain files. Everything the team knows about the project lives in the repository, so it can be diffed, reviewed, and version controlled like any other code.

`farik init` makes a repository a project: it writes `.farik/`, the event log under `.farik/local/`, `project.md` holding the scan's read-back, and a criterion library seeded from what the scan found. When there is no team file it writes a starter team of two agents — a Product Manager and a Software Developer, the two a team cannot work without (F1) — named after their roles for the user to rename in the team editor, on the models 8.2 ships, with no dollar limit (5.5), with one unfinished task per agent (`wip_limit_per_agent: 1`), planning its work in sprints (`plan_in_sprints: true`, so `farik run` on a new project ends idle until `farik sprint start`; added in 0.36), and the rest of the policy as sections 5.2, 5.5 and 5.14 give it, and with the team rules empty but for the three default document paths of 5.12, which it writes out so the user sees them (changed in 0.11). A second `farik init` is a rescan: it keeps the team and the criteria a person wrote and replaces the ones the last scan found, and it refuses rather than writing past a team file or a criterion library that is there and cannot be read. The team and the project are identified in every event by the slug of the team's name and of the repository's own directory name, both fixed by the first `farik init` and read from the log thereafter (added in 0.7); each slug is at most 64 characters, as a team's agent id is, by the rule a template's slug follows (changed in 0.35), and a project already identified keeps its slug from the log. Everything the project knows is then readable from the command line: `farik board` shows the lifecycle, `farik task show` one contract with its events, `farik log` the event log with filters and a JSON-lines export (F11), `farik rules show` and `farik criteria list` the two team files a person hand-edits (F15, F16), `farik metrics` the harness metrics of F17 (added in 0.8), and `farik metrics --sprint <id>` one sprint's, `farik sprint show [<id>]` the sprint named, else the open one, else the latest (added in 0.12), and `farik doctor` every way the files and the log disagree — each recorded as a `drift.detected` event — plus a team rule that does not compile, a setting Farik does not know, a criterion whose verification matches no branch of its `oneOf`, and an active agent's model that no price table prices (5.5). `farik doctor` exits 1 when it found something (added in 0.7).

The team is driven from the command line (added in 0.8). `farik run` starts the daemon and the orchestrator and works the board until nothing on it needs doing, the user stops it, or Ctrl-C; `farik plan` does the same with the planning rules alone, triaging, contracting, breaking down, and assigning without starting any work (8.2). The user's own commands are `farik approve <task>` (a contract awaiting approval), `farik accept <task> [--message <text>]` (a result that waits for the user), `farik answer <question> <answer>`, `farik integrate <task>`, `farik resolve <task> <status> <message>`, `farik cancel <task> <reason>`, `farik stop` (the run, after its session) and `farik stop <session or task>` (one session, now), with `farik triage` and `farik contract lock` and `unlock`, and `farik sprint start [--budget <usd>]` and `farik sprint end` (added in 0.12). Each works from any terminal: while a process drives the project the command is sent to it, and otherwise it is handled where it was typed (ADR 0014). `farik stop` needs a driving process. `farik pause` stops the team starting sessions, letting the running ones finish, until `farik resume`; the pause is kept in the log, so it holds across a restart (added in 0.23). While the team is paused no rule runs but the chat rule (5.9), which still answers the user's one-to-one chats unless the team is paused because the provider refused the key (5.5; changed in 0.34): no other session starts, no criterion is run, nothing integrates by itself, a finished sprint does not end, and no aged escalation is posted. The human's own commands are all still handled, `farik integrate` among them, since the human is not the team, and `farik run` on a paused team ticks once, says the team is paused, and exits as it does on an idle board (added in 0.23). `farik serve` drives the team as `farik run` does but never exits when the board is idle, waiting instead for something to do; it can be started in any folder, and opens the project there, else the last project it served, else the first-run wizard (added in 0.23). The web and desktop apps (8.1) drive the team through the same daemon and give the user the same commands, so a user who never opens a terminal has everything the command line has: in the web app, once `farik serve` is started, and in the desktop app with nothing started at all. There the first run, `farik init` among it, is the apps' wizard (4.1; added in 0.18).

**Task.** The unit of work. A task is not ready until it has a contract. A task belongs to an epic, or stands alone when it came from a small request.

**Epic.** The translation of one large request from the user into a contract, written by the Product Manager after asking the user its questions and approved by the user before anyone breaks it into tasks. A small request becomes a single task instead; the Scrum Master or the Product Manager decides which (5.16; added in 0.3).

**Contract.** A structured document attached to an epic or a task: intent, scope, requirements, exit criteria with a verification method for each, constraints, budget, and a named reviewer who is not the assignee. The schema is in `docs/schemas/task-contract.schema.json`; the `kind` field says which of the two it is. Every exit criterion's `id` names one criterion, and every requirement's `id` names one requirement: a recorded result, a note, and an event all refer to a criterion by its id, and a criterion names the requirements it satisfies by theirs, so a contract that gives one id to two of either is refused when it is read, which JSON Schema cannot express and the validator therefore does. No array in a contract holds more than a hundred entries, so that a refusal naming what is wrong stays readable and an untrusted contract cannot make the governor walk a list without end (added in 0.3).

**Sprint.** A batch of tasks, with a budget when the user sets one. A sprint ends when every task in it is accepted or cancelled; its budget, when it has one, caps what may be assigned inside it (decided 2026-09-15; there is no time box). Sprints exist so that the team stops and looks up periodically rather than grinding an unbounded backlog. Sprints are optional (changed in 0.12): with none open the board flows as it does without them, unless the team plans its work in sprints (below; changed in 0.36). The human starts one with `farik sprint start`, which opens `S<n>`, one at a time, with the budget given or none; the assigner (the Scrum Master, or the Product Manager when the team has no active one) then plans it once, in its planning ceremony, a session about no task (5.9; changed in 0.15), from the ready backlog (6.2): tasks and approved epics that are `ready`, have no parent, and are in no sprint, or, when the team plans its work in sprints, the Backlog's rows with no parent (below; changed in 0.36), an epic bringing every task already under it, and a task later filed under an epic in the open sprint joining it. A planning session that plans nothing is not asked again, and `farik sprint show` then says the empty sprint waits for the human to end it. The sprint ends by itself, recorded as ended by the governor, once it holds at least one task and every task in it is accepted or cancelled; `farik sprint end` ends it early, and its unfinished tasks leave it with their status unchanged, and wait in the Backlog when the team plans its work in sprints (below; changed in 0.36). Every unfinished task the log holds in a sprint leaves it when it ends, whether or not the sprint file lists it, and a plan recorded after a sprint ended puts nothing in it, so that no task is left in an ended sprint; a task filed under an epic whose sprint is ending is filed in no sprint, with a warning. `farik doctor` reports a contract whose `sprint` is not the one the log holds the task in. `farik run` and `farik plan` print a sprint's planning ceremony and its end as `S<n>: <what>`, and `farik run` its standup, review, and retro the same way (changed in 0.15).

**Planning work in sprints (added in 0.36; ADR 0028).** A team may plan its work in sprints, by the team policy `plan_in_sprints` (5.12). It is on for every team setup makes (4.1) and for `farik init`'s starter team, which write it out as `true`, and it is what `settings.defaults` answers, so "Put back the default" turns it on (4.4); a team file without the key has it off, so a project made before works as it did. With it on, the team gets work ready at any time, and nothing new is built outside the open sprint:

- **What is held.** A task, never an epic, that is outside the open sprint (in no sprint, in another one, or any while no sprint is open) and is `ready`, or carries the Backlog mark (below). A `ready` one is not assigned: the orchestrator does not ask, and the assignment gate refuses an agent's or the human's ask (5.2). A marked one is not started, worked on, or reworked after a rejection until a sprint plans it. Holding a task records nothing, as a full agent's does not, and a session already running is never stopped. `farik-core`'s `waits_for_a_sprint` decides it, and the gate and the orchestrator both read it.
- **What runs at any time**, sprint or not: triage, refining and the Product Manager's questions, the plan check, the human's approvals and send-backs, an approved epic's assignment to its breaker-down and its breakdown, which are preparation, an epic's close-out, review and verification (the Designer's design review among them), integration, conversations, one-to-one chats, the budget and channel rules, and the ceremonies.
- **Started work finishes** (the founder, 2026-10-01). A task outside the open sprint in `assigned`, `in_progress`, `blocked`, `verifying` or `rejected`, without the Backlog mark, is under way and carries on to acceptance, rework included. It is read from the status at every tick, with no mark taken when the policy is switched on; since every task is built only after `ready -> assigned`, which is held, the work built outside a sprint can only shrink.
- **The Backlog** is, under the policy, every row outside the open sprint in `ready`, `assigned`, `in_progress` or `rejected` that is an epic, or a task held as above: a ready task, an epic being broken down or broken down with its ready tasks, and work a sprint ended early left. Work under way at the switch keeps its lane, and so does a `blocked`, `escalated` or `verifying` row. `farik-core`'s `in_the_backlog` decides it, and the daemon answers it per row (F3). Waiting in the Backlog changes no status (5.7).
- **Starting a sprint plans the Backlog**, through the planning ceremony that exists (5.9). Work that becomes ready after the planning waits in the Backlog for the next sprint and is not added to the open one; a task filed under an epic the open sprint holds still joins it, as above.
- **A sprint ended under the policy sends its unfinished tasks to the Backlog** (the founder, 2026-10-01); only a sprint ended early has any. Its `sprint.ended` carries `backlog: true` (8.5), and each task in its `left` takes the Backlog mark, `left_for_the_backlog` on the task's projection, until the `sprint.planned` that puts it in a sprint clears it. A sprint ended with the policy off marks nothing, so its tasks finish if the policy is switched on later. A marked task under an epic that left the Backlog, an escalated epic say, is not planned alone, since `farik_plan_sprint` refuses a task with a parent, and waits until its epic is back in the Backlog.
- **Switching the policy off clears every Backlog mark.** Every write of the team file records `team.updated` with the team's `plan_in_sprints` (8.5), and one with `false` clears the mark on every task, so a task built while the policy was off is never held mid-work when it is switched back on.
- **An idle tick** with work in the Backlog and no sprint open says "the ready work waits for a sprint", unless the day's budget is spent or an agent sleeps, which it says first, and `farik run` then ends with the Backlog's line (5.7).
- **The one exception** ADR 0028 keeps is the DevOps Engineer's incident fix (6.9), which skips planning and runs even outside a sprint. Nothing marks a contract as an incident's fix yet, so it is not built here: phase 7 step 11 adds it to `waits_for_a_sprint`, and, with the policy off, to the open sprint's membership rule (5.2).

**Governor.** Deterministic code, not an agent, that sits between every agent and every tool. It checks permissions, budgets, iteration limits, and path allowlists, and it is the only component that can move a task between certain states. Agents propose; the governor disposes.

**Channel.** The team's group chat. Agents post there in a human register when events happen and when they want to coordinate. A message in the channel is never an instruction to do work. Work comes only from tasks. On the command line (added in 0.14), `farik say <text>` posts the human's message, sent to a process driving the project as the user's other commands are, and says "posted in the channel"; `farik channel [--last <n>]` prints the channel from the log, oldest first, the last 50 messages unless `--last` says otherwise, one line each as `<time> <author> [<thread>] <text>` with the thread only when there is one, every message escaped as agent-written text is (8.6), and `--json` gives one object per message: its fields and its `seq`, `recorded_at`, and `task_id`.

**Chat (added in 0.34).** The user's private conversation with one agent (4.3), one per agent, kept in the log apart from the channel and never read by another agent (5.9). On the command line, `farik chat <agent> <text>` sends the user's message, as the user's other commands are sent, and says "sent to <Name>"; an agent not on the team is not found, and a retired agent's chat is refused, being read-only. `farik chat <agent>` prints the chat from the log, oldest first, one block per message, blocks apart by a blank line: `<time> <author>`, the text with its line breaks, and a proposed request's title and text after "Proposed request:", every message escaped as agent-written text is (8.6); `--json` gives one object per message, its fields and its `seq` and `recorded_at`.

**Skill.** A folder containing a `SKILL.md` and supporting files, following the Agent Skills format, that teaches an agent a procedure. Skills can be assigned to one agent, to a role, or to the whole team.

**MCP connection.** A configured Model Context Protocol server (stdio or remote) that gives an agent tools. Connections are configured per agent, with credentials stored in the operating system keychain locally or in a vault when hosted.

## 4. User journeys

### 4.1 First run

The user opens Farik, in the browser or in the desktop app, and a wizard starts. It asks one question per screen, each with a safe default and a one-line explanation in plain language (section 10), and nothing in it needs a terminal (changed in 0.18). In the desktop app it opens on an empty pixel office with a door. The wizard's order is the computer, the AI account, the project, and then the rest (changed in 0.23; approved by the founder 2026-09-29): the first two need no project, and taking a project on starts the team's driver, which needs the key. In the browser, Farik is started with `farik serve`, which opens the page itself (added in 0.23). Run outside a project, `farik serve` serves setup and reopens the last project it served, when that is still a project (added in 0.23). A project found with no credential, under the Claude engine, opens on the account screen with the project already chosen (added in 0.23); connecting the account there takes that project on at once, and the page follows Farik to it (added in 0.23).

First, Farik checks the computer: the Claude Code program at its oldest supported version, git, and Docker, and whether the sandbox image is built, saying in plain words how to fix what is missing; it builds the sandbox image when Docker is ready and the image is not (added in 0.23), and offers no-sandbox mode with its warning (8.3) when Docker is missing. When the team has a UI/UX Designer, which setup's suggested six always do, the check has a row "Browser for the UI/UX Designer", ready when the pinned Playwright image (8.3) is on the computer and missing otherwise, with a button that fetches it (added in 0.33). Then the user connects their AI account: they paste an API key, or a subscription token the screen shows them how to make, and Farik keeps it in the OS keychain or, where the computer has none, a file only its owner can read (8.6; added in 0.23, changed in 0.23, ADR 0022).

Then the first question about the work: existing project or new one? For an existing project they pick a folder, in the browser from a folder browser limited to their home directory (added in 0.23); Farik checks it is the root of a git repository and not the home folder itself, where Farik's own settings folder would be part of the project (added in 0.38 after review), that no other Farik is running it, and that the account is connected, scans the tree, and produces a short read-back ("TypeScript monorepo, pnpm, 3 packages, tests in vitest, last commit 4 days ago") (a project on cargo or go names no other test runner, since that toolchain runs its tests, whatever runner a web app inside it configures; changed in 0.37). For a new project they write a paragraph and pick where its folder goes; Farik makes it, runs `git init` and makes a first commit, and files the paragraph as the project's first request (added in 0.23). Farik takes the project on in the same process, on the same port, without a restart, and the page carries on (added in 0.23). A take-on that fails goes back to the wizard, which says why, on that same port: `farik serve` holds its port from start to end, so nothing else can take it between the wizard and the team, and a page that connects in between waits rather than being refused (added in 0.31; changed in 0.37). Until setup ends the team is kept paused, marked by `.farik/local/setup-pending` (added in 0.23).

They then build the team. "Your team" offers three starts above the rows, on one screen (added in 0.35): the suggested team, chosen at first; a saved team (3), picked by name with its faces from the templates kept on this computer; and from scratch. A saved team fills the rows with its agents, each active (the screen's intro stays above the starts, and "Filled from <name>" sits between the starts and the rows; 0.35 fix wave), with its role's suggested persona, picture, model and connectors wherever the template has none; its answers fill the later screens, already chosen; and its permission answers carry over (the founder, 2026-09-30): the two permission questions are not asked again, the last screen lists them in words under "What they may do, from <name>", and Settings changes them later. Setup does not use the switching rules of 4.4, since the starter team it replaces is the setup's own. From scratch is two rows, a Product Manager and a Developer, their roles fixed and their names empty, with "Add someone"; Continue waits until both are named. "A saved team" is offered but cannot be chosen when none is saved, or when Farik has no state folder (8.4), and says which. The default suggestion is six agents, one per launch role but the Finance Specialist, which is offered and not suggested: the Product Manager, the Scrum Master, the Architect, the Developer, the UI/UX Designer, and the Marketing Specialist, in that order; the user may untick any of them, the UI/UX Designer among them (changed in 0.31). Each agent gets a generated name and avatar that the user can change, and a short persona line ("terse, prefers to show rather than tell"). In the wizard each row says the role's job in a sentence, the UI/UX Designer's naming the Product Manager who agrees its plans; the persona shows on the Team page (added in 0.31). Adding a seventh agent means picking a role again; two developers is the common case. An added agent's picture is the first of the extra pictures that nobody on the team has, never the UI/UX Designer's own or the Finance Specialist's (added in 0.31). The UI/UX Designer's row says its job ends by checking every screen the Developer builds (added in 0.33). The UI/UX Designer works only with Docker's sandbox, because its browser runs there (the founder, 2026-09-30; added in 0.33): in no-sandbox mode, or when Docker cannot be reached, the suggestion names it unavailable (`team.propose`'s `unavailable`, with `designer_needs_sandbox`), and its row starts unticked and cannot be ticked, a saved team's UI/UX Designer's too whatever its id, since the row is held off by role (0.35 fix wave), says "Needs Docker's sandbox" and why, and offers "Check again" and a link to how to install Docker. While the Designer is ticked, a card under its row, "<name> needs to know how to open your app", asks for the project's preview (8.3): "Get it ready", the `prepare` command, optional; "Start it", the `start` command; the port; and the first page, `/` by default. Left empty, no preview is saved; a value the daemon refuses is shown at its field. After setup the preview is changed from Settings, under "How to open your app", which is there while the team has a Designer that is not retired, the change shown before it is saved (added in 0.33). The wizard's steps after the computer, the account and the project are these (added in 0.26). Step 4 is the read-back of the scan, which the user confirms, or corrects in a few words that Farik keeps in `.farik/project.md`, dated, under "The user says", across every rescan, `farik init`'s and an integration's alike; it lists what the project is, how it is tested and checked, its last change, and the files kept private, the folder Farik keeps its own files in left out. Step 5 is the team; step 6 the two permission questions; step 7 the daily budget; step 8 how finished work is integrated, and Start the team. A team started from a saved one skips step 6, so its wizard has seven steps (added in 0.35). Behind an Advanced switch on the last screen are the team's rules, the checks, the plan check (5.3), and the switch "Plan work in sprints" (added in 0.36). Setup asks no question about sprints (added in 0.36): a team it starts plans its work in sprints (section 3), `team.propose` answering `policy.plan_in_sprints: true` whatever the file says, since setup only makes a new team, and the last screen says so in a box, "Your team works in sprints": "The team gets your requests ready straight away, and starts building when you start a sprint from the Board. Until then, ready work waits in the Backlog." and "Change this under advanced settings, or later in Settings."; the box is there while the switch is on, and `team.start` saves the answer. A team started from a saved one takes the template's answer, and plans in sprints when the template has none. Before it saves, the wizard asks the daemon to validate the team, and a refusal is shown at the agent it concerns. The team starts paused, and Start the team saves the team and the checks, removes the marker and resumes the team, in one call; the page opens on the setup screens for as long as the marker is there. The marker is `.farik/local/setup-pending`, which the setup host writes when it made the project a Farik project; only a start while it is there resumes the team, so a team paused by a budget's stop or by the user stays paused (changed in 0.26).

The user is shown the default permissions for each role and asked to confirm two things explicitly: whether agents may run commands, and whether they may push to git. The user is also offered a daily budget in dollars, which is optional: left empty, the team has no daily dollar limit (ADR 0015). Nothing runs until the two permissions are set.

The team appears on the board, and in the desktop app the office populates and agents walk to their desks (changed in 0.18). The Product Manager posts in the channel a first reading of the project and asks the user two or three questions. The user's first request, typed in reply, is filed, triaged, and contracted (5.16).

### 4.2 The working loop

The user opens the app in the morning. The board shows what moved overnight. The channel shows the standup summary the Scrum Master posted. Two tasks are waiting on the user: one is a contract for a risky change that the governor routed to human acceptance, the other is an escalation from the developer who hit the iteration limit on a flaky test. The user reads both, accepts one, and answers the developer directly from their desk.

### 4.3 Asking an agent a question

Clicking on an agent opens a one-on-one. This conversation is outside the task system: the agent answers using its memory and read access to the project but cannot change anything. As built (changed in 0.34; ADR 0026), the user opens "Chats" on the rail and picks the agent (F8). The agent answers by itself: each message of the user's gets one `chat` session of that agent (8.2), which the governor, not the prompt, holds to the read tier, and whose one write is its reply. If the conversation produces something actionable, the reply carries a proposed request, a title and a text, and nothing is filed until the user presses "Send as a request", which files the words, edited or not, as the user's own request, triaged and contracted like anything else (5.16); the agent files nothing itself. A chat is answered while the team is paused and while its agent is paused (4.4); it waits while the day's budget is spent (5.5), while its agent sleeps at its provider's limit, and while the provider refuses the key, and the chat says why. Nothing said in a chat reaches the channel or another agent (5.9).

### 4.4 Changing the team

The user can pause, retire, or replace an agent at any time. Retiring an agent keeps its memory in the project so a successor can read it. Replacing an agent retires it and adds its successor in one change to the team file, checked as a whole, so a team is never left for a moment without a role it needs; pausing or retiring the last active agent of a role the team needs, or of the role it named to check plans, is refused in words that say what to do first (added in 0.26). Changing a role's permissions takes effect on the next session that agent starts, never mid-session (changed in 0.26: a session is held to the tiers it started with, whatever the team's file says by then; whether an agent is paused or retired is still read on every call, so pausing stops it at once). A paused agent still answers its one-to-one chat: a `chat` session is the one whose calls a paused agent may make (8.2), and a retired agent's chat is read-only. Pausing an agent aborts every session it is running except a chat session under way, which runs to its end and answers its message, since a paused agent still answers its chats; retiring an agent aborts that too (F8; added in 0.34; changed in 0.37). Retiring an agent blocks each task it holds `in_progress` or `assigned`, with the blocker "agent retired by the user", so the user sees it and no task waits on an agent that will not start it; pausing blocks only its `in_progress` tasks, since a resumed agent starts what it was assigned (changed in 0.37). After setup, the answers setup asked for (what agents may do, the daily spending limit, how finished work is added, and how plans are checked) are changed from Settings, each part on its own, with its effect shown before Save and "Put back the default" beside it; a change starts with each agent's next session (added in 0.27). Settings' team rules have "Planning work" after "How finished work is added", with the switch "Plan work in sprints" and the line "The team gets work ready at any time: it asks its questions, writes the plans and has them checked, and breaks big requests into tasks. Nobody starts building until you start a sprint. Work that becomes ready during a sprint waits in the Backlog for the next one." (added in 0.36). It saves through `team.save`, which wakes the team (8.2), so the switch takes effect at once; "Put back the default" turns it on, since the default is on (section 3). Its effects, from `team.validate`: turned on, "Ready work now waits in the Backlog until you start a sprint.", then, when tasks are under way outside the open sprint, "The task already under way finishes first." or "The <n> tasks already under way finish first."; turned off, "Ready work starts as soon as someone is free, without waiting for a sprint.", then, when the Backlog holds work, "<title>, waiting in the Backlog, can start now." or "The <n> pieces of work in the Backlog, <titles>, can start now.", its rows with no parent, and always "You can still start sprints from the Board."

The Team page saves the team as a template and uses a saved one (added in 0.35). "Save as a template" asks for a name and saves what section 3 says a template holds; a name a saved team already has, by its slug, is refused with `template_exists` and the words "A saved team is called <name> already. Replace it, or choose another name.", and Replace saves over it. Saving records no event. "Use a saved team" lists the saved teams, then shows what using one would do before anything is saved: who stays, who joins, who is retired and who is removed, each with face, name and role, and the change in words under "What changes": first, for each agent retired, the unfinished (`in_progress` or `assigned`, changed in 0.37) task its retirement puts on hold, by id and title ("Sol is retired. Sol's unfinished task, T-18 “Plan the next sprint”, is put on hold until you give it to someone."), then each agent who stays and whose model or effort changes, in the words the Team cards use ("Kai's model changes from the strongest model to the everyday model.", "Kai now works carefully."; never a model id, unless no family Farik names the model), words `farik-core` keeps so that this preview, Settings and `team.validate` say the same, then the team's answers (added in the 0.35 fix wave); "Use this team" applies it and the Team page reads the team again at once, not on the next event, so "Add someone" and the cards act on the team as written, giving the digest of the template file its preview read, so a template whose file changed since (Replace from another tab, say, or an edit by hand) is refused with `template_changed` and the words "This saved team was saved again since you looked at it. Go back and look at what changes once more.", and its answers are applied only as a preview showed them; going back and looking again shows the file as it is now and gives its digest, so using it then applies (added in the 0.35 fix wave). The rules, which `apply_template` in `farik-core` works out with no I/O:

- **Stays**: an agent of the template whose id and role match a project agent that is not retired. It takes the template's name, and its persona, picture and model where the template has them, and keeps its status, grants, revokes and connectors, its work and its chats; a paused match stays paused.
- **Retired**: every other agent that is not retired and has worked, meaning the log holds an event that names it, a one-to-one chat with it among them (8.5), or a task names it as its assignee or reviewer, so no task is left with nobody (changed in 0.37). The same rule decides whether saving the team may drop an agent or must retire it. Its retirement has the effects a retirement from its card has (F1).
- **Removed**: every other agent that never worked, taken off the team file. A removed agent's id is free, so a joining agent may take it.
- **Joins**: every template agent that does not stay, active, with no grants or revokes, and its role's suggested persona, picture, model and connectors where the template has none (the UI/UX Designer its browser connector, 5.6). Its id takes `-2`, `-3`… when an agent left on the team holds it, cut short to stay within 64 characters.
- The team takes the template's permissions, plan check, how finished work is added, and daily limit, none clearing the project's, and whether it plans in sprints when the template says, none keeping the project's (added in 0.36); everything else, the rules among it, is the project's. Agents already retired are left as they are.

The result is held to the team's rules (F1). It can fail where a match that stays is paused: a template whose Developer is a paused agent of the project leaves the team with no active Developer once the active one is retired. The preview then shows why in words, naming the paused agent and saying to resume it first or choose another saved team, and "Use this team" cannot be pressed. Using a template works the result out again rather than taking the preview's, under the one lock that every write of the team file in a daemon holds from its read to its write (`team.save`, `team.start`, a status change from a card, `agent.replace`, connecting and disconnecting a connector (changed in 0.38), and using a template), so what is written is checked against the team it replaces, `agent.replace`'s newcomer among it; a result refused then writes nothing and is answered as a refusal (-32005) with the team's errors. It writes the team file once, then records each retirement's effects in order, then one `team.updated` naming the template (8.5). Settings has "Saved teams": each saved team with its faces, renamed in place or deleted after "Delete it" ("Keep it" keeps it); a file that is not a template is listed with Delete alone and the words "This saved team cannot be read. Delete it, or fix the file by hand."; and the folder they are kept in is named. Renaming keeps when the team was saved and moves the file to the new name's slug, refused when another saved team holds it; deleting removes the file; neither records an event, and neither changes a project's team.

## 5. The harness

This section is the heart of the specification. Everything else could be rebuilt; this is what makes Farik worth building.

### 5.1 Principles

Contracts before work. No agent starts a task that lacks a contract passing the Definition of Ready. This applies to tasks the user creates by hand too. Every request from the user is triaged and becomes an epic contract or a single task contract first; tasks otherwise exist only as the breakdown of an approved epic (5.16).

Nobody grades their own homework. The reviewer on a contract is never the assignee, and the human's send-back of a result comes after the reviewer has finished (5.2; added in 0.25). For the developer's code, the reviewer is the Architect; if the team has none, another Developer, since a Software Developer may review another Developer's work but never its own; if there is neither, no contract for a Developer can pass the Definition of Ready, and the Product Manager asks the human to add a reviewer agent, an Architect or a second Developer (decided 2026-09-15). The UI/UX Designer's code is reviewed by the Architect, and by a Developer when the team has no active Architect; every team has a Developer (F1), so its tasks always have a reviewer (6.8; added in 0.31). The human does not stand in as reviewer: the harness verifies agents' work with agents. For the Architect's designs, the reviewer is the Product Manager. The Product Manager's contracts are checked by the team's judge for completeness (the judgment part of the Definition of Ready; the judge is the Architect, else the Scrum Master, else the Product Manager, decided by the founder 2026-09-29, 5.3) and, above a risk threshold or for every epic, approved by the human. An epic's reviewer, the one who runs its exit criteria at the end, is the Product Manager when the Scrum Master broke it down and the human when the Product Manager did (5.16); it is the one place the human is a reviewer, and it costs nothing extra because the human accepts every epic anyway. The human does not run commands: when the human reviews an epic, Farik runs its `command`, `test`, and `artifact` criteria for them, on the integration branch, and the human answers the rest (5.4; ADR 0013; added in 0.8). Farik runs them the same way for the Product Manager when it is an epic's reviewer (added in 0.10).

Governance is code, not prompts. The prompts tell agents what good behavior looks like. The governor makes bad behavior impossible or expensive. A system prompt that says "never push to main" is a suggestion; a governor that returns a permission error is a rule.

Bounded everything. Every session has a token budget, a wall clock limit, a tool call limit, and an iteration limit. Hitting any of them is a normal, handled outcome that produces an escalation, not a crash.

Chat is not command. The channel exists for coordination and for the humans watching. The only way work enters the system is as a task with a contract. This rule is what stops a chatty team from talking itself into a rewrite. It holds through one tool (added in 0.14): the only way from the channel to work is `farik_create_task` without a parent, a request that goes to triage (an incident's fix is the one exception, 6.9; changed in 0.32) like one of the user's (5.16). A conversation session is given that tool and refused a `parent` on it, so it cannot file a task under an epic, and no other tool it has creates or moves work. A one-to-one chat (4.3; added in 0.34) holds the rule more tightly still: its session has neither that tool nor any other that files or moves work, only the reading tools and its reply, and a request its reply proposes is filed only when the user sends it, as the user's own.

Everything is a file, every action is an event. Contracts, decisions, memories, and role definitions are plain files in `.farik/`. Every tool call, state transition, message, and expense is an event in an append-only log. The user can reconstruct exactly what happened and why.

### 5.2 Task lifecycle

```
draft ──▶ refining ──▶ ready ──▶ assigned ──▶ in_progress ──▶ verifying ──▶ accepted
                        │            │             │               │
                        │            │             ▼               ▼
                        │            │          blocked         rejected ──▶ in_progress (bounded)
                        │            │             │
                        ▼            ▼             ▼
                    escalated    escalated     escalated ──▶ (human decides) ──▶ any state or cancelled
```

Transitions and who may trigger them:

| From | To | Triggered by | Gate |
|---|---|---|---|
| draft | refining | Product Manager picks it up | the request has been triaged (5.16; added in 0.3) |
| refining | ready | Governor, after PM submits contract | Definition of Ready (5.3), and the human's acceptance of the contract where it is required: an epic always, a `high` risk, or the team's policy (5.16 item 2, 5.12; added in 0.3) |
| refining | escalated | Governor | contract fails DoR three times, or human acceptance of the contract itself is required: risk is `high`, the policy says so, or the contract is an epic (5.16). An epic waiting for approval sits here with reason `approval` |
