| ready | assigned | Scrum Master, or the Product Manager when the team has no active Scrum Master, within WIP limits; a task waiting in the Backlog fills no agent's limit, so a leftover a later sprint plans again can put its assignee over the limit until it finishes (added in 0.36) | assignee role matches the contract, an epic's assignee being the Scrum Master, or the Product Manager when the team has no active one (5.16 item 3); the reviewer holds the contract's reviewer role, an epic's reviewer being the Product Manager when the Scrum Master broke it down and the human when the Product Manager did (5.1, 5.16 item 4), and is never the assignee; the assignee is below the team's work-in-progress limit, which counts every task it holds that is neither `accepted` nor `cancelled`, because nothing gates `assigned -> in_progress` and `rejected -> in_progress` hands a task straight back to the same agent, so counting only the started ones, or only those assigned, in progress and blocked, would let the limit be walked around (added in 0.3), except that, when the team plans its work in sprints, a task that waits in the Backlog for a sprint, such as one a sprint ended early left there with its assignee, is not counted, because it waits and is not being worked, so its assignee can take work the next sprint planned (section 3; added in 0.36); while a sprint is open, the task is in it, or is under an epic that is in no sprint, so that work begun under an epic before any sprint goes on, and its `max_cost_usd` fits what is left of the open sprint's budget, when it has one (changed in 0.12; tasks assigned before the sprint started go on as they were, and the orchestrator passes over a task this gate would refuse rather than asking for it); when the team plans its work in sprints (section 3; added in 0.36), a task is assigned only once it is in the open sprint, whatever its epic and whether or not a sprint is open, and is refused with "this team plans work in sprints, and <task> waits in the Backlog until a sprint plans it" in place of the sentence about the open sprint, while an epic passes, its assignment being preparation, and only a row in the open sprint is held to that sprint's budget; every dependency accepted and integrated (5.14; added in 0.2); a task of a role with a private folder (the Finance Specialist's, 6.6) is not assigned while another task holds that folder, whatever the assignee's room under the work-in-progress limit and however many agents of the role the team has: another task assigned to an agent, in any status but `accepted` and `cancelled`, whose own contract names a role with that folder, holds it (the task's role, not its agent's current one, so an agent who has left the team does not free it), so one sent back, blocked or escalated does, since `rejected -> in_progress` and `blocked -> in_progress` pass no assignment gate, and so does one waiting in the Backlog, whose copy of the folder is kept; the human's own moves of an escalated task (`escalated -> assigned`, `escalated -> in_progress`) are the existing override of the `escalated -> any` row and do not wait for the folder; the refusal is `private_folder_busy`, and the orchestrator passes over such a task rather than asking for it (added in 0.21; as built in 0.62) |
| assigned | in_progress | assignee starts session | none |
| in_progress | verifying | assignee declares done | every exit criterion the assignee can run has a recorded result with evidence from its own run, a `human` criterion being the human's to answer and a `review` one the reviewer's, both checked at acceptance instead (5.3, 5.4), and the task branch has at least one commit and a clean worktree (added in 0.2), or, for a task in a private folder (6.6), which has no branch, the assignee names the workbooks it wrote in `farik_request_transition`'s `workbooks`, one to twenty paths in the folder such as `books.xlsx`, and each is a file in the folder reached through no link: a request naming none is refused "name the workbooks you wrote", and each path that is no such file "<path> is not in your folder" (added in 0.21; as built in 0.62); an epic is asked none of that and asked instead that every task under it is accepted or cancelled and at least one is accepted, which is the whole of its gate (5.16 item 4; added in 0.3) |
| in_progress | blocked | assignee | a written blocker with what is needed |
| assigned | blocked | assignee | a written blocker with what is needed; Farik moves it so when the user retires the task's assignee before it started (added in 0.37) |
| blocked | in_progress | Scrum Master or human | blocker resolved, with a written resolution, so that the next session knows what changed (added in 0.3) |
| blocked | escalated | Governor | blocked for the configured limit or longer (default: 24 hours) |
| verifying | accepted | reviewer, then Product Manager | Definition of Done (5.4). Human acceptance required when risk is `high`. A task in a private folder's move carries the effect `nothing_to_integrate` (5.14; added in 0.62) |
| verifying | rejected | reviewer | written reasons mapped to failed criteria |
| verifying | rejected | human | the result waits for the human (5.4: an epic, risk `high`, or a `human` criterion), and, for a task, its review has passed; a written message, and optionally the failed criteria's ids (added in 0.25; ADR 0024) |
| rejected | in_progress | Governor | iteration count below limit (default 3, plus the extra tries the human granted, 5.7) |
| rejected | escalated | Governor | iteration limit reached, the limit being the default plus the extra tries granted (5.7) |
| any | escalated | Governor | a budget whose consequence is escalation is exhausted — the task's dollars or its sessions (5.5), a move Farik asks for on the task's behalf as soon as it sees the budget spent, whatever the task's status other than terminal, `escalated`, or waiting on the human (changed in 0.13) — or a permission was denied on a required action, or Farik could not run one of the task's criteria for its reviewer for a reason that is not the work's (5.4; added in 0.8), or a UI/UX Designer's plans for the task were returned as many times as the iteration limit, the default plus the extra tries granted, which escalates with the reason `iterations` and is checked after the budgets and before a denied permission (6.8; added in 0.31); a `stop` from the user takes the human's row below, which needs no gate, because the governor is not what hears the user (added in 0.3); the user's stop of a running session also aborts it, at once when Farik runs it in the process that heard the stop, and every later tool call of it is denied (added in 0.8) |
| any | cancelled | human only | none (added in 0.2) |
| escalated | any | human only | none |

More than one row can carry one move: three send a `refining` contract to `escalated`, and a blocked or rejected task has its own row and the governor's. The rows are taken in the order they appear here and the first whose gate opens is the one recorded, so the more specific reason is the one the user reads; when none opens, the refusal says what every gate it tried was waiting for. A row whose actor is the assignee or the reviewer is open only to the agent the contract names in that field, so one Developer cannot declare another's task done (5.1), and who asked for an assignment is the actor of the request rather than anything the runtime repeats back. A move to `escalated` raises an escalation whose reason is the row's: the readiness failures, an epic's approval or a task's risk gate, the blocker's age, the iteration limit, the exhausted budget (the task's sessions being their own reason), the denied permission, a criterion Farik could not run, which asks the human and so is `explicit_request` (added in 0.8), or the user's own request on the human's row (5.7). The approval row opens only once the contract passes the structural checks (5.16 item 2), so a contract that fails them is refined again rather than sent to the user, and the readiness row is the mirror of it: a contract that now passes is not escalated on its earlier failures, however many the runtime counted, so the two rows never both open and a contract that has been fixed reaches the user as an approval rather than as a failure. `iteration` counts returns: it goes up by one as the task goes from `rejected` back to `in_progress`, which is what `max_iterations` bounds, so a task gets `max_iterations` + 1 tries, plus the extra tries granted, and the web UI says the try in progress as `iteration` + 1 of that many (changed in 0.26 by step 08's landing review), and every move into `in_progress` clears the blocker and its time, including the one the human makes from `escalated`, because a task that escalated out of `blocked` kept its blocker and a stale one would age again. A block the runtime recorded no time for cannot be aged, so the governor refuses to escalate it and says so, as it does for any value it was not given; the time is stamped by the same decision that blocks the task, so that it is recorded rather than remembered. An agent id is compared once trimmed and a blank names nobody, which is why a task the contract names no assignee for cannot be moved by one (added in 0.3).

A UI/UX Designer's task (added in 0.31) stays `in_progress` from its first `explore` session to its `implement` session: its plan gate (6.8) adds events, not states, so the diagram and the table are unchanged but for the escalation above. Until the Product Manager has approved the task's latest plan, the governor refuses the Designer's `write_workspace`, `execute`, `git_local` and `git_remote` calls, and its `external_effect` connector calls without asking the human (changed in 0.39), in any session of the task with `design_plan_not_approved` (5.6), so no commit, and therefore no move to `verifying`, comes before the approval.

The governor judges an assignment on what the runtime tells it, and refuses rather than guessing when what it is told cannot be true: an assignment the runtime named no assignee for, a task that lists itself as a dependency, and two reports about the state of one dependency, or of one task under an epic, are all refused, because a dependency has one state and taking the first report would make the answer depend on the order they arrived in. A work-in-progress limit of zero refuses every assignment, which is how a team pauses an agent without retiring it. An epic whose reviewer is the human needs no reviewer agent, the human not being one (5.16 item 4; added in 0.3).

A one-to-one chat (4.3; added in 0.34) is no task and has no place in this lifecycle. Its rule is the first of a tick scoped to no task, before the sprint rules and the rules of work, and it is the one rule that runs while the team is paused (section 3), except while the pause is Farik's for a key the provider refused (5.5) (5.9).

`accepted` and `cancelled` are terminal: no transition leaves them, `any` in the table excludes them, and `any` never means staying in the same status. The governor is the only actor allowed to write the `status` field for transitions marked as its own. Agents request transitions through a tool; the governor evaluates and either applies them or refuses with a reason that goes back to the agent and into the log. A contract is frozen once its task leaves `refining` (5.11). The blocked-age rule measures from the moment the runtime stamped, so the runtime must never persist a block time later than the present and must re-stamp it if its clock is corrected backwards: the governor cannot age a task whose block time has not arrived, and it keeps waiting rather than escalating every blocked task the instant a clock steps back. When the orchestrator asks for a move on a task's behalf that it does not start a session for (an assigned task's start, a rejected task's return or escalation, an old block's escalation) and the governor refuses it, the task is passed over and that move is not asked again until the task next moves, so a refusal is recorded once and left on the board for the human rather than recorded on every pass (added in 0.8).

### 5.3 Definition of Ready

A contract passes when all of the following hold. The governor checks the structural ones mechanically; the team's judge checks the judgment ones and records the result (changed in 0.26).

Structural (mechanical):
- Intent is non-empty and states the user-facing reason for the task.
- At least one exit criterion exists, and every criterion has a `verification` with a `method` that is one of `command`, `test`, `artifact`, `review`, `human`. A `review` criterion is a rubric the reviewer answers and a `human` one a question only the user answers; neither is the assignee's to run, because nobody grades their own homework (5.1), so the gate on `in_progress -> verifying` does not ask the assignee for them and the Definition of Done asks the reviewer instead (added in 0.3).
- Every `command` and `test` criterion has the command to run and what a passing result looks like.
- Budget is set and does not exceed the remaining sprint budget, when the sprint has one. The sprint is the contract's own, the open one when the contract is in it, and a contract in no sprint is held to no sprint budget (changed in 0.12): a contract being refined is in none, but for a task filed under an epic already in the open sprint, and what goes into a sprint is held to its budget when it is planned (5.5), so a contract is never refused, refined again, and escalated for a sprint it is not in.
- The reviewer will not be the assignee. When the reviewer role differs from the assignee role, the team has at least one active agent of the reviewer role; when they are the same role (two Developers reviewing each other), the team has at least two active agents of it. The governor picks a reviewer agent other than the assignee at assignment (decided 2026-09-15).
- Risk level is set.
- Scope lists at least one `out_of_scope` item. (An empty exclusion list is a reliable predictor of scope creep, so we require the PM to think about it.)
- Every listed dependency exists and is at least `ready` (added in 0.2; moved here from the judgment list because it is mechanical).
- When no agent can review, the readiness result says which role to add (added in 0.3).
- The contract satisfies the team rules (5.12): the required verification methods are present, `allowed_paths` fall within the ceiling, and the budget is within the team maximum (added in 0.2).
- No `allowed_paths` entry reaches under `.farik/`, whatever the role or kind, because the files there change only through Farik's tools (5.8): an entry is refused when its first segment, after `.` segments are dropped and each `{a,b}` is expanded, contains `**` or matches `.farik` regardless of letter case, and it goes on below it or is `.farik` itself, so `.farik/**`, `**`, `**/*.md`, and `*/memory.md` are refused and `*.md` and `src/**` are not; the failure names the entries (added in 0.17). An entry with a backslash is refused outright, because the glob engine reads `\` as an escape and would otherwise match under `.farik/` through a segment this rule reads as not reaching it (added in 0.17). The one exception (added in 0.21; as built in 0.62) is a task of a role with a private folder (6.6), a task and not an epic: its `allowed_paths` may reach under its own folder, `.farik/local/finance/` for the Finance Specialist, and nowhere else under `.farik/`. An entry is within the folder as a path is within the team's ceiling (5.12): `.farik/local/finance/**`, a path in the folder and the folder itself are, and `.farik/local/**`, the sibling `.farik/local/financeX/**`, `**` and an entry with `..` are not, and the first three of those are still refused here as reaching under `.farik/`. Such a task is held to its folder by one rule of its own, `private_folder_task`, which takes the place of the ceiling and of the document paths below: every `allowed_paths` entry lies within the folder; no criterion is a `command` or a `test`, since the task has no worktree to run one in; an `artifact` criterion names a workbook as the folder holds it, by the path rule of 6.6 and never starting `.farik/`, and has no `must_contain`, since a workbook is not text; and the task has no `parent`, since an epic's paths cannot name `.farik/`. A command or a test the team's rules require (5.12) is not asked of it, and every other method they require still is. Its plain words: "A finance task works only in the private folder: no commands, no tests, no text searched in a workbook, and no parent epic."
- A task assigned to any role but the Software Developer and the UI/UX Designer keeps its `allowed_paths` inside the team's `document_paths` (5.12), because only those two change code; the failure names the paths outside (added in 0.11; the Designer added in 0.31). An allowed path is inside when it lies within a document glob as a path lies within the ceiling (`docs/adr/**` within `docs/**`), or when it has no wildcard and a document glob matches it (`README.md` and `notes/plan.md` by `**/*.md`). A path with a wildcard that lies within no document glob is outside (`notes/**`, `notes/*.md`), because it could name code. A document glob that does not compile puts every path outside, as 5.6 says of any glob, and the failure names the glob. An epic is not held to the rule; each of its tasks is, against its own assignee role. A task in a private folder is excepted here too, and from the team's ceiling (5.12), by the rule above (6.6; added in 0.21; as built in 0.62): its `allowed_paths` lie under `.farik/local/finance/`, which no document glob names.
- While the team has an active Marketing Specialist, a task assigned to any other role names no path that could reach `docs/marketing/`, where the brand kit, the brand persona and the marketing plans live (6.5); the failure, `marketing_paths_owned`, names the paths (added in 0.52; ADR 0042; built in 0.55, phase 7 step 08c). An entry could reach it when, after `.` segments are dropped, a backslash is read as a separator and each `{a,b}` is expanded, its first segment contains `**`, or matches `docs` regardless of letter case and either ends the entry as a literal, or its second segment contains `**`, or matches `marketing` regardless of letter case and the entry goes on or ends there as a literal; a segment that does not compile is taken to match, as for `.farik/` above. So `docs/**`, `docs`, `**/*.md`, `Docs/Marketing/x.md`, `docs/{marketing,adr}/**` and `docs[/]marketing/x` are refused, and `docs/adr/**`, `docs/*.md`, `src/**` and `*.md` are not. An epic is not held to it, each of its tasks is, and the Marketing Specialist's own tasks are not. The rule reads the agents active now, so a task made ready before the Marketing Specialist joined is not checked again. The Product Manager's `writing-task-contracts` and the Scrum Master's `keeping-work-flowing` tell them to name the folder a task needs, such as `docs/adr/**`, never `docs/**` or `docs`, while the team has a Marketing Specialist. Its plain words: "Only the Marketing Specialist changes the brand kit and the marketing plans, and this plan lets someone else."

Judgment (the team's judge, recorded as `contract.judged`; changed in 0.10 from a review event, and in 0.26 from the Scrum Master alone):
- The task is small enough to finish within its budget. If not, it goes back to the PM to be split.
- The criteria would actually detect the failure the intent worries about, not just that something ran.

How this release judges (added in 0.10; changed in 0.26, when the policy below was built). A team policy `judgment` in `team.yaml` holds three settings, each with a default, and the whole object may be left out. `required` is `always` (the default) or `never`: under `never` no contract is judged and the Definition of Ready holds on its other rules alone. `questions` is the rubric, up to five questions in plain language of 10 to 200 characters each, and defaults to the two above; the web UI also offers a third, "Is it small enough to finish in one go?". `judge` is `auto` (the default), `architect` or `scrum_master`. The judge is the role it names when it names one; under `auto` it is the Architect, else the Scrum Master, else the Product Manager, taking the first that has an active agent (the founder's rule, 2026-09-29: the Architect knows the code, and a team with neither still has a judge). Every team has an active Product Manager (F1), so a judge is always found. The Product Manager's exception: a team with neither an Architect nor a Scrum Master has the Product Manager judge the contracts it wrote itself; the mechanical rules above and the human's approval (5.16 item 2) stand beside it. A team with two active agents of the judge's role sends the session to the first, and either may record it.

The governor evaluates the judgment rules only once every other rule passes, so a contract that fails a structural, team, or parent rule is refused on those alone, and the Product Manager is not told of a missing judgment the judge has not yet been asked for; an epic's approval (5.16 item 2) therefore waits for the judgment too. When a contract is judged (5.16 item 2) and its Definition of Ready, evaluated as the governor would evaluate it, fails on the missing judgment alone, Farik first starts a judge session of purpose `refine`, on the judge's own model, given `farik_record_judgment` as its one tool and no built-in tool, whose message lists the team's questions numbered; that preview records nothing, so no readiness attempt is spent on a contract nobody has judged, and a contract that fails another rule goes back to the Product Manager without a judge session. `farik_record_judgment` takes `answers`, one for each question in order, each a `pass` (true or false) and a reason, and an overall reason that is not blank. It refuses anyone but the judge's role, and a task that is not `refining`, with `judgment_not_allowed`, whose message names the role that judges, and a count of answers that differs from the number of questions, with `judgment_answers`; it records `contract.judged` (8.5), with each answer carrying its question's text. The tool's description is the same for every team; the questions reach the judge in the session's message. Readiness reads the last `contract.judged` recorded after both the contract's last `contract.written` and where refining last began, and no judgment otherwise, so a contract written again after its judgment needs a new one; it does not compare the answers with the current questions, so a contract judged under an older rubric stays judged. A judgment with any answer no is a failed Definition of Ready like any other: it counts as one of the three attempts (5.2), and the Product Manager's next refine session is given it as its failures, naming each failed question, its reason, and the overall reason. A judgment session that ends without recording one is asked again on the next tick, as a refine session that writes nothing is, and the contract's `max_sessions` bounds both. An event recorded before 0.26 has no `answers`, only `fits_budget` and `criteria_detect_failure`; it is read as an answer to each of the two default questions, a missing boolean as a no.

The team editor holds the policy to section 10's foolproof configuration: a judge role no active agent holds, or an empty rubric with the judgment required, is refused before it is saved, in words that say what to change (built in 0.26, F15).

### 5.4 Definition of Done

A task is accepted when:

1. Every exit criterion has been run by the reviewer, independently of the assignee's run, and passed. A recorded result from the reviewer counts only when it carries evidence: a result with nothing in it is the "I ran the tests and they passed" this section exists to refuse. `human` criteria are satisfied only by an explicit human acceptance event, which needs no evidence of its own because the acceptance is the evidence.
2. No file outside the contract's `allowed_paths` was changed, and no protected path (5.6) was, even one inside them (added in 0.9), nor any path under `.farik/`, whatever the role (5.8; added in 0.17). The governor computes this from the git diff and refuses acceptance otherwise, naming the protected paths and the paths under `.farik/` apart from the ones outside the allowed paths.
3. The assignee wrote a completion note: what changed, what was not done, and anything the reviewer should look at first.
4. The reviewer wrote a review note that maps each criterion to evidence (a command output, a file path, a test name).
5. For tasks with risk `high`, and for every epic, the human has accepted.
6. A Software Developer's change to the interface has passed the UI/UX Designer's design review, when the team has a Designer (below; added in 0.33).

Verification runs in a fresh session for the reviewer. The reviewer does not receive the assignee's transcript, only the contract, the diff, the completion note, and the tools to run the criteria. This is deliberate: a reviewer that reads "I ran the tests and they passed" is measurably worse than one that runs the tests.

How this release verifies (added in 0.8). When a task reaches `verifying`, Farik runs the contract's `command`, `test`, and `artifact` criteria itself, one at a time, in a sandbox of the task's made afresh for them rather than the one the assignee's commands ran in, so that nothing those commands left outside the worktree reaches the run, and records each result as the reviewer's run, recorded by the governor: that is item 1's run by the reviewer, independently of the assignee, and the reviewer is shown the results. The sandbox is fresh, but it mounts the task's worktree, so gitignored build output inside it, such as `target/`, is still what the assignee left (added in 0.9). A criterion Farik could not run because the task's container went, which the work's own commands can cause, is recorded as failed with the error as its evidence, and the next one runs in a new sandbox; one it could not run for any other reason (git, the base-branch sandbox, a file it could not write, a command that would not start) is not the work's fault, so the governor escalates the task with reason `explicit_request` and the error as its detail, rather than send it back to an assignee who cannot fix it. The reviewer may not record those criteria itself, so that it cannot record a pass over Farik's failure, and no agent records a `human` criterion, which only the human's acceptance answers. The reviewer answers the `review` criteria and writes the review note; it may not commit or push, because nobody rewrites what they grade. When a criterion the reviewer ran failed, Farik files the rejection in the reviewer's name, with the failed criteria's ids and the review note as its reasons. When every criterion passed, the Product Manager's session is asked to accept; a task of risk `high`, or with a `human` criterion, waits for the human instead. A request the governor refused during a verification is left on the board for the human rather than asked again, whoever made it: a refused request from the reviewer's or the Product Manager's own session, a rejection the reviewer asked for itself among them, leaves the task waiting for the human even where Farik would otherwise have filed the rejection from the review note. Once the human has accepted the result of a task of risk `high` or with a `human` criterion, the Product Manager's session is asked to accept it, the acceptance standing for each `human` criterion. Each verification is summed up once in a `review.recorded` event. The reviewer's session is given Claude Code's read-only built-in tools alone: the `PreToolUse` hook judges a call by the agent's tiers, or by its kit tag for a connector's tool (5.6, 6.7; added in 0.22), rather than by the session's purpose, so a reviewer whose role holds `write_workspace` would otherwise be allowed to write in the worktree it reviews, and that allowlist is what stops it. Neither the reviewer's session nor the Product Manager's acceptance session is offered `farik_exec`, `farik_git_commit`, or `farik_git_push`: neither has a sandbox to run a command in, and git writes are refused to anyone but the assignee, so the tools are left off the session's list rather than offered and refused; `farik_git_status` and `farik_git_diff`, which only read, stay for an agent whose tiers hold `git_local` (added in 0.9).

A task in a private folder (a Finance Specialist's; added in 0.21, as built in 0.62) has no diff and no worktree. Its criteria are `artifact`, `review` and `human` (5.3). Farik checks each `artifact` criterion itself, on the host and with no sandbox, as the reviewer's run: the path, a workbook named as the folder holds it, passes when it is a regular file in the folder reached through no link, none of the folder's parts from the project's root down (`.farik`, `.farik/local`, the folder) being one and none of the artifact's, and fails, with its reason as the evidence, when it is missing, a folder, a link or through one, or not a workbook's path; a criterion that searches a workbook for text fails. A `command` or `test` criterion of such a task, which readiness refuses, is one Farik cannot run, and escalates it as any criterion it cannot run. Item 2 is checked against the folder: when the task is assigned, Farik copies every regular file of the folder but `.history/` to `.history/<task-id>/`, once, so a task sent back and assigned again keeps its first copy; the files that differ from that copy (their bytes, not their times), are new to it, or are gone from it are the task's diff, each path under the folder, and `allowed_paths` hold them as they hold a branch's. The protected paths (5.6) and `.farik/` (item 2 above, 5.3) except the task's own folder and nothing else under it: a path that climbs out, the sibling `.farik/local/financeX/`, and any other path under `.farik/` are refused as for any task. One piece of work touches the folder at a time (5.2, 6.6), so the copy is the state the task started from. Its reviewer's first message, in place of a diff, lists each changed file in one untrusted block: its path, whether it is new, changed or removed, and its size, a removed file's being the copy's, with how to read a file with `farik_read_sheet` and its copy from the start of the task with `farik_read_sheet` and `baseline: true` (6.6). The reviewer's session and the Product Manager's acceptance session run in the folder, with no executor, and are given `farik_read_sheet` for it. `task.diff` answers such a task with `diff` empty, `files` the changed paths in the folder (none until the task has its copy, which its assignment takes), `added` and `removed` 0 and `private_folder: true`, and the task's page and its gate say where the changes are and list the names, and its page says there is nothing to add to the project, since acceptance was its end; `farik task show --diff` prints no diff. The list is read against the folder as it is when asked, so once a later task has changed the folder it names that task's files too; the reviewer reads it while the task is the only one holding the folder.

A UI/UX Designer's task (added in 0.31) is verified as a Developer's is, items 1 to 5 unchanged. Its reviewer is the Architect, else a Developer (5.1), and the reviewer's session can come only after the Designer's `implement` session, which Farik starts only once the Product Manager has approved the task's plan (6.8).

The design review (added in 0.33; ADR 0026, the founder's decision of 2026-09-30). A Software Developer's change to the interface is checked by the UI/UX Designer in the browser before the Architect reviews it, and only a change it passed reaches the Architect. A change is to the interface when the task's assignee is a Software Developer and either its contract sets `ui_change: true`, a content field (5.11), or a path its branch changed since its merge base with the integration branch matches the team rule `ui_paths` (5.12); the worktree is never read, since Farik's own criterion runs write into it. A glob of `ui_paths` that does not compile, or a path that climbs with `..`, counts as a change to the interface. A task of any other role's is never one. When a task with such a change reaches `verifying` on a team with a Designer that is not retired, Farik runs its criteria as above and then, before the reviewer's session, reads the latest `design_review.recorded` since the task entered `verifying`: one that passed lets the Architect's review go ahead; one that failed is filed as the rejection, `verifying → rejected` in the Designer's name, with the review's reasons and no failed criterion, which the governor admits from the Designer alone and only with written reasons (5.2); it counts as a try, as any rejection does, and after it the next `verifying` needs the Designer's pass again. A recorded answer is acted on even if the preview or the Designer has since gone, since it needs neither. With none recorded, the task waits, and no session starts, while the team has no preview set (`preview_missing`), while the Designer cannot open the app because the sandbox is off or Docker is gone (`designer_needs_sandbox`), while the active Designer has Playwright off (`designer_needs_browser`; added in the step's fix wave, since a Designer without its browser is given no work), and while every Designer is paused (`waiting_on_designer`); otherwise Farik starts the first active Designer's design review, a `verify` session in the task's worktree with the read tier's built-ins, the Playwright connector (5.6), and seven Farik tools: `farik_read_task`, `farik_read_board`, `farik_read_rules`, `farik_read_criteria`, `farik_read_decisions`, `farik_check_page`, and `farik_record_design_review`, with no `farik_exec`. Its message holds the diff, as untrusted text, and the preview's first page. It checks the page at 360 and at 1280 pixels, each in the light and the dark theme, with `farik_check_page` (8.3), and ends with `farik_record_design_review { pass, reasons }`, which records `design_review.recorded` with a copy of the session's latest check of each of the four. The tool is refused with `design_review_refused` to anyone but the Designer in a `verify` session about a task, with `blank_reason` for blank reasons, and with `design_review_incomplete`, naming the checks missing, until all four exist, so Farik's own measurement, not the agent's word, stands behind a pass (5.1). If recorded again in the same session, the latest counts. A design review session that ends with no record is started again, and each restart counts toward the contract's sessions allowance (5.5). Without a Designer, or with every Designer retired, the Architect reviews alone, and item 6 holds.

An epic (added in 0.8 for an epic the human reviews; ADR 0013; changed in 0.10 to every epic, whoever reviews it). Item 1 holds for an epic without an exception. While any task under the epic is still awaiting integration, nothing is run, because its criteria are meant for the integrated work. Then Farik runs each of the epic's `command`, `test`, and `artifact` criteria once per verification, on a detached worktree of the integration branch's head with the network off, and records each as the reviewer's run, recorded by the governor, its evidence opening with the commit it ran at. A `test` criterion's new-tests check stays with the epic's tasks, since an epic has no branch to compare. The human may accept any epic's result only once every one of those runs passed, and only with a message, whoever reviews the epic (changed in 0.10). For an epic the human reviews, the acceptance then stands for the reviewer's answer to each `review` criterion and the human's to each `human` criterion, and its message is the review note. The Product Manager's session is then told the human accepted and asks for `accepted`, and the epic's `review.recorded` names the human as its reviewer. When a run failed, the human escalates the epic and sends it back to `in_progress` with a message saying what is missing, and the Product Manager files the tasks for it.

An epic the Product Manager reviews (added in 0.10), which is one the Scrum Master broke down (5.16 item 4), starts as every epic does, with nothing run while a task under it awaits integration and then Farik's runs on the integration branch. Then the Product Manager's `verify` session reviews it, with the read-only built-in tools a task's reviewer has, in the project root, since an epic has no worktree; its first message holds the epic's contract, Farik's results, and each task under the epic with its status and its last completion note, in place of a diff. It answers the `review` criteria and writes the review note, and at the end of that session the epic's `review.recorded` names the Product Manager as its reviewer. When a criterion failed, Farik files the rejection in the Product Manager's name, with the failed criteria's ids and the review note as its reasons, as it does for a task, and the governor's row returns the epic to `in_progress` (5.16 item 4). When the review note is written but a criterion other than a `human` one has no result of the reviewer's, the Product Manager's review session runs again, told which criteria are still unanswered, as a task's reviewer's does, and the epic waits for no acceptance until they are answered. When every criterion passed, the epic waits for the human's acceptance, which every epic needs (item 5) and which may come before or after the review; then the Product Manager's session, told the human accepted, asks for `accepted`.

What a human gate shows (added in 0.18). Three gates wait for the human: a contract's approval (5.16 item 2, and any contract the risk or the team's policy sends to the human), the acceptance of a task of risk `high` or with a `human` criterion (item 5), and the acceptance of an epic's result. Each shows the human three things, so that a user who cannot read code can decide and one who can may check. The first is a plain-language summary, written by the agent that asks: for a contract, a summary for the human that the Product Manager writes in the contract; for a result, the opening of the assignee's completion note (item 3) and of the review note (item 4), each of which starts with one. The second is the diff: a task's branch against the integration branch, an epic's as its tasks' integrated diffs, and, for a contract, which changes no code yet, the contract in full. The diff is always one step away and never required. The third is Farik's check results: the Definition of Ready's for a contract, and Farik's runs of the mechanical criteria (above) for a result. The summaries are required (changed in 0.25; phase 6 step 07). A contract's `summary` is a string of 20 to 600 characters, optional in the schema and a readiness rule (`summary_present`, 5.3) where a human decides on the plan: every epic, and every task the team's policy `human_accepts_contracts` asks the human to approve, which is a `high` risk task under `high_risk` and any task under `all`; a plan without one fails with "the plan has no summary for the user; write two or three plain sentences they can decide on". The Scrum Master's breakdown tasks are bound only when that policy asks. A completion note and a review note must open with a summary: the first paragraph, up to the first blank line, of 20 to 600 characters, and `farik_write_note` refuses one that does not with `summary_missing: open the note with two or three plain sentences for the user, then a blank line`. Progress notes, an agent's or Farik's, are exempt. The contract's `summary` is a different thing from the `summary` on `task.created` and `contract.written`, which is the log's one line. The human sends a result back (5.2) with `farik send-back <task> <message> [--criterion <id>]…` or the web UI; a task's send-back is refused with `review_first` until its review has passed (a review passing is the latest `review.recorded` since the task entered `verifying`), and a result that does not wait for the human with `not_waiting_for_the_human`. The criteria a send-back names are held as a reviewer's rejection's are: a blank id, or one the contract has no criterion of, refuses it; a send-back may name none (added in 0.25's landing review). A contract awaiting approval is sent back to `refining` the same way, with `not_awaiting_approval` otherwise. The send-back counts as a try, as any rejection does. What waits on the human, as the web UI and `farik run` and `farik plan` list it, holds a task's result once its review has passed since it last entered `verifying`, and an epic's as soon as it is there (added in 0.25's landing review).

### 5.5 Budgets and limits

Five budgets, each enforced by the governor when it is set, all configurable per team, the three dollar ones optional (ADR 0015), the others with role-based defaults:

| Budget | Default | On exhaustion |
|---|---|---|
| Per session (tokens) | 400k input, 40k output | session ends with a note from Farik; the task keeps its status and the next session resumes from the note (changed in 0.13, which no longer moves the task to `blocked`) |
| Per task (dollars) | set in contract, PM proposes | task to `escalated` |
| Per task (sessions) | `max_sessions` in the contract, default 14 (added in 0.2; the default raised from 5 in 0.9, and to 14 in 0.17) | task to `escalated` |
| Per sprint (dollars) | set when the human starts the sprint, optional (changed in 0.12) | no new assignments; in-progress tasks may finish |
| Per day, team-wide (dollars) | set by the user, optional | everything pauses; user is notified |

Plus non-monetary limits: a session wall clock (default 30 minutes), a tool-call count per session (default 200), and the rejection iteration limit (default 3; the human may grant more tries, 5.7). Per-role defaults: the Scrum Master's session budget is 200k input and 20k output tokens; every other role uses the team default. Farik ships no dollar limit: the daily budget, a sprint's budget, and the team's cap on a task's budget (5.12) are each the user's to set, and one left out is no limit of that kind (ADR 0015); the token, wall-clock, tool-call, session, and iteration limits ship as above, because they bound a loop rather than spending.

More than one budget can be exhausted at the same moment, and every consequence applies. The governor reports all of them rather than the first, because none of these consequences subsumes another: stopping new assignments does not end a session that is already running, so a sprint that has run out must never hide a day that has. A budget is exhausted when what was spent reaches its limit, and a spend that is not a number counts as exhausted.

Costs are computed from the usage fields returned by the model API and from the model's published price table, which Farik ships as a versioned file the user can override at `.farik/prices.json` (added in 0.6). An override whose `version` this program does not read is refused rather than read as the version it knows, because a later format priced as this one would price a session by guesswork. An override replaces the shipped table as a whole rather than being merged into it, so a model the user took out of it is not priced. Each usage report a session returns is recorded as one `cost.recorded` event carrying the dollars computed at that moment, so a later change to the prices does not rewrite what was charged; a report for a model the table in use does not price is recorded with its tokens, a cost of zero and `unpriced: true`, and no dollar limit counts it, so that an agent can run on any model its user chooses (ADR 0015); `farik doctor` and every start of `farik run` name such a model. A budget's `budget.exhausted` event is recorded once, when it crosses from having room to none (added in 0.8). The daily budget's day is the UTC date the cost was recorded on, because the log does not know the user's time zone; a team eight hours behind UTC sees its day end at four in the afternoon (added in 0.8).

The period of a connector allowance (added in 0.45; ADR 0037) is the open sprint's, else the UTC day as the daily budget counts it, from the later of its start and the last `sprint.ended`.

This release enforces the table above (changed in 0.13; 0.8 enforced its minimum): every session's cost is recorded, one that reports no usage as a session costing nothing; a session the orchestrator cannot go on with, because something failed after it started, is aborted and recorded as ended with `error` before the failure is reported; token and dollar budgets are checked when a session reports its usage, which Claude Code does once, at the session's end, so a live session is bounded by its wall clock and its tool-call limit alone (corrected in 0.9); a session limit, its task's dollars, or a daily budget that is set, crossed by what a session reported, is recorded as exhausted then, a session limit with the consequence `end_session_with_note`, and a task's dollars or a daily budget crossed during a session stops the next one from starting rather than cutting that one short. An `implement` session, the one purpose whose next session reads the task's last note (8.2), that ended at a limit (its wall clock or its turns) or at its model provider's limit, or whose usage crossed its tokens, its wall clock, or its tool calls, gets one `note.written` of kind `progress`, written by `farik`, naming each cause and what the session said, and quoting the agent's own last progress note of that session when it wrote one, so the agent's words are kept: "This session stopped at <causes>: <detail>. Your last note in it: <text>. Resume from the last commit and this note." The task keeps its status. A session of any other purpose leaves no note, and its next session is asked again from its own first message. A task whose dollars or sessions are spent is escalated by the governor (5.2), with the reason `budget` or `sessions`, before the other rules look at it, so a `verifying` task out of sessions is escalated before Farik runs its criteria; `farik plan` and `farik contract new`, which do not govern work in progress, pass such a task over as before. A session that ended at the provider's limit counts toward its task's `max_sessions` like any other that started. The default of 14 is what a task's normal path needs before its iteration limit escalates it (changed in 0.17): triage, refinement, the Scrum Master's judgment, plan, and acceptance, then an implementation and a review for each of `max_iterations` 3 passes and the one after them, plus one for a session a provider's limit cut short, so that a task that passes on its last allowed iteration is accepted rather than escalated for its sessions, and `max_sessions` bounds only the loops the iteration limit cannot see. No session starts for anyone once a daily budget that is set is spent, until there is room again; no agent is paused for it, and the channel line that tells the user comes with the channel. The governor's own moves, which cost nothing, still run. A one-to-one chat's session (added in 0.34) is recorded with the purpose `chat` and no task, so it counts toward the day and toward no sprint and no task; no chat session starts while the daily budget is spent, the user's message being kept and answered once there is room again, and the chat saying why (F8). The Costs page shows today's chat spending as "Conversations today" (F17).

The sprint budget is enforced from phase 4 (added in 0.12). When the team plans its work in sprints (section 3), only a row in the open sprint is held to its budget at assignment, so an epic broken down outside the sprint is not (added in 0.36). It is read from the open sprint, its `budget_usd` in `.farik/sprints/S<n>.yaml`, and with no sprint open, or one started with no budget, there is no sprint limit. What a sprint has spent is every `cost.recorded` of a task that was in it when the cost was recorded, so a cost stays with the sprint it was spent in when its task later leaves; a planning session is about no task, so its cost counts against the day and against no sprint. The assigner's plan is refused when the `max_cost_usd` of what it plans, added to that of the tasks already in the sprint, exceeds the budget, an epic counting once, its own budget covering its tasks. Once the sprint's budget is spent, no new assignment starts in it: the governor refuses one that does not fit what is left, and the orchestrator starts none; a session already running is not aborted for it, and the tasks already assigned go on ("in-progress tasks may finish"). The daily budget stays enforced as above.

A model provider's usage limit (added in 0.13). A session can stop because the account that pays for it has used what its model provider allows for now, which is none of the budgets above. Farik reads it from the session's stream (8.2): a session whose result is an error ended at its provider's limit when the last rate-limit report of the session has a status that does not start with `allowed` (so a warning is not a limit, and neither is a report with no status), when the result's API status is 429, or when what the result says contains `usage limit` or `rate limit` in any letter case. It reads all three because no capture of a session its provider refused exists yet, Claude Code 2.1.280's captures showing only `allowed`: each form is tested on a synthetic transcript, and a refusal in a shape none of them matches ends as an ordinary `error`, the residual this rule accepts. The session ends `provider_limit`, and its agent sleeps: Farik records `agent.slept` with the time the agent wakes, the reset time the provider last reported when that is later than now, else one hour from now. An agent is asleep while its last `agent.slept` names a time later than now. Sleep is not a status: the team file is not written, the agent stays `active` (F1), and work is still assigned to it within its work-in-progress limit; only its own sessions wait, no rule starting one or making a sandbox for it until it wakes. Farik's own criterion runs are not an agent's and are not held up. A tick with nothing to do but wait for a sleeping agent says so, naming the agent and when it wakes, and the run waits until the first one wakes and ticks again rather than ending (8.2), because a run that ended would leave the sleeping agent's work for nobody. A key the model provider refuses (added in 0.30). A session whose result is an error with the API status 401 ended because the provider refused the AI account's credential, which Claude Code 2.1.285 reports after retrying: its captured transcript is the test. A result with the API status 403, which is how the provider refuses a key whose organisation is revoked or disabled, is the same refusal, and so is a third retry of a 401 or a 403: Claude Code retries a refused subscription token twice before its result, but a refused API key ten times over about three minutes, so the session ends at the third retry of one request, which the retry's own attempt number says, and the user is not left waiting in silence; single retries spread over a session, as when a subscription token is refreshed, never add up (changed in 0.37). The session ends `credential_refused`. It is not its task's fault, so it does not count toward the task's `max_sessions`, leaves no note, and puts no agent to sleep; and since the same key fails every session alike, Farik pauses the team at once with one `team.paused` whose `by` is `farik`, its `reason` `credential_refused`, and its `detail` what the provider said, unless the user paused the team while the session ended, whose pause then stands. No session starts after it until the user connects the account again, which resumes a team paused for this reason, or resumes the team themselves; a team the user paused stays paused when the account is connected. Farik does not try the key when it is connected, because a try would spend the user's tokens; a transient 401 therefore pauses the team too, and the user resumes it. A sprint's planning session that stopped at a limit or at the provider's limit is asked again: a sprint has had its planning once a planning session after its start ended `completed`, `aborted`, or `error`, or once three have, whatever their ends, so that one that keeps hitting its limit is not asked for ever.

### 5.6 Permissions

Permissions are capability tiers attached to a role, overridable per agent by the user. The governor checks them on every tool call.

| Tier | What it allows | Default for |
|---|---|---|
| `read` | read files in the project, read `.farik/`, search | everyone |
| `write_workspace` | write files under `allowed_paths` in the task contract | Developer, UI/UX Designer (added in 0.31), Architect, Marketing |
| `execute` | run commands inside the sandbox | Developer, UI/UX Designer, Architect |
| `network` | outbound HTTP from the sandbox, web search | Marketing, Architect, PM, Finance (added in 0.21) |
| `git_local` | commit on a task branch | Developer, UI/UX Designer, Architect, Marketing |
| `git_remote` | push, open pull requests | nobody by default; user grants explicitly |
| `external_effect` | anything that changes state outside the sandbox: posting to services, sending mail, deploying | nobody by default; each use requires human approval unless pre-authorized per tool, which a connector's tool never is (changed in 0.38), or, for the DevOps Engineer's `farik_deploy`, `farik_restart` and `farik_roll_back`, approved as 6.9 says (changed in 0.32), or, for a post and a Google Ads change the owner's approved marketing plan covers, approved by that plan as 6.5 says (ADR 0042; changed in 0.52), and a post outside the plan waits for the owner's yes on Today and holds no task (changed in 0.57); from phase 7 step 10h, a team whose `approvals` policy is `auto` runs a connector's call without asking, within its allowance and the spending limits (ADR 0041; changed in 0.50) |

The Architect and the Marketing Specialist hold `write_workspace` and `git_local` because a task reaches `verifying` only with a commit on its branch, and each commits the documents that are its work; neither writes application code (6.3, 6.5; changed in 0.10, when the Architect's `write_workspace` was for spikes and neither held `git_local`). The user's setup screen asks about `execute` and `git_remote` explicitly because those are the two that can hurt. The two answers are the team's policy `permissions: { run_commands, push }` in `team.yaml` (defaults `true` and `false`), and they apply to every agent of the role, one added later among them: an agent's tiers are its role's defaults, without `execute` for Developers, UI/UX Designers and Architects when `run_commands` is `false`, with `git_remote` for Developers and UI/UX Designers when `push` is `true` (the Designer added in 0.31), and then its own grants and revokes, a revoke winning. A UI/UX Designer's `write_workspace`, `execute`, `git_local` and `git_remote`, and its `external_effect` connector calls (changed in 0.39), are further held by its plan gate, which the hook and Farik's tool server read from the log at each call (5.2, 6.8; added in 0.31). A "No" to commands leaves every check to Farik, which runs them itself (added in 0.26). `git_remote` governs what an agent's session may do. When Farik itself pushes accepted work or opens a pull request, it does so under the team's `integration` policy (5.14). No agent is acting, so no tier applies. The setup screen and `farik init` therefore say which policy the team has, and, under `auto_merge`, that Farik pushes the integration branch to `origin` when the repository has one (ADR 0012). Farik's coordination tools (reading the board, triage, writing a contract, asking for a transition, recording a result, writing a note, asking the human, writing a product document) all need only `read`, which every role holds: what governs each is the rule it names (5.2, 5.11, 5.16), checked by the tool itself, not a tier of its own (added in 0.8). `farik_exec` needs `execute`; git is four tools, `farik_git_status`, `farik_git_diff`, and `farik_git_commit` under `git_local` and `farik_git_push` under `git_remote`, so that each is checked as its own tool. `farik_git_commit` gives git each path it names as a path, never as a glob or a pathspec, and refuses one that is a directory in the worktree, because the path checks judge the name the agent gave, and a directory or a pattern would stage a protected file under it that no check saw (added in 0.9). `farik_exec` refuses a command when any segment of it (split on `&&`, `||`, `;`, `|`, and newlines, without parsing quotes) runs git, because git is a Farik tool with its own tiers (ADR 0004). A segment runs git when its first word is `git`, or when its first word is a `NAME=value` assignment or one of the wrappers `env`, `sudo`, `doas`, `command`, `exec`, `nohup`, `time`, `timeout`, `nice`, `setsid`, `xargs` and `git` appears anywhere later in the segment, which catches `sudo -u root git push` at the cost of refusing `sudo apt install git`. A word is read bare: quotes and shell punctuation are stripped, a path keeps only its last segment, a `.exe` suffix is dropped, and a redirection is not a command name. A call hidden in a subshell, a variable, or a script (`$(git push)`, `GIT=git; $GIT push`, `sh -c "git push"`) is the residual ADR 0004 accepts.

Protected paths (added in 0.2). A team rule (5.12) lists globs that no tool may read or write whatever the tier, so that secrets kept in the repository never enter a session. The default list is `.env`, `.env.*`, `**/*.pem`, `**/*.key`, and `.farik/local/**`. Protected and allowed globs match the whole path relative to the project root, after backslashes become `/` and `.` segments are dropped: `*` stays within one directory, `**` crosses directories, a bare name such as `.env` names the root file only and `**/.env` names it anywhere, protected globs match without regard to letter case and protect a directory they name along with its children, an absolute path, an empty path, or a path with a `..` segment is always refused, and a glob that does not compile refuses the check rather than matching nothing. The Definition of Done holds a task's diff to the same list (5.4 item 2), because a command run under `execute` can write a protected file that no tool call named (added in 0.9).

Tools that write to a private folder of Farik's own (added in 0.56). A tool of the `read` tier may write to Farik's own files: `farik_write_memory` the agent's notebook (5.8), and `farik_write_sheet` a workbook in the Finance Specialist's private folder, `.farik/local/finance/` (6.6). A tool that writes to a private folder reports no path to the check above and holds its own folder line instead: it refuses a path outside its folder, a path through a link and a path of another shape (`private_path_refused`), so `.farik/local/**` stays a protected path for every other call. A session about a task in a private folder runs with that folder as its working directory (8.2), and the hook judges each path of a built-in tool against the working directory, so such a session reaches the folder and nothing outside it, and no exception to the protected `.farik/local/**` is made for it (as built in 0.62).

An MCP tool is tagged, not tiered (changed in 0.38). When the user connects an MCP server of their own to an agent, Farik lists the server's tools and the user labels each one (6.7; changed in 0.38, from "read-only or side-effecting"); a connector from a role's kit comes tagged, and the user tags nothing (6.7; added in 0.22). A tag (added in 0.22) is `network`, `external_effect`, or `denied`; `denied` is not a tier but a tool the agent is never offered. A tool's tag governs the call whatever the agent's own tiers, a kit's, the built-in's and a user's server's alike, so a Developer without `network` reads library documentation through its connector: the hook judges a connector's call by its tag alone and asks no tier (changed in 0.38, from a tier check at `network` after the tag). Untagged tools default to `external_effect`. `preauthorized_external_tools` never applies to a connector's tool: the hook does not consult it for an `mcp__<server>__<tool>` call (added in 0.38). An `external_effect` connector tool stays offered, and a call to it waits for the human, who allows that one call or refuses it (changed in 0.39, from refused `external_effect_refused` in 0.38; ADR 0031). With no grant that matches it, the hook records `tool_approval.requested { server, tool, input, input_sha256 }`, whose sequence number is the approval's id, and denies the call `approval_needed: <server> <tool> waits for the human (approval <seq>)`, which stops the session (8.2); the task then waits on the human (5.7). `input` is the call's whole input as compact JSON, never cut, so the human sees all of what they allow. An input whose compact JSON is over 64 KiB is not asked about: it is denied `tool_input_too_large: <tool>'s input is over 64 KiB, too long to show you, so it is refused`, grant or not, and only its `tool.denied` is recorded. A grant allows exactly one later call: by the agent that asked, on the same task, to the same server and tool, with the same `input_sha256`, the sha256 of the input's canonical JSON (compact, object keys sorted at every depth, as `spec_sha256`'s, 6.7), in a session of that agent about the task that started after the grant. That call is allowed, and its `tool.called` carries `approval: <seq>`, which uses the grant up; the hook finds the grant and records its use while holding the daemon's one sessions lock, so two identical calls after one grant cannot both run, and the second asks again. The grant lapses when a session of the asking agent about the task that started after it ends without the call, and a later call asks again. Another agent's session about the task, a reviewer's, cannot use it, and a changed field asks again. A session about no task has nothing to wait on, and a user's servers are given to none (8.2): its call is refused `external_effect_refused: <tool> of <server> changes something outside the sandbox, and only a session about a task can ask you to allow it`, without asking (added in 0.39). A session is given Farik's own server, the one built-in connector where the next paragraph says, and its agent's own servers where 8.2 says, and no other (added in 0.8; the built-in connector added in 0.33, the user's servers in 0.38); the hook takes any tool named `mcp__<server>__<tool>` that is not Farik's for a connector's, and denies one whose server the session was not given `connector_not_in_session` (changed in 0.38, from `tool_not_allowed`, no tier).

The order of a connector call in the hook (added in 0.45; ADR 0037): the session's connector, the tag, `denied`, the preview's `url`s, the Designer's plan gate, the 64 KiB limit, the human's grant for this exact call, then the tool's allowance while the agent's calls of it this period are under it, then the ask. A grant is used before the allowance. The count and the `tool.called` it is checked against are read and recorded under the daemon's one sessions lock, so two calls at the last place cannot both run; a call the allowance lets through records `allowance: <n>`.

Connectors per agent (added in 0.33; ADR 0026). An agent's `mcp_servers` in `team.yaml` lists the connectors it is given, each `{ name, source: builtin }` or, added in 0.38, a server of the user's own, `source: custom` (6.7), at most 20 and no name twice on one agent (`connector_name_twice`, added in 0.38); a name is lower-case letters, digits and dashes, starting with a letter, at most 40, never an underscore, because the hook splits `mcp__<server>__<tool>` at the first `__` (changed in 0.38); and a built-in name Farik does not ship is refused with `unknown_connector` at the entry. This release ships one, `playwright`, the Playwright MCP server, a browser confined to the project's preview (8.3). A connector may also be `source: kit` (added in 0.44; 6.7, ADR 0036): a service of the agent's role's kit, written whole into the team file with the kit's tags as its `tools`, held to every rule of a custom entry, and refused at connect unless it is exactly the kit's. The browser's definition now lives in the Designer's kit. Any agent may have it, and the team builder gives it to the UI/UX Designer; the agent page switches it on and off (F9). A session is given it when its agent has it, its purpose is `explore`, `implement`, or the Designer's design review (5.4), and a preview can open: a preview is set and Docker's sandbox is on and reachable. The Designer's own connector being off takes it from no one else; it counts only where the Designer's assignment and design review are decided (5.4, 6.8). Such a session is registered with `network` added to its tiers (6.7), which a user's server never adds (changed in 0.38). Every tool of the connector is tagged in its pinned list (6.7): the tools that browse, `network`, reach only the preview; every other is `denied`, never offered and also passed to Claude Code as `--disallowedTools`. The hook judges a call to any connector's tool in this order (changed in 0.38, from a built-in connector's): the connector must be the session's (`connector_not_in_session`), the tool tagged (`tool_not_tagged`) and not `denied` (`tool_denied`), and, for a connector confined to the preview, every field named `url`, at any depth of the call's input, a string that is the preview's origin, `http://localhost:<port>`, exactly or followed by `/`, `?`, or `#` (`url_outside_preview`, which a `url` that is not a string gets too), whatever the tool's tag, so a grant never takes a call outside the preview (changed in 0.39, from `network` tools alone); a user's server has no such confinement. An `external_effect` call is first held by the Designer's plan gate, which refuses a UI/UX Designer's call before the Product Manager approves its plan `design_plan_not_approved`, as it refuses a write, and asks nothing (5.2, 6.8); once the checks above pass, it is refused `tool_input_too_large` over 64 KiB, allowed with a matching grant, or asked about (above; changed in 0.39, from `external_effect_refused`). Nothing else follows: the tier check at `network` no longer runs for a connector's call (changed in 0.38), and the plan gate runs for no other tag. `tool.called` and `tool.denied` record the server and the tag (8.5).

What the hook cannot see (added in 0.8). The hook judges a search by the directory it names, and a protected glob protects files, not the directories they sit in, so a `Grep` or `Glob` over a directory reads a protected file under it without naming it. For those, Farik relies on Claude Code: each protected path is given to the session as a `permissions.deny` rule `Read(<glob>)`, which Claude Code applies to its reading tools, searches included. That is enforcement by the program Farik drives, not by Farik, and it is the residual this rule accepts. A finance session's deny list is the team's, with no glob left out: its working directory is the finance folder, and Claude Code reads a `Read(<glob>)` rule relative to the working directory, so `Read(.farik/local/**)` names nothing it can reach, and the folder's workbooks are read through `farik_read_sheet` (6.6; changed in 0.62, from a deny list that left the folder out).

### 5.7 Escalation

An escalation is a task state and a message to the user. It carries: the task, the reason, what the agent tried, and the options the agent proposes. There are eleven reasons, written on the wire as `budget`, `sessions`, `iterations`, `blocker_age`, `permission`, `risk_gate`, `approval` (a contract waiting for the user's approval: every epic, and any contract the team's policy `human_accepts_contracts` sends to the human, 5.16; the contract's own `high` risk is `risk_gate` instead), `readiness_failures` (a contract that failed the Definition of Ready three times, 5.2), `integration` (5.14), `explicit_request`, and `preview` (changed in 0.33 from ten). A `preview` escalation is a preview Farik could not open for a session that needed it (8.3): a `prepare` that failed or ran past its fifteen minutes, a `start` that never answered, or Docker gone. Its detail is the error with the output's last 40 lines, `preview.stopped` records the reason, and no session starts; the human fixes the commands in Settings and resolves it. Farik does not retry, since a broken command fails the same way each time. An `integration` escalation is the one that moves nothing: the task it is about is `accepted`, which no row leaves, so it stays there, awaiting integration, with the escalation as a message to the user until they run `farik integrate` (5.14; added in 0.8). The user resolves it from the board or from the channel. Nothing else on the board waits for an escalation unless it depends on that task. A resolution names the status the task moves to and carries a message: the task moves out of `escalated` as the human asked, and on the move `escalation.resolved` records the status and the message, so a move the governor refuses leaves only its refusal. A resolution never takes a contract awaiting the user's approval to `ready`: approving it does, which records `human.accepted` of the contract and then moves it, so that a move the governor refuses leaves the approval on a contract still awaiting it. Nor does it take to `ready` an epic whose contract the user has not approved, one escalated for failing the Definition of Ready three times among them: that epic goes back to `refining`, and once its contract passes it waits for the approval (5.16 item 2). More tries (added in 0.25; ADR 0024). A resolution of an `iterations` escalation may carry `extra_tries`, from 1 to 5, recorded on `escalation.resolved`; on any other reason, or with a move to any status but `in_progress`, since more tries resume the work, it is refused with `extra_tries_only_for_tries` (the move added in 0.25's landing review), and a value outside 1 to 5 as invalid. The task's limit becomes the iteration limit plus the sum of its granted tries, and the resolve raises `iteration` by one, so the resumed attempt is counted and N more tries mean N more attempts; a resolution without `extra_tries` leaves `iteration` alone. The task's session limit grows by four for each try granted. The `integration` escalation offers no resolution: its one action is to add the accepted task to the project (5.14). A `budget` or `sessions` escalation resolved to a working status while the task's dollars or sessions are still spent is raised again on the next tick, which is how Farik says the budget is still spent: the user gives a task room by raising its `max_cost_usd` or `max_sessions`, a change to its content, which sends it back to `refining` (5.11; added in 0.13). Outside an escalation the user moves a task from any other status with a reason, which the move records, and which out of `blocked` is also the blocker's resolution and into `escalated` the escalation's words (added in 0.8).

The Scrum Master is responsible for making sure escalations do not pile up silently (changed in 0.15). Its planning ceremony (5.9), the Product Manager's when the team has no active Scrum Master, is given a digest and posts it in the planning thread: every open escalation with its task, title, reason, detail, and the hours it has waited, and every spent daily or sprint budget recorded since the previous sprint's planning. An open escalation is a task in `escalated`, with the reason and detail of its last `escalation.raised`, waiting since then, or an `accepted` task whose last `integration` escalation since it was accepted has no `task.integrated` after it. An escalation left waiting past the team policy `escalation_age_hours`, from 1 to 720, default 24, is aged once: Farik records `escalation.aged` with the hours it has waited, rounded down, and posts the system line `<task> has waited <hours> hours on the human: <reason>`, which mentions no one. One escalation is aged per tick, the oldest first, and only in `farik run` in a tick about no one task, after the channel's conversation rule. Aging happens only while something ticks: `farik run` ends when the board is idle, so an escalation that ages while no process drives the project is said on the next run. A new escalation of the same task is aged again. The web app's notification of it comes with phase 9, and the desktop app's with phase 13 (changed in 0.18, from the desktop app's in phase 6, and in 0.20; renumbered in 0.24 and by ADRs 0025 and 0029).

Questions (added in 0.2). An agent that needs an answer from the human before it can continue, a Product Manager refining a contract most often, asks it through the `farik_ask_human` tool. The session ends, the task keeps its status and is shown as waiting on the human, the question appears on the board and in the channel, and the human's answer starts the next session with the answer in its prompt. A question is not an escalation: nothing has gone wrong, and the answer is context, not a state decision. Escalations that carry a message from the human work the same way: the next session for that task starts with the message. A question is answered once, by the sequence number of its `question.asked`; the answer is recorded as `question.answered` with the question's task, and a task is shown as waiting on the human while any question about it is unanswered. The next session about a task is given, as the human's message, every answer (each after its question, which is marked as the asking agent's words), every resolution's message, and every acceptance's words recorded on the task since the last session about it started, in the order they were given (added in 0.8).

A connector's call that waits for the human (added in 0.39; ADR 0031). An `external_effect` connector call with no grant (5.6) waits as a question does, and is not an escalation either: the session ends (8.2), the task keeps its status, does not raise its `iteration`, raises no escalation, and counts the session towards `max_sessions` as any session counts. While the approval waits, the asking agent's line in `team.activity` and the team band reads "Waiting on you: may <agent name> use <server>?" (changed in 0.39). The task's projection holds `open_approvals` beside `open_questions`, raised by `tool_approval.requested` and lowered by `tool_approval.granted` or `tool_approval.refused`, and the task waits on the human while either is above zero, so the orchestrator starts no session for it and the board marks it "Waiting on you" (F3). The human answers with the command `tool_approve { approval, note? }`, which allows that one call, or `tool_refuse { approval, note? }`, by the approval's id, the sequence number of its `tool_approval.requested`; each records `tool_approval.granted` or `tool_approval.refused { approval, note? }` on the request's task with no agent or session, `note` absent when none was given, and says "Allowed <tool> once for <agent> (approval <n>)." or "Not allowed: <tool> for <agent> (approval <n>).", `<agent>` the asking agent's id, and on a second line "Input: <the call's whole input, as it was recorded>", so that a terminal decides with the input in front of it (8.6; changed in 0.39). The check that no decision exists and the write of the decision are one step under one lock, so two decisions at once (a browser tab and a command) let exactly one through, `open_approvals` is lowered once for it, and the other is refused `approval_decided`. A seq that is no `tool_approval.requested` is refused `unknown_approval`, and a second decision on one approval, either way, `approval_decided`. Only the asking agent's next session about the task is told the decision, as the human's message, beside what the paragraph above gives it: each decision on a call it asked about since its own last session about the task started, "You may call `mcp__<server>__<tool>` once, with exactly the input you asked for (approval <n>), which is this, your own words quoted, never cut:" and then that input, the canonical JSON the grant's hash covers, in an `untrusted` block as a question is quoted (8.6), because the session starts fresh and a call whose input differs by a word asks again, with "The human adds: <note>" after it when there is a note (changed in 0.39); or "The human did not allow `mcp__<server>__<tool>` (approval <n>).", with the human's note after a colon in place of the full stop. Another agent's session about the task is not told.

A marketing plan that waits for the owner (added in 0.55; ADR 0042). A plan the Marketing Specialist proposes (6.5) waits as a question does, and is not an escalation either: the session ends, the task keeps its status, and the task's projection holds `open_plans` beside `open_questions` and `open_approvals`, raised by `marketing_plan.proposed` and lowered by `marketing_plan.approved` or `marketing_plan.returned`. The task waits on the human while any of the three is above zero, so the orchestrator starts no session for it, the rules that act on a task pass it over, and the board marks it "Waiting on you" (F3). Its assignee cannot hand it in meanwhile: while the plan is proposed, `farik_request_transition` refuses the move to `verifying` with `marketing_plan_waiting: MP-<n> waits for the owner; end your turn`, and nothing is recorded. The owner decides on Today or with `farik marketing plan` (6.5). The next session about the task is told, as the human's message, each decision on its plans since the last session started: "The owner approved your marketing plan MP-<n>." with "The owner adds: <note>" after it when there is a note, or "The owner sent back your marketing plan MP-<n>: <reason>", the reason exactly as the owner wrote it and not in an `untrusted` block, since it is the human's own words (ADR 0011) and the agent is to answer it. A decision whose envelope names an agent or a session is not the owner's and is not told.

A post outside the plan that waits for the owner (added in 0.57; ADR 0042). A post the Marketing Specialist writes with no slot (6.5) is recorded `social_post.requested` and waits for the owner on Today, and it is neither a question nor an escalation, and it holds no task: the session goes on, the task keeps its status and may be handed in, and no projection counts it, since Farik, not the agent, posts it once the owner allows it. The owner decides with `social_post_decide` or `farik marketing post send` and `decline`; an undecided request whose time is within five minutes is recorded missed and leaves the list. The agent is told of it in its next `implement` session's message, after the rest, from its previous session on: each of its posts missed or failed, which the owner's yes could not save, with the reason as untrusted text, each the owner did not allow, with their note as their own words, and each the owner stopped (6.5). Posts go out between sessions: the hand-over is a rule with no model that runs on a tick, and a session runs to its end before the next tick, so a post can be handed over late by one running session, and a session limit over 55 minutes (`max_wall_clock_seconds`) can make one missed.

The user answers, approves, accepts, and resolves from any terminal, while a process drives the project or not (section 3, ADR 0014), and from the web and desktop apps. Every approval and acceptance the user is asked for shows the plain-language summary written by the agent that asks, the diff, and Farik's check results, as 5.4 says of a human gate (added in 0.18).
