While the team is paused because the provider refused the key (5.5), Today's list of what waits on the user starts with one row, "Your AI account's key did not work", which links to Settings to connect the account again (added in 0.30). A design review that cannot start (5.4) is listed there too (added in 0.33): with no preview set, "Tell Farik how to open your app", which opens Settings at "How to open your app"; and while the Designer cannot open the app, "<name> needs Docker's sandbox", with the words "The UI/UX Designer needs Docker's sandbox to open your app. Turn the sandbox on, or retire the Designer.", which opens the Team page; and while the Designer has its Playwright connector off, "<name>'s browser is off", with the words "<name> opens your app with Playwright. Turn Playwright on for <name> on the Team page; until then <name> takes no work, and nobody checks the screens <Developer> builds.", which opens that Designer's page on the Team page (`designer_needs_browser`, added in the step's fix wave). A connector's call waiting for the human is listed there too (added in 0.39): `waiting.list` gives it a row of kind `tool_approval` carrying `approval`, `server`, `tool` and the whole `input`, which reads "<agent name> wants to use <server>", then "To <tool, its underscores as spaces>, for <task> <title>. <agent name> waits until you decide.", and offers "Review", which opens `ToolApproval` (F9). A post outside the plan waiting for the owner is listed there too (added in 0.57): `waiting.list` gives it a row of kind `social_post` carrying `post`, `channel`, `text`, `media` and `at`, which reads "<agent name> wants to post on <Network>", after the marketing plans, and Today shows it with the post's words and pictures, "Post it" and "Don't post"; the agent's activity line is "Waiting on you: may <agent name> post on <Network>?". A marketing plan waiting for the owner is listed there too (added in 0.55): `waiting.list` gives it a row of kind `marketing_plan` carrying `plan`, `summary`, `total`, `currency`, `starts_on` and `ends_on`, which reads "<agent name> proposes a marketing plan: <title>", the plan's own title, after the connector calls and before the approvals, and offers "Review", which opens the plan's page (6.5). When a process driving the project ends, it says what waits on the user, in this order and by task: each unanswered question with the command that answers it; each connector call waiting for the human, "<task> waits: <agent name> wants to use <server>: farik tool approve <n>, or farik tool refuse <n>" and, on the next line, "  input: <the call's whole input>" (added in 0.39; the input line with `--json` is an `input` field); each marketing plan waiting for the owner, "<task> waits: <agent name> proposes a marketing plan: <title>: farik marketing plan approve <plan>, or farik marketing plan return <plan> --reason <text>", with `--json` a `plan` field (added in 0.55); each post outside the plan waiting for the owner, "<task> waits: <agent name> wants to post on <Network>: farik marketing post send <n>, or farik marketing post decline <n>", with `--json` a `post` field (added in 0.57); each contract awaiting approval; each other escalation with its last reason; each `verifying` epic or `high` risk task, which may need the user's acceptance; and, under the `manual` policy, each accepted task waiting to be integrated (added in 0.8). Then, when the team plans its work in sprints, no sprint is open and the Backlog holds work, it prints "start a sprint: <n> waits in the Backlog (`farik sprint start`)", `<n>` the Backlog's rows with no parent, so an epic counts once, and with `--json` `{"backlog": {"count": <n>}}` (section 3; added in 0.36). Work waiting in the Backlog is not something that waits on the user, so `waiting.list`, Today's "Waiting on you", has no row for it; Today counts it in the team band instead (F3). Nor is it blocked: a Backlog row keeps its status, so the blocked-age rule never counts the wait, escalation aging reads only escalated rows, the escalation digest is unchanged, and nothing escalates because no sprint was started (added in 0.36).

### 5.8 Memory

Three memories, all files in `.farik/`. Files under `.farik/` change only through Farik's tools, never through a task's commit: the Definition of Ready refuses a contract whose allowed paths reach there (5.3) and the Definition of Done a diff that changes a path there (5.4), for every role, the Finance Specialist's own `.farik/local/finance/` excepted (6.6; changed in 0.21) (added in 0.17).

- `decisions/` holds architecture decision records and product decisions, written by the Architect and PM, one file each, immutable once written (changed in 0.16, from "once accepted": nothing accepts a decision). A decision is written only through `farik_write_decision { title, text }`, a tool of the read tier that refuses anyone but the Architect and the Product Manager with `decision_refused: only the Architect and the Product Manager write decisions`. Its title is 1 to 120 characters once trimmed, on one line with no control characters, and its text 1 to 32,000 characters and not blank; anything else is refused with `decision_refused`. It is numbered one past the highest number among the decision files, in four digits, and a project that has 9999 is refused. Its file is `decisions/<NNNN>-<slug>.md`. The slug is the title in lower case, with each run of characters other than `a-z` and `0-9` turned into one `-`, trimmed of `-`, cut at 60 characters and trimmed again, or `decision` when nothing is left. The file holds `# <NNNN>. <title>`, a blank line, `Date: <UTC date>`, `By: <agent id>`, a blank line, and then the text. The file is written under a temporary name and then hard-linked to its own name, which fails when that name exists, so a crash never leaves a half-written decision and no decision is written over. When another writer took the number first, whatever title it gave, the tool tries the next number, three times at most, and then refuses. No tool edits a decision. Each decision is recorded as `decision.written`. Every agent reads the decisions through `farik_read_decisions { number? }`, a tool of the read tier offered wherever `farik_read_rules` is. With no number it answers every decision's number, title, date, and author, oldest first; with a number it answers that decision's whole file; an unknown number is refused with `no_such_decision: <n>`. It is a tool rather than the file tools because the decisions live in the project's own checkout, which a task's worktree is not (5.14). A file named `NNNN-<slug>.md` in that form and written by hand is listed too: its title comes from its `# ` heading, else its slug, and its date and author from its `Date:` and `By:` lines when it has them. Any other file there is ignored (added in 0.16).
- `agents/<name>/memory.md` is the agent's own notebook. It is included in every session for that agent. The agent may edit it. It is capped at a size (default 8k tokens) and the agent is told to prune. The agent writes it only through `farik_write_memory { text }`, which replaces the whole notebook; an empty text empties it. The tool is of the read tier and offered in every session but the two given one tool alone, triage and the judgment (added in 0.16). The cap is the team policy `memory_cap_tokens`, from 500 to 16,000, default 8,000. A text's tokens are a quarter of its characters, rounded up, which is also how the channel summary is measured (5.9). A text past the cap is refused with `memory_refused: <n> tokens is past your cap of <cap>; prune it`, and nothing is written or recorded. A write is recorded as `memory.written` with the whole text (added in 0.16). Every session's prompt says how full the notebook is, as `<n> of <cap> tokens.`, a line Farik writes outside the notebook's `untrusted` block. When `n` is past 80 percent of the cap and the session can call `farik_write_memory`, the line goes on: `Prune it with farik_write_memory before it reaches the cap.` The prompt cuts the notebook at four characters for each token of the cap, never splitting a character, so a notebook within its cap is never cut (8.2; added in 0.16).
- `team/retro.md` accumulates retrospective learnings. Appended after each sprint by whoever runs the retro ceremony: the Scrum Master, or the Product Manager on a team with no active one (changed in 0.17). Included in every planning session. It is written only by the retro ceremony (5.9), with `farik_append_retro`, once per sprint whichever session asks: a text that is not blank and holds at most 4,000 characters, appended under a heading `## <sprint id> (<date>)`, the ended sprint's id and the UTC date of the append, in a file Farik starts with a `# Retro` title when it is missing, and recorded as `retro.appended`. Anywhere else the tool is refused with `retro_refused`. The planning and retro ceremonies are given its last 16 KiB (added in 0.15).

The project scan from onboarding is stored as `project.md` and refreshed after an integration when what it reads has changed (changed in 0.16, from "whenever the tree changes materially"). The refresh runs once for each integration that records `task.integrated`: Farik's merge, the human's `farik integrate`, a pull request merged on the forge, and a branch the human merged by hand (5.14). A task that was already integrated is not scanned again. The scan reads the working tree at the project's root, so the refresh runs only when the project's own checkout is on the integration branch; on another branch, detached, or unreadable, it is skipped, and the reason is given. It compares the new scan with the last `project.scanned`: the read-back without its closing part about the last commit (`last commit ...` or `no commits yet`), and the names of the criteria found. A new commit alone is no change, and neither is a detected command that changes under the same name. A project with no `project.scanned` counts as changed. When the two differ, Farik rewrites `project.md`, reseeds the criterion library as `farik init` does (5.13), writing it and recording `criteria.updated` by `governor` when it changed, and records `project.scanned` last. Each event is about the integrated task, and no model is involved. A refresh that fails never fails the integration, though what it wrote before the failure stays written; since it recorded no `project.scanned`, the next integration does it again. The integration's own words then end with "; the project scan was refreshed", with nothing when it was unchanged, or with "; the project scan was not refreshed: <why>", in `farik run`'s line and in `farik integrate`'s answer; so do the words of an escalation that follows a merge, such as a failed push (added in 0.16).

There is no vector store in the first release. Grep over the repository and these files is enough for repositories of the size the target users have. This is a known limit to revisit.

### 5.9 The channel

Agents post to the channel in a conversational register. The persona line and a shared style guide control tone. What triggers a post:

- State transitions on tasks the agent owns or reviews (assigned, done, blocked, rejected with the one-line reason).
- Mentions from other agents or the user.
- Planning, standup, review, and retro ceremonies, which are structured conversations that run in the channel.
- A configurable "ambient" allowance: a small number of unprompted messages per sprint per agent, so the team feels alive without becoming expensive. Default is one, and reactions are one or two sentences (decided 2026-09-15: keep the register minimal).

A message (added in 0.14) is a `message.posted` event, and the log is the channel's only storage. It has an author, a kind, one line of text, the agents it mentions, a ceremony thread when it belongs to one, and the message it answers when it is a reply. The author is an agent, `human`, or `farik`. The text is not blank and holds at most 2,000 characters, and Farik turns each line break in it into a space. There are six kinds:

- `reaction`: an agent's first post in a session that is not a conversation, about the move that session made.
- `ambient`: each later post of such a session, from the agent's allowance.
- `reply`: the one post of a conversation session.
- `ceremony`: a post of a ceremony session, in its thread (changed in 0.15).
- `system`: Farik's line, written with no model.
- `human`: the human's post, from `farik say`.

Mentions are read by Farik from the text, never taken from the agent: each `@<id>` naming an agent of the team, active or not, once each, leaving out the author. `@human` is not a mention, because the human reads the channel. A system line mentions no one.

An agent posts with `farik_post_message`, tier `read`, which every session has except a one-tool session (triage, the judgment; changed in 0.15: sprint planning is now a ceremony, which has it) and a one-to-one chat's session (added in 0.34). Farik decides the kind from the session's purpose, and a refusal says `channel_limit`. The tool's description is the style guide: one or two sentences, what happened and what is next, no instruction to anyone.

The reaction comes from the session that made the move, on that session's model (changed in 0.14; this replaces "ambient and reaction messages use a cheaper model than task work"). The closing instruction of `refine`, `plan`, `implement`, and `verify` sessions asks for it: after asking for a move, post one or two sentences about it in the persona's voice, naming the task. No session is started only to react. A reaction's and an ambient message's envelope names the session's task.

Farik posts a system line with no model:

- for a move made by the governor or the human, which has no session;
- for the rejection Farik files in a reviewer's name once the review session has ended;
- when the daily budget or a sprint's budget is spent;
- when an agent sleeps at its model provider's limit (5.5).

A move's line reads `<task> <from> → <to> (by <who>)`, followed by `: <reason>` when the move carries one: the escalation's detail, the failed criteria and the reviewer's reasons for a rejection, the blocker, the resolution, the human's reason, or the criterion Farik could not run. It is cut to 2,000 characters ending in `…`, so a line never fails a move that is recorded. It is posted after the move's own events. The moves Farik makes on an agent's behalf post nothing, which keeps the register minimal: a triaged draft to `refining`, an assigned task to `in_progress`, an epic's assignment.

The ambient allowance is the team policy `ambient_messages_per_sprint`, from 0 to 20, default 1 (added in 0.14). It counts the agent's `ambient` messages since the open sprint's `sprint.started`, or since the UTC day began when no sprint is open. A post past it is refused.

A mention gets its agent one short conversation session (added in 0.14). A mention is pending while it is newer than the latest message the agent's last conversation session was shown, which that session's `session.started` records as `in_reply_to`, so that a message posted while the session starts stays pending. A conversation that ends any way but at its provider's limit uses up the mentions it was shown; one stopped at the limit leaves them pending until its agent wakes. Mentions in a `reply`, a system line, or a `ceremony` message are never pending, so replies are one deep and a ceremony's words start no conversation (changed in 0.15). In `farik run`, not `farik plan`, after the budget rule and before the third rule, and when a tick is scoped to no task, the orchestrator takes the first agent in team order with a pending mention that is active and not asleep, while the daily budget has room. It starts one `conversation` session about no task, on Claude Sonnet 5.5 at `low` effort whatever the agent's own model. Its first message lists the pending messages with their authors and sequence numbers, then the channel summary, each in an `untrusted` block. Its closing instruction asks it to answer the mentions once with `farik_post_message` and to file any work as a request with `farik_create_task` without a parent. Its one post is a `reply` to the latest pending message, and a second is refused. `farik run` prints it as `<agent>: <what>`. A mention of an agent that is paused, retired, or asleep waits.

The ceremonies (added in 0.15). Planning, standup, review, and retro are each one session of the ceremony runner: the active Scrum Master, else the active Product Manager, and with neither no ceremony runs. The session's purpose is `ceremony`, it is about no task, and it runs on Claude Sonnet 5.5 at `medium` effort whatever the runner's own model, with the read tier's built-in tools alone. Its `session.started` records its thread, `planning`, `standup`, `review`, or `retro`, and each of its posts is a `ceremony` message in that thread, at most three per session: a fourth is refused with `channel_limit`. Its Farik tools are the four reading tools, `farik_post_message`, `farik_write_memory`, and `farik_read_decisions` (the last two added in 0.16); planning adds `farik_plan_sprint`, and retro adds `farik_append_retro`. `farik_plan_sprint` is refused in any other session, whichever tools it lists, with `sprint_plan_refused: only the planning ceremony plans a sprint` (added in 0.17). Its first message gives it the ceremony's facts and then the channel summary, each in an `untrusted` block cut at 16 KiB, and its closing instruction says what to post, which tool ends it, and to mention no one, because the channel is read by all. No ceremony starts on a day whose budget is spent, and one whose runner sleeps at its provider's limit waits for it rather than passing to the other role.

A ceremony has run once a session in its thread started after its anchor and ended completed, aborted, or in error, or once three such sessions started, however they ended, so a ceremony stopped at a limit is tried again and none is tried forever; a retro has also run once it has appended, however its session ended, so no sprint gets two retros. The anchor is the sprint's `sprint.started` for planning, its `sprint.ended` for review and retro, and the start of the UTC day for a standup. A task-less `plan` session with no thread, from a log recorded before this revision, counts as the sprint's planning. The ceremonies run in a tick about no one task, before the rules of work and after the rule that ends a sprint, in the order review, retro, planning, standup:

- Planning runs once when a sprint starts, in `farik run`, `farik serve`, and `farik plan`. It is given the ready backlog, the sprint's budget left, the escalation digest (5.7), and the last 16 KiB of `team/retro.md`. It posts the plan and the digest, then plans the sprint with `farik_plan_sprint`. When the team plans its work in sprints (section 3; added in 0.36), its candidates are the Backlog's rows with no parent, ready tasks and epics that are ready, being broken down or broken down, the message naming them "each waiting in the Backlog" and giving each epic the number of tasks under it, its cancelled ones left out; `farik_plan_sprint` then accepts a Backlog row with no parent, a task under way since the switch not being one, and refuses another with "<task> is <status>, and only work waiting in the Backlog is planned". With the policy off, the candidates stay the `ready` rows with no parent and in no sprint, and the tool accepts only a ready task or an approved epic.
- Standup runs in `farik run` and `farik serve`, while a sprint is open, once per UTC day, when a task of the sprint moved in its window: from the start of the UTC day of the last standup that has run, or from the sprint's start, up to the start of the current UTC day. So a standup reports the days before today, and a move made after midnight but before that day's standup is reported the next day. It is given each move (task, from, to, and who), each blocked task of the sprint with its blocker, and the open escalations, and posts one summary.
- Review and retro run in `farik run` and `farik serve` for the latest ended sprint while no newer sprint has started; a sprint followed at once by another gets neither, and so does one whose file lists no task. Their tasks are those the sprint's file lists, and their events those about those tasks recorded between the sprint's start and its end. Review comes first: it is given each task's status, the first line of its last completion note, and what it cost in the sprint, then the sprint's budget and what it spent, and it posts what the sprint delivered and what it did not. Retro follows once review has run: it is given the sprint's rejections with their failed criteria, escalations with their reason, blocks with their blocker, each task's iterations, and the last 16 KiB of `team/retro.md`, and it posts the retro and records what the next planning should know with `farik_append_retro` (5.8).

`farik run` prints a ceremony as `S<n>: <what>`.

The channel summary is derived with no model (changed in 0.14): the most recent messages that fit 2,000 tokens, counted as a quarter of their characters rounded up, oldest first, one line each as `<author> [<thread>]: <text>`. It is written to `.farik/local/channel-summary.md` each time it is made, so the human can read what an agent was shown.

One-to-one chats (added in 0.34; ADR 0026) are not the channel. A chat message is a `chat_message.posted` event (8.5), never a `message.posted`, so no reader of the channel reads one: not `channel.messages`, the channel summary, `farik channel`, the channel's pending mentions, nor `farik metrics`' count of messages; an `@<id>` in a chat starts no conversation, and no session is shown a chat but its own agent's chat session. The user's message and the agent's reply are 1 to 4,000 characters, counted in code points, not blank, with their line breaks kept; a proposed request's title is 1 to 120 characters on one line and its text 20 to 4,000. A chat is pending when the sequence number of the user's newest message in it is above the `in_reply_to` of every `chat` session its agent has started, whoever wrote last, so a message sent while a session runs is answered next, with the reply in its history, and a session that ended without a reply is not tried again until the user writes again. In `farik run` and `farik serve`, not `farik plan`, as the first rule of a tick scoped to no task, before the sprint rules, the orchestrator takes the oldest pending chat, by that message's sequence number, of an agent that is not retired (a paused agent among them), is not asleep, and whose day's budget has room, and starts one `chat` session for it (8.2), whose `in_reply_to` is that message; `farik run` prints it as `<agent>: <what>`, as a conversation's. While the team is paused the tick runs this rule alone, and none while the pause is Farik's for a key the provider refused (5.5). A command that sends a chat message wakes a waiting `farik serve`. The privacy of chats rests on `.farik/local/**` staying protected (5.6): the log, `.farik/local/farik.db`, lives there, and a chat session's `Read`, `Grep` and `Glob` run in the project's root, so a user who takes that glob out of the protected list lets every session read every chat.

The hard rule again: nothing said in the channel creates work. An agent that wants something done files a request, which is triaged and contracted like a request from the user (5.16). The tool for filing a request is available in the channel session precisely so that the conversational path leads into the governed path rather than around it.

### 5.10 What the harness does not solve

Two honest gaps. First, verification quality is only as good as the exit criteria the PM writes. A vague criterion passed by a sloppy reviewer is still a pass. The Scrum Master's judgment check and the human review of `high` risk contracts are the mitigations; they are not proofs. Second, the governor can prevent forbidden actions but cannot detect a semantically wrong change that stays within its allowed paths and passes its tests. Farik makes that kind of error cheap to find (small tasks, diffs per task, a reviewer who reads them) rather than impossible.

### 5.11 Contract ownership (added in 0.2)

Every contract has an owner. A contract the Product Manager wrote is owned by the team until the human locks it. A contract with `locked: true` is owned by the human: agents may record criterion results and write their notes on it, and the governor refuses every other change by an agent, with the reason `contract_locked` when the change is to the contract's content and the reason for the field's owner when the field is one that agent never writes anyway. A lock holds the contract's content for the human; it does not stop the lifecycle, so the governor still writes `status`, `assignee`, `reviewer`, `iteration`, and `sprint` when it applies a transition, or a locked task could never leave `refining`. Neither locking nor unlocking is a content change: a human who locks a contract from `ready` onward does not send its task back to `refining`, or writing an epic by hand and locking it would undo the approval it was just given (5.16). Locking and unlocking are human commands, recorded as `contract.locked` and `contract.unlocked` events. This is how a user writes a contract themselves, or with the Product Manager's help (5.13), and has the team execute exactly that contract.

The contract's `summary` (added in 0.25) is a content field, the Product Manager's to write like the others, and so frozen with them once the task leaves `refining`. Every field of a contract has exactly one owner, and the governor refuses a write to a field the writer does not own (added in 0.3). The governor's own are `status`, `assignee`, `reviewer`, `iteration`, and `sprint`, at every status and whoever asks: 5.2 gives it the `status` field for its transitions, and every other row's trigger is an actor asking rather than writing, so a write of `status` by anyone else would move a task past every gate with no `task.transitioned` event behind it. The governor writes `sprint` when a task is planned into the open sprint, by the assigner's plan or when it is filed under an epic in that sprint, and clears it when the task leaves a sprint the human ended before the task was accepted or cancelled; an accepted or cancelled task keeps the sprint it finished in (added in 0.12). `locked` is the human's alone. The store's are `id`, `created_by`, `created_at`, and `updated_at`, which no actor writes through a contract tool. `kind` and `parent` are fixed when the contract is created: the triage decides the kind (5.16) and its own tool changes it, and clearing `parent` would take a task out of the epic its readiness was judged against. The notes are everyone's, at every status. Everything else is the contract's content, `change` among it (added in 0.11), written by the Product Manager and by the human on any contract, and by the Scrum Master on a task but never on an epic (6.2, 5.16 item 3); an assignee, a reviewer, and the governor never write content at all. A field name the governor does not recognise is refused as unknown rather than treated as content, so a field added to the schema is refused until somebody says who owns it. A contract whose task is `accepted` or `cancelled` takes no write but a note, the human's included: nothing leaves those two statuses, and a human's write of a frozen contract is defined by sending the task back to `refining`, which a terminal task has no way to do.

Independently of ownership, a contract is frozen once its task leaves `refining`: from `ready` onward, only `status`, `assignee`, `reviewer`, `iteration`, `sprint`, and `notes` change, the sprint because a task is put into one at planning and planning works from the ready backlog, and only through governed transitions and the note tools. An agent that wants a frozen contract changed asks the human (5.7). A human who edits a frozen contract moves the task back to `refining`, and the log says so. From the web UI that edit is saved on an escalated task, written while the task is still held, and only then does the escalation's resolve take it back, so that the session the move wakes reads the human's plan (changed in 0.26 by step 08's landing review); a save of a frozen contract the team is still working to is refused, and the user escalates the task first (added in 0.25's landing review). The human's save of a contract from the web UI never changes `locked`: a save whose `locked` differs from the file's is refused, because a lock is recorded as `contract.locked` or `contract.unlocked`, which a save does not write (added in 0.25's landing review).

### 5.12 Team rules (added in 0.2)

Team rules are constraints the human writes once, in `.farik/team.yaml` under `rules`, that the governor applies to every contract and every tool call. Agents read them in every session and cannot change them.

| Rule | Type | Enforced where |
|---|---|---|
| `protected_paths` | globs | every file tool call, read or write, is refused inside them (5.6) |
| `allowed_paths_ceiling` | globs | Definition of Ready refuses a contract whose `allowed_paths` reach outside them. A path lies within a directory glob such as `docs/**` only when its literal part, up to its first wildcard, reaches past the directory's `/` (`docs/adr/**`, `docs/*.md`), or it has no wildcard and is the directory or below it; `docs*/**` and `docs?/x` are outside, since the wildcard runs on into a sibling such as `docsrc/` (changed in 0.11); a path with a `..` segment anywhere, before or after a wildcard, is within no ceiling (changed in 0.17) |
| `required_criteria` | verification methods | Definition of Ready refuses a contract that has no criterion of each listed method |
| `require_new_tests` | boolean | Definition of Ready refuses a contract whose `test` criteria do not set `new_tests_required` |
| `max_task_budget_usd` | number | Definition of Ready refuses a task whose budget exceeds it, when it is set; an epic is bounded by the sprint budget instead |
| `document_paths` | globs | Definition of Ready refuses a task assigned to any role but the Software Developer and the UI/UX Designer whose `allowed_paths` reach outside them (5.3; added in 0.11; the Designer added in 0.31) |
| `ui_paths` | globs | a Software Developer's task whose branch changes a path inside them is a change to the interface, which the UI/UX Designer checks before the Architect reviews it (5.4; added in 0.33) |
| `forbidden_commands` | regular expressions | `farik_exec` refuses a command that matches one: ECMAScript patterns, which have no inline flags such as `(?i)`, matched against the whole command and against each non-blank segment (split on `&&`, `||`, `;`, `\|`, and newlines), and a pattern that does not compile refuses every command |

Defaults: `protected_paths` as in 5.6, `max_task_budget_usd` unset, no cap (ADR 0015), `document_paths` `docs/**`, `**/*.md`, and `CHANGELOG.md` when the key is left out, `ui_paths` `**/*.tsx`, `**/*.jsx`, `**/*.vue`, `**/*.svelte`, `**/*.css`, `**/*.scss`, and `**/*.html` when the key is left out, where an explicit `[]` is kept and leaves only the contract's `ui_change` (added in 0.33), everything else empty or off. The web app edits `ui_paths` under Advanced, as "Which files are screens" (added in 0.33). An explicit `document_paths: []` is kept, not replaced by the default, and means that no task but a Software Developer's or a UI/UX Designer's can be ready (added in 0.11; the Designer added in 0.31). Rules never loosen a permission tier; they only narrow what a granted tier allows.

Beside the rules, `team.yaml`'s `policy` holds the team's policies, flat keys such as `wip_limit_per_agent`, `escalation_age_hours` and `ambient_messages_per_sprint`. `plan_in_sprints` is one of them (added in 0.36): a boolean, read as `false` when the key is left out, which setup and `farik init` write as `true`, and which section 3 says what it holds. It is a policy, not a rule: the user switches it in Settings (4.4), and it narrows when work is assigned, not what a tool call may do.

### 5.13 Contract authoring and the criterion library (added in 0.2)

A contract can be written three ways, and all three end in the same Definition of Ready check. The human writes it alone, in the editor or as a YAML file, and then the human's own triage sets whether it is an epic or a single task (5.16). The Product Manager writes it from a brief, an issue link, or the backlog, as in 6.1. Or the two write it together: the Product Manager drafts, the human edits, the Definition of Ready results update as they type, and the human locks the result (5.11). The command line offers the same through `farik contract new`, which files the request, runs its triage (or takes the user's), then runs a Product Manager drafting session over a brief or a link, answering its questions at the terminal, and prints the contract with its readiness results.

`farik contract new --brief <text>` or `--from <issue>` (added in 0.8). The request's title is the brief's first line, cut at 120 characters, or the issue's title; its intent is the whole brief, or the issue's title, body, and address, and a brief under twenty characters, the schema's least intent, is refused before anything is filed. Every other field is a placeholder the Product Manager replaces while refining it: one item in scope and one out, a requirement, a `review` criterion, both roles Software Developer, risk `low`, the team's cap on a task's budget, and an allowed path no file matches. When no other process drives the project, this one starts driving it before anything is filed (8.2) and holds the run lock throughout, so that a start that refuses, for want of a credential or of a recent enough `claude`, files nothing. The request is then filed as the human's, sized when `--size` is given, and worked, in this process, with the triage and refining rules alone and for that request alone (8.2): each event about it is printed as `farik task show` prints it, and each question the Product Manager asks is printed with an `answer>` prompt, whose reply is recorded as the answer. The end of the input or Ctrl-C at the prompt leaves the question open for `farik answer`. Once nothing is left to do it prints the contract, its readiness (passed; failed with each failure; passed the structural checks, when it waits for the user's approval; or not judged yet, and why), what waits on the user, and, with `--lock`, takes the contract, last, so that the Product Manager writes first; when the Product Manager has written none, it says so and takes nothing. While another process drives the project, the request is filed and sized and left to that process, and `--lock` is refused before anything is filed.

The criterion library, `.farik/team/criteria.yaml`, holds named, reusable exit criteria: the project's own check and test commands found by the project scan, and any the human adds. When authoring, a criterion is referenced by name and expanded into the contract, so that contracts across a project verify the same way and the Product Manager is not asked to reinvent "the tests pass" every time.

### 5.14 Integration of accepted work (added in 0.2)

Each task works on its own branch in its own git worktree under `.farik/local/worktrees/FRK-<n>`, so that tasks running in parallel never share a working tree. The container for a task mounts that worktree. The branch says what the task changes (changed in 0.11): a Software Developer's or a UI/UX Designer's task (the Designer added in 0.31) works on `feature/FRK-<n>`, or on `fix/FRK-<n>` when its contract's `change` is `fix` (an absent `change` reads as `feature`), and every other role's task on `docs/FRK-<n>`. The name is derived from the contract whenever it is needed, not recorded, since `assignee_role` and `change` are frozen once the task leaves `refining` (5.11). A branch `farik/FRK-<n>` made before 0.11 is neither renamed nor read. A repository with a branch named exactly `feature`, `fix`, or `docs` cannot hold a branch under that name, so git refuses the task's worktree at assignment and the refusal reaches the user as any git failure does; renaming that branch fixes it.

What happens to the branch after `accepted` is the team's `integration` policy: `auto_merge` (the default a new project gets, named `local_merge` until 0.8: Farik merges the task branch into the integration branch with a merge commit and pushes the integration branch to `origin` when the repository has one), `manual` (Farik does nothing until the human runs `farik integrate`, which merges locally and does not push), or `pull_request` (Farik pushes the branch and opens a pull request with the `gh` program, titled with the task's id and title, whose body is the contract's intent, the completion note, and the review note; every such pull request needs the human's approval and merge on the forge, and the task is integrated when it merges, the local integration branch then brought up to the forge's). Under `pull_request` Farik reads each open pull request's state once a tick; one closed without merging is an integration escalation, and the human's `farik integrate` then records it as integrated if the branch is in the integration branch all the same (merged by hand, or through another pull request) and otherwise says to reopen it. `gh` picks the base repository itself, which in a fork with an `upstream` remote can be `upstream`; `gh repo set-default` settles it. Farik's pushes are its own act on the host, with the user's own git credentials, under the policy the user chose; no agent is acting, so no agent's `git_remote` is asked (ADR 0012; added in 0.8). A task is awaiting integration on the board from its move into `accepted` until `task.integrated` records where its branch landed; an epic, whose children carry the branches, never is. The integration branch is the repository's default branch unless the team configures another; the repository's default branch is what `origin/HEAD` points at, and a repository with no remote records one nowhere, so there it is the branch that is checked out (added in 0.5).

A task in a private folder has no branch (6.6; added in 0.21; as built in 0.62). For it, `accepted` is the end: its move into `accepted` carries the effect `nothing_to_integrate`, so the board never shows it awaiting integration, no `task.integrated` is recorded, the integration policy does not apply, and the human's `farik integrate` of it is refused `nothing_to_integrate`; a task that depends on it counts it integrated, so its dependants may be assigned (5.2). It is not assigned while another task holds the folder (5.2, 6.6).

One task integrates at a time (added in 0.5). A merge moves the repository's own checkout — the integration branch is checked out, merged into, and what was there is put back — so two integrations at once on one repository can interleave and land a merge commit on the wrong branch. Tasks work in their own worktrees and are unaffected; it is integration alone that is serialised. Every merge, push, and pull request opening holds an exclusive lock on `.farik/local/integration.lock`, a file lock because `farik integrate` runs in a process of its own beside `farik run`, and reads the log again under it, so that two callers racing for one task record one integration (added in 0.8).

When integration cannot land (added in 0.8). A conflict, git refusing the merge, or a push that fails raises an escalation with reason `integration` whose detail is git's words; nothing leaves `accepted` (5.2), so the task stays `accepted` and awaiting integration, and the escalation is a message rather than a move. A failed push leaves the merge in place, because dependents start from the local integration branch, and its detail says to run `git push origin <branch>` once it can be pushed. Farik does not try again after an integration escalation: the human's `farik integrate` is the way on, and it integrates whatever the escalations, each failed attempt raising its own. A branch the human already merged by hand is "already up to date" to that merge, which records it. No git Farik runs may prompt: each runs with `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` and an empty `GIT_ASKPASS`, so a push or fetch that wants a credential no helper gives fails with git's words. A push or fetch also runs ssh in batch mode (`GIT_SSH_COMMAND="ssh -o BatchMode=yes"`), so that ssh fails rather than asking on the terminal for a host key or a passphrase while the integration lock is held, unless the user chose how git runs ssh (`GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, `GIT_SSH`, or `core.sshCommand`), which Farik leaves as it is. The team's `integration_branch` is checked by git as a branch name before it is used, so a name such as `main:other` or `-f` is refused rather than read as a refspec or an option, and so is a name git reads as another branch (`@`, `@{-1}`).

Who commits (changed in 0.36). A task's commits, made through `farik_git_commit`, and integration's merge commits are the user's: Farik commits as the identity git has for the repository. When git has none there, `user.name` or `user.email` empty or unset at the repository's effective scope, as on a fresh computer or a CI runner, Farik commits as `farik <farik@localhost>`, the identity the project setup's first commit is made with, rather than leave git to refuse. A commit that still fails is answered to the agent with git's words, and Farik also posts a line in the channel about the task saying it could not be committed and why, since a task whose work is not committed never reaches `verifying`.

A task is assigned only when every dependency it lists is accepted and integrated, a Finance Specialist's task counting as integrated once accepted, since it has no branch (6.6; added in 0.21), and its branch starts from the integration branch at that moment. Worktrees and containers are removed when a task is accepted or cancelled; branches are kept. A container that cannot be removed, docker being down, leaves the task's worktrees in place to be removed on a later tick, and holds up no other work.

### 5.15 Recovery (added in 0.2)

Farik can stop at any moment: the laptop closes, the process is killed. On startup it reconciles the event log with itself and with the files (8.4): every session that has a `session.started` event without a `session.ended` one is ended with reason `aborted` and the detail "interrupted: farik stopped before the session ended" (the wire has no `interrupted` reason, and nothing would read one differently; changed in 0.8), and one with no `cost.recorded` is recorded as costing nothing on the model its start names, so that it counts against its task's sessions as any session does; every task that was `in_progress` stays `in_progress` and its next session starts from the last commit on its branch and the last note, in a sandbox made afresh, a container left by the killed run being replaced; a `verifying` task gets its reviewer's session again without its recorded criteria being run again; worktrees and containers that belong to accepted or cancelled tasks are removed, the containers by name since no handle to them survived, and the task's own worktree last, so that a recovery stopped part way does the rest next time. Nothing is lost that was committed or logged, and nothing is repeated that was recorded as done.

### 5.16 Epics: from a request to sub-contracts (added in 0.3)

Every prompt from the user that asks for work is translated into a contract before anything else happens, and so is every request an agent files from the channel, and every request the user sends from a one-to-one chat (changed in 0.34: the agent proposes it there, and the user files it, 4.3). The first thing that happens to a request is triage: the Scrum Master, or the Product Manager when the team has no active Scrum Master, decides whether the request is large or small and records the decision with a reason (`request.triaged`). A large request becomes an epic (`kind: epic`). A small request becomes a single standalone task (`kind: task`, no `parent`), written by the Product Manager like any task, with its questions to the user asked first and the user's approval required only by the team's policy (`human_accepts_contracts`, or `high` risk); it then goes through the ordinary lifecycle without a breakdown. The governor refuses `draft → refining` until the triage is recorded, and the user may overrule it (`farik triage <id> large|small`, or the board) before the Product Manager starts. Triage is a session of the triager's, the Scrum Master's when the team has an active one (changed in 0.10), and runs on the cheaper model, `claude-sonnet-5-5` at low effort whatever the triager's own model (changed in 0.33 from `claude-sonnet-5`), with `farik_triage_request` as its one tool and no built-in tool, and produces one event, so a small request costs one short session on top of its own. Once a request is triaged, Farik moves it to `refining` on the Product Manager's behalf with no session; the user's window to overrule the triage is the draft (added in 0.8).

An epic goes through the same lifecycle as a task with four differences.

1. The Product Manager writes it, and must ask the user every question it needs first. In an epic's `refining` sessions the Product Manager asks through the question mechanism (5.7) before it writes the contract, and it may not write or change product documents (the spec, requirements, or the product roadmap under `.farik/product/`) until the epic is approved: the `farik_write_product_doc` tool is refused until the user has approved the contract the epic has now, and for a `cancelled` epic, whose documents would describe a decision the team abandoned (added in 0.3). The governor asks the approval rather than the status, because an epic waiting for approval, one that failed the Definition of Ready three times, and one whose risk needs the human all sit in `escalated`; a return to `refining` ends the approval and item 2 asks for it again, and an epic escalated after its approval, for a budget or a permission, keeps the documents it was approved for. When the Product Manager believes it has no questions, it says so in the contract's intent, and the user's approval is the check that it was right.
2. The user reads and approves it, from the Product Manager's plain-language summary for the human, the contract in full, and its Definition of Ready results (5.4; added in 0.18). Every epic requires human acceptance of the contract before it leaves `refining`, whatever its risk: once the contract passes the structural checks, the governor moves the epic to `escalated` with reason `approval` and the board shows it as awaiting approval; the user approves, which moves it to `ready`, or sends it back to `refining` with a message that starts the next refining session. Nothing is broken down before this approval. A contract written in `refining`, or one filed whole (a breakdown's task, or a contract the user holds), is judged by the governor before the Product Manager refines it again; one that fails goes back to the Product Manager with its failures (added in 0.8).
3. Its assignee breaks it down. An approved epic is assigned to the Scrum Master, or to the Product Manager when the team has no active Scrum Master, and that assignee's work is to write the epic's tasks (`kind: task`, `parent` set), each with clear deliverables and exit criteria, and to assign them to the appropriate agents (5.2). A task's `allowed_paths` fall within its epic's, its budget within the epic's remaining budget, and its parent must be `in_progress`; the Definition of Ready checks all three. A task under an epic may be created only by its epic's assignee or by the human; a standalone task comes only from triage. Farik assigns an approved epic on its assignee's behalf, with no session, once that assignee holds fewer open tasks than the WIP limit, and starts it (added in 0.8): to the Scrum Master when the team has an active one, with the Product Manager as its reviewer (added in 0.10), and otherwise to the Product Manager, whose epic the human reviews. Who reviews an epic is fixed by that assignment and read from the epic's row thereafter, the human when the row names no reviewer, so that a Scrum Master activated or paused later changes no assigned epic's reviewer. The assignee's `plan` session then breaks it down, and a `plan` session of the epic's assignee assigns each of its tasks once ready (added in 0.8).
4. It is done when its tasks are. An epic moves to `verifying` when every task under it is `accepted` or `cancelled` and at least one is accepted; its own exit criteria are then run by its reviewer, the Product Manager when the Scrum Master broke it down and the human when the Product Manager did, since a reviewer is never the assignee (5.1); and its acceptance requires the user, like its approval did. The epic's assignee closes it out in a `plan` session once its tasks are done, writing its completion note and asking for `verifying`, or filing more tasks when the user's message asks for them; when the user reviews it, Farik runs its mechanical criteria for them first (5.4; added in 0.8), and so it does for the Product Manager, who reviews the epic from its contract, Farik's results, and each task's completion note (5.4; added in 0.10). A rejected epic goes back to `in_progress` by the governor's row, as a task does, and its assignee's close-out `plan` session, once every task under it is done, is given the last rejection, the failed criteria's ids and the review note, and asked to file the tasks that fix it rather than close it out again; once a task has been created under the epic since that rejection, the close-out is the plain one. A new rejection raises the iteration, which the iteration limit bounds (added in 0.10).

A user who wants to write the epic themselves does so in the editor or as a file, locks it (5.11), and approves it; the breakdown still happens by the assignee. When the triage sized a request as small and the Product Manager finds while refining that it is not, it re-triages the request itself as large (`farik_triage_request` accepts this one change from the Product Manager of a `refining` standalone task); the standalone task becomes the epic and its refining starts over. The user may overrule any triage until refining starts.

The product roadmap, `.farik/product/roadmap.md`, and the product documents under `.farik/product/` are written by the Product Manager only through `farik_write_product_doc`, only for approved epics, and every write is a `product_doc.written` event, so that the user can see what changed in the roadmap and why.

## 6. Launch roles

Each role ships as a directory under `roles/<role>/` with `role.yaml` (mandate, what the role produces, what it may not do, its default model and effort, and the skills it ships with), `system.md` (the prompt), and a `skills/` folder holding one `<skill-name>/SKILL.md` per skill in the Agent Skills format. A role's permission tiers and session limits are not in `role.yaml`: they are the governor's defaults (5.6, 5.5), which the team file overrides per agent (`grants`, `revokes`, `budgets.session`), and an agent's own `model` in the team file overrides the role's default model and effort. A copy in the role file that the governor did not read would be one a user edited expecting an effect (added in 0.8). The user can edit all of it. A role's skills are written into the prompt of every session of the role (ADR 0011). The user can also give one agent, or the whole team, a skill of their own (added in 0.40; 6.7, ADR 0034): the team's lives in `.farik/skills/<name>/` and one agent's in `.farik/agents/<agent_id>/skills/<name>/`, and a name resolves agent, then team, then role, a team or agent skill replacing a shipped skill of its name only when the person said so.

### 6.1 Product Manager

Mandate: own the backlog, translate every request from the user into a contract, an epic or a standalone task as the triage sized it, after asking the user its questions (5.16), accept work against contracts, keep the product pointed at a user need. The PM is the source of exit criteria and the last non-human gate. When the team has no Scrum Master, the PM also triages requests, breaks approved epics into tasks, and assigns them.

Produces: epic contracts and standalone task contracts, the questions it asks the user, product decisions, the product roadmap and requirements under `.farik/product/` (only for approved epics), release scope.

Cannot: write application code, which only the Software Developer and the UI/UX Designer do (changed in 0.31), and the DevOps Engineer in an incident's fix (6.9; changed in 0.32); work a task outside the team's `document_paths` (5.3; added in 0.11); write product documents for an epic the user has not approved; run the test suite as a reviewer of its own contracts; accept a task without a reviewer's verification event.

Default tools: read, network (for competitor and docs research), the task and decision tools. The decision tools are `farik_write_decision`, which the Product Manager and the Architect alone may call, and `farik_read_decisions`, which every agent may (5.8; added in 0.16). Default model: the strongest available, since contract quality is leverage on everything downstream.

Kit (added in 0.46; 6.7): five skills, `asking-the-right-questions`, `writing-requirements`, `prioritising-the-backlog`, `scoping-a-release` and `using-product-sources`, and four services: Amplitude, Linear and Notion, which it only reads, and GitHub, which it reads for issues and an organisation's project boards and writes to, an issue or a comment, only after the human allows that call (changed in 0.65; ADR 0044).

### 6.2 Scrum Master

Mandate: keep work flowing and keep the human informed. Triages every request from the user as large or small (5.16), in a session of its own on the cheaper model whatever its own, given the triage tool alone (added in 0.10). Breaks approved epics into tasks with clear deliverables and exit criteria and assigns them to the appropriate agents. Runs planning, standup, review, and retro, one session each, and the Product Manager runs them when the team has no active Scrum Master (5.9; changed in 0.15). Plans each sprint the human starts, once, from the ready backlog, the Backlog when the team plans its work in sprints (section 3; changed in 0.36), and within the sprint's budget, in the planning ceremony, where it also posts the escalation digest (section 3, 5.5, 5.7; added in 0.12, changed in 0.15). Appends what the retro learned to `team/retro.md` (5.8; added in 0.15). Enforces WIP limits (default: one unfinished task per agent, counted as 5.2 says: every task the agent holds that is not `accepted` or `cancelled`). Judges each contract against the Definition of Ready's judgment criteria once every other rule passes, in a session given `farik_record_judgment` alone (5.3; changed in 0.10). Is assigned the approved epics, which the Product Manager then reviews (5.16; added in 0.10). Owns escalation hygiene.

Produces: triage decisions, task contracts under epics, sprint plans, standup summaries, retro notes, escalation digests.

Cannot: change an epic's requirements or contract; change a frozen contract; write application code, which only the Software Developer and the UI/UX Designer do (changed in 0.31), and the DevOps Engineer in an incident's fix (6.9; changed in 0.32); work a task outside the team's `document_paths` (5.3); accept work (changed in 0.11).

Default tools: read, the board tools, the triage tool, the judgment tool (added in 0.10), the sprint planning tool (added in 0.12), the retro tool (added in 0.15), and the contract tools for the tasks of an epic it is breaking down. Runs on a mid-tier model; its work is coordination, not deep reasoning.

Kit (added in 0.46; 6.7): three skills, `planning-a-sprint`, `running-ceremonies` and `writing-escalation-digests`, and no service. The chat bridge is planned in the last phase.

### 6.3 Architect

Mandate: hold the shape of the system. Writes architecture decision records, sets constraints that go into contracts (allowed paths, patterns to follow), and reviews the developer's diffs for design, writing the review note. An Architect's task is a document: it runs no spikes, and it does not write application code, which only the Developer and the UI/UX Designer do (changed in 0.10, and in 0.31 for the Designer, from the founder's decision of 2026-09-24). Its tasks are held to the team's `document_paths` and work on `docs/FRK-<n>` (5.3, 5.14; added in 0.11).

Produces: ADRs, design notes attached to contracts, review notes.

Cannot: write application code, merge or push a shared branch, accept work (changed in 0.10).

Default tools: read, write_workspace, execute, network, git_local, so that it commits the documents that are its work (changed in 0.10). It records its decisions under `.farik/decisions/` with `farik_write_decision`, which the Product Manager and the Architect alone may call, and reads them with `farik_read_decisions` (5.8; added in 0.16).

Kit (added in 0.47; 6.7): five skills, `designing-apis-and-data`, `reviewing-dependencies`, `security-review`, `setting-performance-budgets` and `using-architecture-sources`, and four services it only reads: Context7 (library documentation), Grep (public code search), OSV (known flaws in packages) and GitHub (the code and pull requests of the user's own repositories, private ones included, reached with a key the user pastes until the web launch and by a sign-in through Farik Cloud with Farik's GitHub App from it; changed in 0.65, ADR 0044).

### 6.4 Software Developer

Mandate: implement contracts. With the UI/UX Designer, the only role that writes application code, beside the DevOps Engineer's incident fixes (6.9) (added in 0.11; changed in 0.31 and 0.32). Works on a task branch, `feature/FRK-<n>` or `fix/FRK-<n>` by the contract's `change` (5.14; changed in 0.11), runs the exit criteria before declaring done, writes a completion note.

Produces: diffs, commits on task branches, completion notes.

Cannot: modify contracts, accept work, push to shared branches without the `git_remote` grant, touch files outside allowed paths.

Default tools: read, write_workspace, execute, git_local. The developer runs the project's own tooling; Farik does not reimplement a coding agent. See section 8.

Kit (added in 0.48; 6.7): six skills, `test-driven-development`, `debugging`, `safe-migrations`, `testing-per-stack`, `answering-a-review` and `using-docs-and-the-browser`, and one service it only reads, Context7 (library documentation), the Architect's entry with its own `why`, signed in to by route 1. Its browser is the built-in Playwright of the UI/UX Designer's kit (6.7), off unless the user turns it on for the Developer on its page; it opens the task's preview and nothing else, in the Developer's `explore` and `implement` sessions, and a Developer's `verify` session is not given it. The Developer has no `network` tier; Context7 is its one outbound call, its `query` and `libraryName` are words the agent writes, sent to Context7 (Upstash), each call recorded as `tool.called`, and they are not tagged `external_effect`, so no documentation lookup waits for approval. A development database and deploy status are not in this kit: the database a task uses runs in the sandbox or the preview, which a host server cannot reach, and deploy status is the DevOps Engineer's (6.9).

### 6.5 Marketing Specialist

Mandate: own the business's marketing (changed in 0.52 on the founder's decision of 2026-10-05, ADR 0042; `docs/design/marketing-specialist.md`; built in phase 7 steps 08b to 08f). It owns four things: the brand kit, the brand persona, the marketing plan, and the social presence on the business's channels. It researches the market, the audience, competitors, the words customers search for and what each channel costs, to decide the best plan; writes the plan with its budget for the owner to approve; posts on the business's social channels and runs its search ads as the approved plan says; and still writes release notes, landing and README copy and positioning, and synthesizes user feedback the user pastes in (the research and the plan added in 0.10).

Produces: files under the team's `document_paths` (default `docs/**`, `**/*.md`, and `CHANGELOG.md`; changed in 0.11), among them the brand kit (`docs/marketing/brand/brand-kit.md` and its files under `docs/marketing/brand/assets/`), the brand persona (`docs/marketing/brand/persona.md`), each marketing plan (`docs/marketing/plans/MP-<n>.md`) and its research (`docs/marketing/research/`); positioning decisions; the posts Farik sends for it; the Google Ads campaigns it runs for an approved plan. While the team has an active Marketing Specialist, another role's task may not name `docs/marketing/**` in its allowed paths (5.3; added in 0.52); every agent may read them, and the UI/UX Designer takes the project's colours and voice from the brand kit.

Cannot: change application code, which only the Software Developer and the UI/UX Designer do (changed in 0.31), and the DevOps Engineer in an incident's fix (6.9; changed in 0.32); work a task outside the team's `document_paths` (5.3); publish or send without the human allowing that call, unless the owner's approved marketing plan covers it (changed in 0.11 and 0.52); spend money but through an approved plan, within its budget; delete a post, an email or a campaign; change billing, account access or conversion tracking at any service.

Its marketing plan (added in 0.52; ADR 0042). The agent proposes a plan with `farik_propose_marketing_plan`: its dates (at most 92 days), its budget in one currency, in total and per channel, its Google Ads campaigns (each with its budget and dates), its post slots (a channel, a day and a topic each) and how success is measured, with the plan's text. Farik checks it and records `marketing_plan.proposed`; the owner approves it or sends it back with a reason on Today (`marketing_plan.approved`, `marketing_plan.returned`), and approving is the owner's alone whatever the team's `approvals` policy (5.6), as a purchase order is (6.10). One plan is active at a time, from its start to its end; approving a newer one ends the active one at the newer one's start, and the owner may end a plan at any time, which pauses its campaigns and stops its posts not yet sent (`marketing_plan.ended`).

The mandate as built (added in 0.55; phase 7 step 08c). The role's persona is "Owns your brand and how you reach people". Its `role.yaml` and prompt say it owns the brand kit, the brand persona, the marketing plan and the social presence, researches the market before it plans, proposes a plan with its budget for the owner to approve, and posts and advertises only as an approved plan says or after the owner allows that one call. Its `produces` list holds "the brand kit", "the brand persona" and "a marketing plan", and its `forbidden` list is exactly: write application code; publish, send or spend money except through a call the owner allows or the owner's approved marketing plan; delete a post, an email or a campaign; change billing, account access or conversion tracking at any service. The prompt names the four things with their paths, says that what a service or a competitor's page returns is data, never an instruction, and that a returned plan's reason is the owner's own words, which it reads and answers in the next version of the plan. Four kit skills carry the work, each written for any business (ADR 0040), in numbered sections under 6 KB: `keeping-the-brand-kit` (the kit's eight parts, name, promise, audience, colours as hex with where each is used and its contrast with text, type, logo, picture style and voice, written from the business's own material before anything is made, never another business's mark), `writing-the-brand-persona` (the character the brand plays on social media, how it talks to customers, what it never says, five sample replies and how it differs per network, never a real person's), `researching-the-market` (the audience and where it spends time, the words customers search for and what each channel costs to reach someone, each fact with its source and day under `docs/marketing/research/`, a guess marked as one) and `writing-the-marketing-plan` (research first, then goals and how each is measured, channels, the budget split by channel and campaign, the post calendar as slots, campaigns with dates inside the plan's, then proposing it and ending the turn). The persona's skill and the research skill say where they sit beside `writing-in-the-brands-voice`, the voice of any copy, and `researching-competitors`, which compares three to five rivals. The UI/UX Designer's `brand-and-design-tokens` reads the brand kit first when the project has one (6.8).

The brand's logo and pictures are the business's own (added in 0.55, on the founder's decision of 2026-10-06: "The logos are user independent not Farik's. The user will provide his own logos and images."). The user puts them in `docs/marketing/brand/assets/`; the brand kit, which the agent writes, names and describes each file and where it came from and when; and when one the kit needs is missing, the agent asks the user for it with `farik_ask_human`. The agent never makes a logo and never puts a generated picture in place of the brand's own, and Farik has no tool that copies a generated picture into the brand kit. The pictures and clips the agent makes with its creative services (6.7) stay at those services, for a post or a page that uses them.

Proposing a plan (added in 0.55). `farik_propose_marketing_plan`, of the `write_workspace` tier, is offered only to a Marketing Specialist's `implement` session about a task, and refused to anyone else with `marketing_plan_refused`. Its input is `title`, `summary`, `text`, `starts_on`, `ends_on`, `currency`, `budget { total, google_ads }`, `campaigns` (0 to 10, each `{ key, channel, name, goal, budget, starts_on, ends_on }`), `posts` (0 to 200, each `{ key, channel, on, topic }`), `measures`, and two fields the first design left open: `google_ads_account` (`NNN-NNN-NNNN`, which a plan with campaigns needs and one without them refuses) and `replaces`, the id of an approved plan still in force that the new one supersedes. Every amount is a decimal string of at most eight digits and at most two decimals, compared in hundredths and never as a float. Farik checks the plan and answers every fault at once, each with its code and field: `marketing_plan_text` (a title of 3 to 100 characters, a summary of 20 to 600 and a text of 200 to 16,000, trimmed); `marketing_plan_dates` (`starts_on` no earlier than yesterday's UTC date, so an owner west of UTC is not refused their own today, and `ends_on` not before it and at most 92 days later, both days counted); `marketing_plan_currency` (three capital letters); `marketing_plan_budget` (Google Ads at most the total, and the campaigns' budgets adding up to at most Google Ads'); `marketing_plan_campaign` (each of channel `google_ads`, a name of 1 to 100 characters, a goal of 1 to 300, a budget above 0 and dates inside the plan's); `marketing_plan_post` (each of one of eleven channels, `instagram`, `x`, `facebook`, `linkedin`, `threads`, `bluesky`, `tiktok`, `pinterest`, `youtube`, `google_business` and `mastodon`, on a day inside the plan's, with a topic of 1 to 200 characters); `marketing_plan_key` (every campaign's and post's key is lower-case words joined by `-`, at most 40 characters, and unique across the plan); `marketing_plan_measures` (1 to 10, of 3 to 200 characters each); `google_ads_account_needed`; and `marketing_plan_unknown` for a `replaces` that names no approved plan in force. A plan whose dates, amounts or channels cannot be read is answered with those faults alone, since the checks after them would be guesses, and a task with a plan waiting already is refused `marketing_plan_waiting` before anything else is checked. The plan's number is `MP-<n>`, one more than the highest of the log's plans and of the `MP-<n>.md` files in `docs/marketing/plans/` of the project's root and of the task's worktree, taken under one lock, so a fresh clone never reuses a committed number. Farik writes the text, under the heading `# MP-<n>: <title>`, to `docs/marketing/plans/MP-<n>.md` in the task's worktree (`marketing_plan_file_exists` when it is there), after the permission check of that path, which is why the daemon's hook judges a Farik tool of the `write_workspace` tier by the path its input names and the contract must allow it (`path_outside_allowed` otherwise). It records `marketing_plan.proposed` and answers the plan's id and that the agent is to end its turn: the owner's decision starts the next session, as a question's answer does (5.7).

The owner's decision (added in 0.55). Only the owner decides, under `ask` and `auto` alike (ADR 0041), with the commands `marketing_plan_decide { plan, decision, note? }`, `decision` `approve` or `return`, and `marketing_plan_end { plan, note? }`, which arrive only on `POST /command` behind the daemon's token or the browser's `command` behind its cookie, as `tool_approve` does (ADR 0031, 8.6), and never from an agent's session. `note` is at most 600 characters; it is the reason, and required, for `return` (`marketing_plan_reason_needed`), and optional for the rest. The refusals are `unknown_marketing_plan`, `marketing_plan_decided` (decided before), `marketing_plan_expired` (approving a plan whose `ends_on` is past), `marketing_plan_not_approved` (ending one that was never approved) and `marketing_plan_ended`. A plan whose task was cancelled may still be approved, since the plan does not depend on its task. The check and the write are one step under one lock, `PLANS`, which the owner's decision, the owner's end and the dated ends all hold, and every end goes through one function that reads the plans again under it and records nothing for a plan already ended, so two ends at once record one. `marketing_plan.approved { plan, note }` and `marketing_plan.returned { plan, reason }` (8.5) carry the proposing task on their envelope and no agent or session; the store reads one that names either as deciding nothing, as it reads a connector call's decision, and an end that names either likewise. The decision lowers the task's `open_plans` (5.7), and the next session is told it as the owner's own words (5.7). One plan is active at a time: among the approved plans not ended, the one approved last whose first and last day hold today, in UTC. Approving a plan ends, in the same step and with `marketing_plan.ended { why: replaced, replaced_by }`, every approved plan not ended that starts on or after the new one's first day; one that started earlier ends on the new one's first day, by the rule below, and a plan that is ended before its first day replaces nothing. A plan whose last day has passed ends `expired`. Those two dated ends are made by a rule with no model, `end_marketing_plans`, which every tick whose scope names no task runs first, before its pause check, so that it also runs while the team is paused, starts no session and does not use up the tick. The owner ends an approved plan at any time (`why: by_owner`). Ending a plan records its event and, in the same step, stops its posts as Posts below says (added in 0.57); pausing its campaigns comes with Google Ads (08f), which extends the one function that records an end.

Where the owner sees a plan (added in 0.55). The queries `marketing_plan.list {}`, every plan newest first with its `plan`, `title`, `state` (`proposed`, `returned`, `approved`, `active` or `ended`), dates, `currency`, `total`, `agent_id`, `task_id` and `proposed_at`, and `marketing_plan.get { plan }`, the proposal whole with its state, the owner's decision with their words and time, and the end with its `why`, the owner's note when they ended it with one, and the plan that replaced it when a newer plan did, and `not_found` for a plan nobody proposed, both read the log; `waiting.list` gives the row of 5.7 (a plan whose task is not on the board has no row). On Today the row "Marketing plan to approve: <title>" shows the agent's picture and name, the summary, the total budget with its currency, the dates, and "Review", which opens the plan's page at `/marketing/plans/MP-<n>`; a row of a kind the page does not know is skipped, never an error. The page shows the summary first, the budget as a table by channel and campaign, the post calendar by week, the measures, and the plan's text as text, never rendered as markup; while the plan waits, "Approve" and "Send back", whose reason is required and at most 600 characters; while it is approved or active, "End the plan", with an optional note and a confirmation that says what ending does; and once it is decided, who decided it, when, and their words; an ended plan says how it ended, quoting the owner's note when they ended it with one and linking the plan that replaced it when a newer plan did. It shows the budget alone: what is spent comes with step 08f. What its posts have done is shown slot by slot under the calendar (added in 0.57; Posts, below). The command line is `farik marketing plan show [<plan>] [--json]`, which reads the log and lists the plans or shows one whole, `farik marketing plan approve <plan> [--note <text>]`, `farik marketing plan return <plan> --reason <text>` and `farik marketing plan end <plan> [--note <text>]`, which send the decision to the daemon when one drives the project and make it in the process otherwise (section 3, ADR 0014).

Posts (added in 0.52; as built in 0.57, phase 7 step 08d, ADR 0042). The agent writes a post with `farik_schedule_post` and never calls a posting service's write tool itself: Buffer's `create_post` and `edit_post` are `denied` to it (6.7), and Farik makes the call itself, one hour before the post's time, with the connection of the agent that wrote it. The tool, of the `read` tier since what governs it is the plan and the owner, is offered only to a Marketing Specialist's `implement` session about a task, and is `post_refused` from any other. Its input is `channel` (one of the plan's eleven), `buffer_channel` (Buffer's id for the channel, which the agent reads with Buffer's `list_channels`; `^[A-Za-z0-9_-]{1,64}$`), `text`, `media` (at most four `{ url, kind }`, `kind` `image` or `video`), `at`, `details?` and `slot?`. Farik checks it in this order and answers the first fault with its code: `post_channel_unknown`, `post_buffer_channel_invalid`, `post_too_long` (1 character to the network's limit, counted in Unicode scalar values, with no NUL: x 280, bluesky 300, threads 500, mastodon 500, pinterest 500, google_business 1,500, instagram 2,200, tiktok 2,200, linkedin 3,000, youtube 5,000, facebook 63,206), `post_media_invalid` (more than four, or another kind), `media_url_refused`, `post_needs_media` (Instagram and Pinterest need a picture or a clip, TikTok and YouTube a clip), `post_details` and `post_board_unknown` (YouTube needs `{ title, category_id }`, a title of 1 to 100 characters and one of Buffer's fifteen categories, as strings: 1, 2, 10, 15, 17, 19, 20 and 22 to 29; Pinterest needs `{ board }`, one of the channel's boards in Buffer's `get_channel`; no other network takes details), `post_time_invalid` (`at` is RFC 3339 with an offset), `post_in_the_past` and `post_too_far` (at most 92 days ahead). With a `slot`: `no_active_marketing_plan`, `not_a_plan_slot` (a slot of the active plan, of the same channel), `slot_used` (no other post of that plan and slot is scheduled or sent; a stopped, missed or failed one frees it), `post_off_its_day` (the date of `at` in its own offset is the slot's day, so Farik needs no time-zone table) and `post_too_soon` (at least three hours from now). Last, Farik's own `get_channel` through the agent's connection says whether the channel is the network's: `post_wrong_channel` (a channel that is another network's names that network in Farik's own words, never in Buffer's, and says "another network" for a word it does not know; when Buffer refuses the channel, its words come in an `untrusted` block), `buffer_not_connected` or `buffer_unreachable`. A picture's address is checked at scheduling and again at hand-over (`media_url_refused`): `https` only, port 443, no user or password, at most 2,000 characters, a public IP address or a name of two labels or more that is not `.local`, `.localhost`, `.internal`, `.lan` or `.home.arpa`; Farik's request for it follows no redirect and no proxy, connects only to a public address (a name that resolves to a private one is refused at the connection), and must be answered with a PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP picture (never an SVG) of at most 20 MiB, or with a video of at most a gigabyte, whose size it says when it says one. The agent's pictures are the business's own, ones the owner gave it, or ones it made with its creative services (6.7), never another's; the address must stay public until the post goes out, since Buffer reads it then.

A post that fills an unused slot of the active plan is recorded `social_post.scheduled { approved_by: plan, plan, slot }`, whose sequence number is the post's number, and the tool answers `{ post, hands_over_at }`. Today shows it under "Going out" with its text, pictures, channel and time in the browser's time zone, why it may go out, and Stop; one hour before its time Farik calls Buffer's `create_post { channelId, text, schedulingType: automatic, mode: customScheduled, dueAt, assets, metadata? }` itself (`metadata` for YouTube's title and category and Pinterest's board alone) and records `social_post.sent { post, buffer_post }`. A post outside the plan (no `slot`) is recorded `social_post.requested` and waits for the owner on Today with "Post it" and "Don't post" (`social_post_decide { post, decision: post | dont_post, note? }`, from the daemon's token or the browser's cookie alone): "Post it" records `social_post.scheduled { post, approved_by: owner }` on the request's task, with no agent or session, and the hand-over comes an hour before its time or at once when that has passed; "Don't post" records `social_post.stopped { by: declined }`. `unknown_post`, `post_decided` and `post_in_the_past` (to allow a request whose time has passed or is within five minutes, too near for Farik to hand over, or to decline one whose time has passed) refuse a decision. A request whose time comes within five minutes undecided is recorded `social_post.missed { why: undecided }`. Under `auto` it is sent as 5.6 lets any outward act (ADR 0041; phase 7 step 10h). The task does not wait for a request or for a post.

Stop (`social_post_stop { post }`, the owner's alone) takes a scheduled post not yet with Buffer back at once (`social_post.stopped { by: owner }`); for a post Buffer has, whose time is still ahead, Farik first calls Buffer's own `delete_post` and records `stopped { by: owner, taken_back: true }` only on its success, else refuses `post_not_taken_back` with nothing recorded; once its time has passed it is `post_already_out`; `post_being_handed_over` while Farik is giving it to Buffer, `post_not_going_out` for a post requested, stopped, missed or failed, and `unknown_post`. Ending a plan stops, as `stopped { by: plan_ended }`, the plan's posts not yet with Buffer when the owner ended it, or when a newer plan replaced it and the post's slot day is on or after the newer plan's first day; never when the plan only ran out, since each post was checked to fall on a slot day inside the plan, and never a post Farik is handing over at that moment, which stays with its Stop.
