The daemon and `farik connect` sign a Farik connector in as any server that signs in: `connector.sign_in` answers `provider` and no `user_code`; an attempt is bound to the agent, the server's name and the server's whole transport, so one made for the pair serves neither an `http` server of the same name nor the pair with other settings (`sign_in_unknown`); and `connector.sign_in_status`'s words name the provider where an app signs in. The launch route answers a signed-in Farik connector with its command and an empty environment, never a token, after refreshing a grant that expires within a minute, and 403 `sign_in_again` for a lapsed one; a session's setup refreshes it as it does an `http` one's and leaves a lapsed one out; its tools are listed with no token, since a `stdio` server takes no bearer; and `team.get` says `auth: oauth`, `provider` and `revokes: false`. On the command line, `farik connect <agent> <name>` writes `Sign in to <provider> in your browser: <address>` and then `Signed in to <provider>.` to the error stream.

Farik Cloud (added in 0.61; ADR 0044; it replaces 0.60's paragraph on the user's own sign-in apps, which were dropped with phase 7 step 03f before they were built). Farik is open source, so no build carries a credential of Farik's, before or after the launch (8.6). Until the web launch, nothing that needs an app of Farik's is offered: no build has an entry in the table above, GitHub's server (`https://api.githubcopilot.com/mcp/`) is connected with a pasted fine-grained token, sent as `Authorization: Bearer`, and Farik's own `google-ads` connector cannot be signed in to (`sign_in_not_supported`). The code read `FARIK_GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` at build time until phase 7 step 08e's Task 8 removed it (changed in 0.64): `REGISTERED_APPS` is empty in every build, a test fails for any source that reads a variable at build time with `env!` or `option_env!`, bar the names Cargo itself sets for a build (`CARGO_PKG_*`, `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR`, `CARGO_BIN_EXE_*`, `CARGO_CRATE_NAME` and `CARGO_BIN_NAME`), and the loopback sign-in with PKCE, the refresh and the table's matching stay, for the sign-in through Farik Cloud to build on. From the web launch (phase 11), Farik Cloud, a service Farik runs on AWS, signs users in. Its free tier holds Farik's GitHub App and Farik's Google app and their secrets, server side. The local Farik starts each sign-in through it and refreshes each grant through it, with the user's Farik account; Farik Cloud answers the authorization address, adds the secret on the exchange and each refresh, and hands the tokens straight back, storing none, and they are kept with the agent's keys, as every grant is. A Farik account is signed in to once per computer, in the browser; its session is the user's own credential, kept in the OS keychain, and Farik Cloud's fair-use limits are per account. Phase 11 plans how the account signs in, GitHub's flow, which the founder wants to be a single click, and the screens.

GitHub with a pasted key (added in 0.65; ADR 0044; phase 7 step 07c). Until Farik Cloud signs users in (above), the Product Manager's and the Architect's kits each carry GitHub's official server, `https://api.githubcopilot.com/mcp/`, as an `http` connector named `github` with `credential_keys: [GITHUB_KEY]`, `Authorization: Bearer {GITHUB_KEY}`, a `key_page` and no `oauth`, so `team.get`'s row says `auth: keys` and the connect page shows its key field. The kit format takes a sign-in or keys, never both (`oauth_with_keys`, `oauth_header_conflict`), and a sign-in would be `sign_in_not_supported`, since GitHub registers no client by itself. The key is a fine-grained personal access token: with one, GitHub's server lists every tool and its API enforces the key's permissions (GitHub's changelog of 2026-01-28), so the listing is the same for every user's key and a pin can hold; a classic key reaches every repository the user can and the server hides tools by its scopes, which differ by user, so the kits do not take one. The entries narrow the server with headers, which the listing and every session send as they send the key: the Product Manager's `X-MCP-Toolsets: issues,projects`; the Architect's `X-MCP-Toolsets: repos,pull_requests` and `X-MCP-Readonly: true`, which keeps only the read tools. Neither entry names `X-MCP-Tools`, since GitHub makes the server fail to start on a tool name it does not know, so one tool GitHub removed would stop every user's GitHub until a Farik release, whereas a toolset that loses a tool only drops it, which the live pin test reports; nor `X-MCP-Exclude-Tools`, since a `denied` tool is never offered already, the pin comparison runs both ways and GitHub's page is silent on an unknown name there; nor `X-MCP-Lockdown`, which hides the public issue details of users without push access, the reports a Product Manager imports, and which GitHub calls a best-effort content filter, not a security boundary. Three fences hold what an agent may do: GitHub refuses what the key may not do; the headers keep other toolsets off the listing; and the tags decide what is offered, so a `denied` tool and a tool the kit does not name (`tool_not_tagged`) are never offered, and nothing that deletes, administers, touches secrets or workflows, merges, pushes or changes a file is. A tool GitHub lists that the kit does not name is never offered until a pin update tags it, and the update's rule is `denied`, with no label. The Product Manager's tags: eight reads are `network` (`issue_read`, `list_issues`, `search_issues`, `list_issue_types`, `list_issue_fields`, `get_label`, `projects_list` and `projects_get`); `issue_write` and `add_issue_comment` are `external_effect` with no allowance, so each call waits for the human, who sees its whole input, since an issue or a comment is published to everyone who can see the repository; `sub_issue_write`, `update_issue_comment` and `projects_write` are `denied`. A kit tags a tool, not an argument, and both writes reach pull requests too, a pull request being an issue to GitHub's API (`issue_write`'s `update` method edits, closes and reassigns), so the skill `using-product-sources` forbids giving either a pull request's number, and whether a key with Issues write alone can close one is recorded by the live check, not fixed. The Architect's tags: fifteen reads are `network` (`search_code`, `get_file_contents`, `list_branches`, `list_commits`, `get_commit`, `search_commits`, `list_tags`, `get_tag`, `list_releases`, `get_latest_release`, `get_release_by_tag`, `search_repositories`, `list_pull_requests`, `pull_request_read` and `search_pull_requests`) and `list_repository_collaborators`, a list of who may change a repository, is `denied`; GitHub refuses `pull_request_read`'s `get_status` and `get_check_runs` to its key, which `using-architecture-sources` says. The two entries share the address, the key's name and `about`, and differ in headers, `key_page`, tools, labels, `why` and `setup`, so they hash differently, and a team file that gives an Architect the Product Manager's wider entry fails `matches_kit`. "Get your key" opens `key_page`, GitHub's template address for a fine-grained key (`name`, `description`, `expires_in`, and `<permission>=<level>`, which GitHub's form validates), with a life of 366 days, GitHub's longest dated one: the Product Manager's asks for repository Issues read and write, the Architect's for Contents and Pull requests read. Neither sets the resource owner, which is the user's to choose, nor organisation Projects, which GitHub's form accepts only when the owner is an organisation, and most users' owner is their account, so the Product Manager's setup says to add it for an organisation's boards. A fine-grained key has one owner, the user's account or one organisation, so a user whose code sits under both connects GitHub for one of them per agent until the sign-in; an organisation that requires approval leaves the key `pending`, reading public resources only, until an administrator approves it; it does not reach Projects owned by a user account, so the Product Manager reads an organisation's boards only; an organisation may cap a key's life below a year, or need the user signed in to its single sign-on when the key is made, which both setups say; and every key reads all public repositories. GitHub's documentation (read 2026-10-06) says its server is available to every GitHub user whatever the plan and lists Copilot as a prerequisite only for Copilot's own editors, which Farik is not, so the setups say nothing about Copilot; a key GitHub refuses shows the connect page's words for a refused key, and the founder's live check (step 07c, Task 6) settles whether an account that never turned Copilot on connects. Remove deletes the key from the keychain, as for every keyed service, and the key works at GitHub until it ends or the user deletes it there. The live pin test reads `FARIK_KIT_GITHUB_GITHUB_KEY` once for both kits, since they share a connector name. From the web launch (phase 11) each entry signs in through Farik Cloud instead: it loses `headers.Authorization`, `credential_keys` and `key_page`, gains `oauth: {}` (the table's GitHub entry, matched by host) and keeps its `X-MCP-*` headers; its hash changes, so every agent connected with a key sees "Connect again"; and Farik's GitHub App then asks for repository Contents and Pull requests read, Issues read and write, and organisation Projects read (ADR 0044's amendment of 2026-10-06), or the Product Manager's two writes stop at the switch.

A user's own skills (added in 0.40; ADR 0034). Before any kit, the user can give one agent, or the whole team, a skill in the Agent Skills format: a folder with a `SKILL.md` and the files it refers to. The team's lives in `.farik/skills/<name>/` and one agent's in `.farik/agents/<agent_id>/skills/<name>/`, both read from the project's root, never a task's worktree, as `team.yaml` is; agents cannot write `.farik/` (5.3). A name resolves agent, then team, then role. An agent's pin replaces the team's skill of that name whatever its state, so a skill waiting for review never falls back silently to the team's. A team or agent skill whose name is one of the shipped skills replaces it only when the person said so: `skill_save` and `skill_confirm` with such a name are refused `skill_name_taken` unless they carry `replace_shipped: true`. While such a replacement is `in_use`, the sessions that load skills (8.2) leave the shipped skill out of the prompt and load the replacement on demand; the sessions given one Farik tool alone have no `Skill` tool, so they keep the shipped skill in their prompt.

A skill is instructions an agent follows, so the person reads all of it before it is used: adding or editing one shows the whole `SKILL.md` and every file first, and the add is the confirmation. `team.yaml` pins each skill, `skills: [{ name, sha256 }]` at the top level for the team and on each agent for that agent, at most 20 in a list and a name once in it (`skill_name_twice`). `sha256` is `skill_sha256`: the sha256, in lower-case hex, of the canonical JSON (compact, keys sorted at every depth) of `{ <relative path>: <sha256 of the file's bytes> }` over every file in the folder. As a connector is (ADR 0030), a skill is confirmed on this computer, because `.farik/` travels with the repository and a clone, a pull or a hand edit could change a skill's instructions and its pin together. The states, checked in this order as `skills.list` computes them for one agent: `replaced`, a team row when the agent pins a skill of that name and a role row when a team or agent skill of that name is `in_use`; `missing`, a pinned folder that is not there; `review`, the folder exists but its hash differs from the pin, or the newest `skill.added`, `skill.changed` or `skill.confirmed` for it in this machine's log (8.4, never committed) does not carry the pin's hash, or a `skill.removed` follows it, or there is none, or reading the folder or the checks below refuse it; and `in_use` otherwise, the only state a skill loads in. `skill_confirm`, with the hash the person saw, reads the folder, refuses `skill_hash_mismatch` unless its hash is that one, refuses whatever the checks refuse with that refusal's code, writes the hash as the pin when it differs, then records `skill.confirmed`, so a skill whose folder or pin changed outside Farik is used again only after the person has read the folder as it is now. Shipped skills are Farik's own, neither pinned nor confirmed. In the browser's key mapper the paths of a command's or answer's `files` are names, not wire keys, and cross unchanged (added in 0.42).

The checks, which hold in `skill_save`, `skill_confirm` and when a session assembles its skills. `SKILL.md` opens with a `---` line and closes its frontmatter with another (CRLF is read as LF); the frontmatter is a YAML mapping read under ADR 0007's options. `name` and `description` are strings: a missing `SKILL.md`, no frontmatter, a frontmatter that is not a mapping or a missing `name` is `skill_frontmatter_invalid`; `name` is lower-case letters and digits joined by single hyphens, at most 64 characters, and its folder's (`skill_name_invalid`, `skill_name_mismatch`); `description` has 1 to 1,024 characters, counted in Unicode scalar values (`skill_description_invalid`). Limits: `SKILL.md` at most 32 KiB; at most 16 files, `SKILL.md` among them, each at most 64 KiB, UTF-8 text with no NUL; 256 KiB in all; a path of at most 3 parts, the file's name included, each part matching `^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._-]{0,99}$`, so none starts with a dot, and no link anywhere (`skill_too_large`, `skill_too_many_files`, `skill_file_not_text`, `skill_path_invalid`). A skill may not run commands or attach files, because Claude Code does both when a skill loads, and an attachment is read by its own code, which no hook sees: a `SKILL.md` holding `` !` `` or ```` ```! ```` anywhere, or a line whose first characters after spaces are three or more backticks or tildes followed by `!`, is refused `skill_runs_commands`; one with an `@` is refused `skill_attaches_files` unless the character before it is an ASCII letter or digit or one of `._%+-`, as in an email address (Claude Code attaches `@<path>` after any whitespace, a byte order mark included, and after 。、？！). A skill names a bundled file as a path or a Markdown link, which the agent reads with `Read`, which the hook judges. Every other frontmatter field (`allowed-tools`, `hooks`, `model`, `context` and the rest) is accepted, dropped from the session's copy, and listed to the person ("Farik ignores: allowed-tools, hooks").

The commands (sent through `here_or_sent` from the command line and as `command` through the RPC from the browser) are `skill_save { level, agent?, files, replace_shipped? }`, which adds a skill, or replaces the one of its name, `files` mapping each relative path to its text; `skill_remove { level, agent?, name }`; and `skill_confirm { level, agent?, name, sha256, replace_shipped? }`; `level` is `team`, or `agent` with the agent's id. A save writes the new folder beside its place as `.<name>.new-<random>`, moves any old folder to `.<name>.old-<random>`, renames the new one into place (`rename(2)` will not replace a folder that is not empty), deletes the old one, then writes the pin in the team file, then records `skill.added`, or `skill.changed` for a name already pinned; the pin is written before the event for a confirmation as well. A removal deletes the folder, unpins it and records `skill.removed`, and for a `missing` skill it unpins and records and does not fail. The refusals are `skill_unknown`, a name not pinned there, `skill_hash_mismatch`, `skill_name_taken`, `skill_limit_reached`, the twenty-first of a list, `agent_unknown`, and each check's code; a refusal writes nothing. A whole-team write keeps the pins: `team.save` and `agent.replace` keep the top-level `skills` and each kept agent's `skills` as the team file holds them, whatever the browser sends (an agent a save adds has none), and `template.apply` keeps the top-level `skills` and the skills of each agent it keeps, an agent it removes losing its pins as removing an agent does; only the three commands change a pin. Removing an agent leaves its skill folders, which are the user's files, unpinned and unused. A saved team template carries no skills. `farik skill list [--agent <id>]`, `farik skill show <name> (--team | --agent <id>)`, `farik skill add <folder> (--team | --agent <id>) [--yes] [--replace]`, `farik skill remove` and `farik skill confirm <name> (--team | --agent <id>) <hash> [--replace]` are F9's.

What stays: a confirmed skill can steer its agent within the agent's own tiers and connectors in every later session, and its description is listed in each; the reading before adding is the defence against the instructions themselves (8.6). A skill that runs scripts is not supported.

The kit file (added in 0.44; ADR 0036). `roles/<role>/kit.yaml` holds `role` (the folder's), `skills` (names, at most 20) and `connectors` (at most 20), validated by `docs/schemas/kit.schema.json` and then by the loader in `farik-roles`, which embeds the file with its skills and refuses, naming the field as a JSON pointer and a code, a `role` that is not the folder's (`kit_role_mismatch`); a skill with no `skills/<name>/SKILL.md` (`kit_skill_missing`), one `role.yaml` names too (`kit_skill_in_role`; it stays in the prompt there), or one the checks of a user's skill refuse, with that refusal's code (6.7, ADR 0034); a connector name twice (`kit_connector_twice`). A connector is `stdio`, `http` or `container`. A `stdio` or `http` one takes the team file's fields (`command`, `args`, `url`, `headers`, `credential_keys`, `oauth`), each refusal `validate_team` gives the same entry (so a kit cannot ship a key in a header or an argument), and the setup copy: `title`, `about`, `why`, `setup`, `key_page` (an `https` address, required when it takes keys: `key_page_missing`) and `labels`, a tool to what it does in the user's words (`label_not_a_tool` for one that is not a tool); a field a transport does not take or lacks is `kit_field_not_allowed` or `kit_field_missing`. Every connector has `tools`, each `network`, `external_effect` or `denied`, never `read`, and a tool with no value is a schema error. A `stdio` connector starts only `npx`, `bunx`, `uvx` or `pipx run` (also by path or as `.cmd`/`.exe`), with its first non-flag argument the package at an exact version, `name@1.2.3` or `name==1.2.3`, and none of `-p`, `--package`, `--from`, `--spec`, `--with`, `--registry` or `--index-url`; or an absolute path to a binary Farik ships (`farik`, `farik-…`). Any other command, every shell and every other runner (`sh`, `pnpm`, `npm`, `yarn`, `bun`, `uv`, `deno`, `docker`, `node`, `python`) is `package_not_pinned` (at `command` or `args`), so the code Farik runs changes only with a Farik release and its pin review. A `container` connector, a server Farik runs in Docker itself with an `image`, `module_root` and `args` and no copy, is only the UI/UX Designer's built-in browser (`container_not_builtin`) with its image pinned by `@sha256:` (`image_not_pinned`). `allowances` map a tool tagged `external_effect` to `{ calls, what }` (`allowance_not_external`); phase 7 step 05b reads them. Farik's own words never say "MCP", "OAuth" or "token", so no field of the copy holds one in any case (`copy_word_refused`), with one exception the founder made on 2026-10-02: `setup` may quote a service's own label so the user finds it on the service's page ("On Slack's page, copy the value labelled ‘Bot User OAuth Token’."). A quoted label is the text after `‘` up to `’`, after `“` up to `”`, or after `"` up to `"`, on one line, at most 60 characters; the ASCII apostrophe never opens one, an opening mark with no closing one quotes nothing, and a longer span is checked as the rest is. Every role ships a `kit.yaml`; none has a service before step 06, and only the Designer's has the browser.

Kit skills and kit connectors (added in 0.44; ADR 0036). A kit's skills live in the role's `skills/` folder, are Farik's own (neither pinned nor confirmed) and load on demand after the agent's and the team's: one name resolves agent, team, kit, a pin of the name at either level standing for the kit's skill whatever its state. They count among the shipped names (a team or agent skill of that name needs `replace_shipped`), `skills.list` lists one as a `role` row after the role's rows, `replaced` while the agent's or the team's skill of that name is in use, and `skill.get`'s role level answers its `SKILL.md` as the session's copy holds it. A kit's `stdio` or `http` connector is connected by name: `farik connect <agent> <name>` with neither `--command` nor `--url`, or the browser's `{ name, source: "kit" }`. The entry is written whole into the team file, `{ name, source: kit, transport, ... }`, with every field from the kit and the kit's tags as its `tools`, held to every rule of a custom entry and hashed with `"source": "kit"` added so that every kept hash stands and a relabelled entry needs "Connect again". `connector_connect` refuses a `source: kit` entry that is not exactly the one its agent's role's kit gives (`connector_not_in_kit`), which also refuses a service of another role's kit. Connecting lists the service's tools to prove the key or the sign-in works, and writes the kit's tags: a tool the service lists and the kit does not tag is `tool_not_tagged`, and a pinned tool the service dropped costs nothing. A request for a kit service carries `tags: {}` (`kit_names_these` otherwise), and the command line refuses `--arg`, `--header`, `--key`, `--tag`, `--client-id`, `--callback-port`, `--scope` and `--sign-in` with it, reads a value for each of the kit's keys from standard input after "Make a key at <key_page>, then paste <KEY>:" on the error stream, and signs in when the kit's service does. A kit entry that is not what its role's kit says now (a release tagged a tool `denied`, or changed a field) is left out of the session's `mcp.json` and registration and shown `connect_again`; one whose service the kit no longer has is shown `not_in_kit`, with "Remove" alone. A team file or a clone that forges a `source: kit` entry with wider tags hashes differently from anything kept and is refused at connect and left out at session setup; the launch route and the hook take only what a session's registration holds and re-check the kept hash. Every shipped `stdio` and `http` kit connector is pinned: `crates/runtime/tests/live_kit_pins.rs`, run with `FARIK_LIVE_TESTS=1` and each key from `FARIK_KIT_<NAME>_<KEY>` (a bearer from `FARIK_KIT_<NAME>_BEARER` for one that signs in), fails naming the connector and the unset variable, and on any drift with "a pin update re-reviews every tag"; `pin_drift` names the tools a service added and dropped.

### 6.8 UI/UX Designer (added in 0.31)

Mandate: a developer focused on the interface (ADR 0026; `docs/design/designer-chats-templates.md`). It explores the app, plans a change to it, and implements the change once the Product Manager approves the plan. With the Software Developer it is the only role that changes code, beside the DevOps Engineer's incident fixes (6.9; changed in 0.32): its tasks are not held to the team's `document_paths` (5.3), and they work on `feature/FRK-<n>` or `fix/FRK-<n>` by the contract's `change` (5.14). The team builder suggests one (4.1), named Iris, "Makes it clear, calm and easy to use", with the `extra-1` picture and the tag "UX" in its own colour, pale clay; the user may untick it (the founder's choices of 2026-09-30).

Produces: design plans, diffs, commits on task branches, completion notes.

Cannot: change code before the Product Manager approves the task's plan; modify contracts; accept work; push to shared branches without the `git_remote` grant; touch files outside the contract's allowed paths.

Default tools: read, write_workspace, execute, git_local, the Developer's, and the team's two permission answers apply to it as to the Developer (5.6). Default model: the Developer's, Claude Opus 5.5 at `high`. It ships six skills: `ux-review-heuristics`, `wcag-accessibility-checks`, `brand-and-design-tokens`, `plain-language-interface-wording`, `writing-mockups`, and `responsive-and-phone-checks`. Its `brand-and-design-tokens` reads `docs/marketing/brand/brand-kit.md` first when it exists and takes the colours and the voice from it, then finds where the project keeps its design tokens (added in 0.55; the kit is the Marketing Specialist's, 6.5).

Its flow. Every task assigned to a Designer runs this way, whatever its risk. The task's latest `design_plan.*` event, read from the log at every tick, decides which session runs next:

1. Explore. With no plan yet, or with the last one returned, Farik starts the Designer's `explore` session (8.2) in the task's worktree. It holds the read tier's built-ins whatever the agent's tiers, its every call is held to the read tier by the hook and Farik's tool server, and a call to its browser to `network` (changed in 0.33), it runs no commands, and it has six Farik tools: `farik_read_task`, `farik_read_board`, `farik_read_rules`, `farik_read_criteria`, `farik_read_decisions`, and `farik_propose_design_plan`, and a seventh, `farik_check_page`, with the browser (added in 0.33). With its Playwright connector on and the app's preview set (5.6), it reads the project's files and also opens the app in the browser, and its message says so; browsing before the plan is approved is intended, since the plan gate holds only writes, commands, git and, from 0.39, a connector's `external_effect` call (changed in 0.33, from no browser). When the last plan was returned, the message holds the Product Manager's reason as untrusted text (8.6). The session ends with `farik_propose_design_plan { plan }`, which records `design_plan.proposed`. The plan is plain text of 200 to 8,000 characters. It opens with a summary for the user of 20 to 600 characters and a blank line, as a note does (5.4), and says what the agent saw, what it will change, and what it will leave alone. The tool is refused with `design_plan_refused` outside the Designer's `explore` session of its task, and for a plan outside those bounds.
2. The Product Manager's approval. With a plan proposed, Farik starts the active Product Manager's `verify` session about the task. The plan is in its message, and its one Farik tool is `farik_decide_design_plan { approve, reason }`, which records `design_plan.approved` or `design_plan.returned`. The tool is refused with `design_plan_refused` from any other agent or session, for a blank reason, and when no plan waits for a decision. While the team has no active Product Manager, the task waits. The human's own gates are unchanged (5.4, 5.16), and the human is not asked to approve the plan.
3. Implement. With the plan approved, Farik starts the Designer's `implement` session with the agent's full tiers and the approved plan in its message. From there the task is an ordinary one: a completion note, `verifying`, the review (5.4), and acceptance.

A session that ends without its one answer, an explore session with no plan or a decision session with no decision, is started again, and each restart counts toward the contract's sessions allowance (5.5). Each returned plan counts against the task's iteration limit, and when the returns reach it the governor escalates the task with `iterations` (5.2). The order is the governor's to enforce, not the prompt's. In any session of a Designer's task whose latest plan is not approved, the hook and Farik's tool server refuse the Designer's `write_workspace`, `execute`, `git_local` and `git_remote` calls with `design_plan_not_approved`, and the hook refuses its `external_effect` connector calls the same way, before any is asked about (5.6; added in 0.39). They read the plan from the log at each call, so a plan proposed during a session closes the gate at once. A Designer's session that is about no task is held as not approved.

Its reviewer is the Architect, else a Developer (5.1, 5.4).

What the web app shows. Setup and the Team page draw each agent's picture in a ring of its role's colour. On the Team page, when the team has a Designer and a Developer, the cost section says that the Designer uses the Developer's model, so the Designer adds to what a day costs, and that planning costs a little too, and it links to Costs. The board says "Planning" for a Designer in its `explore` session, and `team.activity` says "Planning" and the task's title for it too. While a plan the Designer proposed waits for a decision on a task in progress, the Designer's activity line is "Waiting for" the active Product Manager's name "to approve a plan", about that task. Each card on the Team page says its agent's activity line in place of "On the team", and "Paused" for a paused agent. A Designer's task page opens its plan tab with the latest plan, signed with its day and time and read from `task.get`'s `design_plan` (`plan`, `state` `proposed`, `approved` or `returned`, and the `reason` once decided; null when there is none). A card above it says one of three things: the plan waits for the active Product Manager, and the user need do nothing; it was approved, so the Designer is changing the page as the plan says, with the time and the reason; or it was sent back, so the Designer is looking again and will write a new plan, with the time, the reason, and how many plans have been sent back of the number that stops the task ("If a third is sent back"). A decision counts only for the plan it followed. Times say "today" for the day the page is read, in UTC. While the task is in progress, the page's heading says the same, and its lead counts the plans ("First plan.") in place of the tries. The task's history words the three `design_plan.*` events.

Its browser and its design review (added in 0.33; ADR 0026). The Designer opens the app in the preview Farik runs in Docker's sandbox (8.3), through the Playwright connector (5.6), in its `explore` and `implement` sessions and in its design review of a Software Developer's change to the interface (5.4). It cannot be assigned a task while it cannot open the app: the governor refuses the assignment with `preview_not_set` when the team has no preview and with `designer_needs_sandbox` when the sandbox is off or Docker cannot be reached (checked once a minute at most), and with `designer_needs_browser` when its Playwright connector is off, so that Farik gives it no work, as the agent page says. A process that has not said what runs previews, a command handled in its own process among them, runs none, so the assignment is refused there too (both added in the step's fix wave). What the web app shows of it: a task whose design review is due shows "Checking the screens" on its page, as its board card does while the Designer's design review session runs, and "Waiting on the Designer" on both while every Designer is paused, the page with a link to resume it, or "The Designer's browser is off" on both while its Playwright connector is off, the page saying so and linking to the Designer's page to turn it on, the active Designer's when a paused one is also on the team (added in the step's fix wave). The task page and the gate of a task whose change is to the interface show the Designer's letter, "<name>, your UI/UX Designer, checked the screens first", with its reasons and whether it passed the change on or sent it back, and "The four checks", one figure per width and theme with the screenshot, the width in pixels, and "No problems found" or each problem's help, rule and impact; on the gate the letter and the checks come before the Architect's review. The letter is signed by the Designer who recorded the review, not the team's first Designer. The Designer's earlier send-backs fold under "<name> sent it back once, on <day>" (or so many times, last on a day), with their reasons. The gate's "About this task" gains "Screens checked", the day of the passing review and its Designer, "Code reviewed", the day of the latest review that passed the code and its reviewer, and "Who looked at it, in order": Farik's checks, then each design review and code review in time order with its outcome, then "Now you decide" while the work waits on the person. Once the Designer passed the screens, the reviewer's letter is headed "<name>, your <role>, reviewed the code after <Designer> passed the screens", and the send-backs end with "<builder> changed it, and the next look passed." No wire carries what the builder changed in answer to a send-back, so the line does not say it; the builder's letter above it is its latest completion note. `task.get` carries `ui_change` and `design_review`, null for a change that is not to the interface, else its `state` (`not_needed`, `waiting`, `waiting_on_designer`, `preview_missing`, `designer_needs_sandbox`, `designer_needs_browser`, `passed` or `failed`), its `reasons` once recorded, and its `checks`; and `design_reviews`, every `design_review.recorded` of the task oldest first, each with the Designer's `agent_id`, `pass`, `reasons` and `recorded_at`; `tasks.list` gives a task in `verifying` whose change is to the interface its `design_review_state`, the same `state`, which the board's card reads, and leaves it out for a task whose review cannot be read rather than failing the whole list (changed in 0.37); the query `task.screenshot { task_id, file }` answers `{ png_base64 }` for a file one of that task's `page.checked` events names, and `not_found` for any other. A Designer's task page has a side panel, "How <name> works", with the flow's five steps marked done or now and the plans sent back of the number that stops the task.

### 6.9 DevOps Engineer (added in 0.32)

Mandate: deploy what the team integrated, watch production, restore the service when it fails, and fix the cause (ADR 0027; `docs/design/devops-engineer.md`). It is optional: the team builder offers it and does not suggest it. It changes code only in an incident's fix task and in the project's deploy configuration a task names.

Produces: deployments, incident notes, fixes on `fix/FRK-<n>` branches, completion notes.

Cannot: deploy anything but the commit the team integrated; call a platform's write tool itself; change secrets, environment variables, access rights, or scaling, or delete anything in production; open a shell in a production container.

Default tools: read, write_workspace, execute, git_local and network, the Developer's model, and three Farik tools only it may call, each of the `external_effect` tier and each taking no version or service: `farik_deploy`, which deploys the commit the default branch holds after the integrations its task depends on; `farik_restart`, which restarts the service's pods or redeploys the same version where there are none; and `farik_roll_back`, which returns the service to the last deployment Farik recorded as healthy. Its reviewer is the Architect, or the Developer when the team has no Architect. Its kit (6.7) connects the one platform the project runs on (AWS ECS or EKS, Kubernetes, Vercel or Netlify, Render, Railway or Fly), with the read tools `network` and every other tool `denied`.

Approval. The three tools are `external_effect` (5.6), and their approval is given in two ways only: the human's start of a sprint approves `farik_deploy` in that sprint's deploy tasks, and an open incident pre-approves `farik_restart` once and `farik_roll_back` once, as below. Every other call asks the human.

Deploys. A deployment is a task whose `change` is `deploy`, planned into a sprint; starting the sprint approves it. Phase 7 step 11 adds `deploy` to the contract's `change` field (5.11), and holds the DevOps Engineer's other tasks, its fixes and its deploy configuration, to the rules 5.3 and 5.12 set for the roles that change code. `farik_deploy` is refused outside a deploy task's session and an incident's fix. The task reaches `verifying` when the platform reports the deploy live and the service stays healthy for the project's settling period (five minutes by default): its health URL answers `2xx` and, where the connector offers one, the log error rate stays under the project's threshold.

Watching. While Farik runs, a watch tick asks the connected platform for its deployments and health once a minute, through the read tools, with no model and no session; it records `health.changed` only when the health changes. On the user's own computer Farik watches only while it runs; on the hosted service Farik runs the watch.

Incidents. A failed deploy or an unhealthy service records `incident.opened` and starts a DevOps Engineer session in which `farik_restart` is pre-approved once. If the service is not healthy within the settling period after the restart, `farik_roll_back` is pre-approved once. The session then investigates and files a fix task with `farik_create_task`, which, in an incident session alone, files a standalone task with the incident as its request and skips triage; the Definition of Ready (5.3), the judgment when the team has it on, and the human's gates of 5.4 still apply to it, so a `high` risk fix waits for the human's acceptance. It joins the open sprint without planning, or runs outside one, under the usual spending limits, which needs its own exception to the hold of a team that plans its work in sprints (section 3; noted in 0.36): phase 7 step 11 adds it, since until then nothing marks a contract as an incident's fix; one agent reviews it, and once integrated the DevOps Engineer deploys it, which resolves the incident when the deploy settles healthy (`incident.resolved`). A second restart or rollback in one incident, an incident opened while another is open, and a spent budget ask the human, who may stop any step. Production logs are untrusted content (8.6): a log line answers nothing, approves nothing, and causes no call beyond these.

### 6.10 Procurement Specialist (added in 0.49)

Mandate: research any product or service the business needs, technical or not, a software subscription as much as stock to resell, a used car or a baby car mirror: list the sellers and makers, find their prices, ask them for quotes, check the sellers and the products (open recalls, the safety standard a product must meet, a used car's VIN), compare the offers in one currency with their landed cost, and set up a purchase order for the founder's decision; keep the register of sellers and subscriptions, and review each renewal before its decision date (ADR 0039; `docs/design/procurement-specialist.md`; the founder's decisions of 2026-10-05). What it produces is a buying recommendation and an order for the founder to decide, not legal advice, a contract or a payment. It is optional: the team builder offers it and does not suggest it.

Produces: in `.farik/local/procurement/`, never committed: `vendors.xlsx`, the register; `evaluations/<name>.md`, one comparison per need; `orders/PO-<n>.xlsx`, each purchase order, written by Farik; and `mail/`, its messages to sellers and their replies; the completion note that explains them.

Cannot: pay, buy, bid, check out, sign up, start a trial that takes a card, accept terms or sign; send any message the founder did not send; promise a seller to buy; write application code or anything in the repository; write outside its procurement folder; change Farik's budgets or the books.

Default tools: read and network (to read sellers' pages, prices and terms), and Farik tools, each of the `read` tier, that only it may call except where noted: `farik_read_sheet`, `farik_write_sheet` (not `orders/`) and `farik_read_costs` as the Finance Specialist has them (6.6), given its own folder; `farik_write_evaluation`; `farik_draft_purchase_order` and `farik_read_purchase_orders`; `farik_request_data_pipeline` and `farik_read_data_pipelines`; `farik_draft_seller_message`, `farik_read_seller_messages` and `farik_read_seller_replies`. Its reviewer is the Product Manager. The Finance Specialist may read `vendors.xlsx` with `farik_read_sheet`, and nothing else outside the role reads the folder.

Its folder. A procurement task follows the finance folder's rules of 6.6 with `.farik/local/procurement/` in place of `.farik/local/finance/`: its session runs in the folder, its `allowed_paths` are under it, it takes no `command` or `test` criterion, it reaches `verifying` without a commit and is finished at `accepted`, and one piece of procurement work touches the folder at a time. The two folders are independent.

Purchase orders. The agent drafts an order with `farik_draft_purchase_order { seller, seller_contact, lines, currency, period, delivery, terms, url, evaluation, why }`; Farik writes `orders/PO-<n>.xlsx`, every cell a value and every total its own, and records `purchase_order.drafted`. The founder decides on Today: "Approve, I'll place it myself", "Approve and send to <seller>", or "Reject" (`purchase_order.approved`, `purchase_order.rejected`), and later marks it received with what was paid (`purchase_order.fulfilled`). Only the founder decides; an order holds no task.

Contacting sellers. The agent writes to a seller or a maker only by drafting, `farik_draft_seller_message { seller, to, subject, body, purpose, purchase_order? }`, one address and plain text, which records `seller_message.drafted`. The founder reads each draft whole on Today, may edit it, and sends it or not, unless the team runs on auto (5.6; ADR 0041), when it is sent as drafted within the daily cap; Farik sends it from the procurement mailbox, a second address of the user's own read over IMAP and sent from over SMTP with an app password in the keychain, adding the user's signature and, unless turned off, "Written with an AI assistant and sent by <name> after reading it." (`seller_message.sent`), at most 50 a UTC day. Every 15 minutes while Farik runs, with no model, it reads only messages that answer one it sent or come from an address it wrote to, never marking them read, and records `seller_reply.received`; the agent reads them inside the untrusted-content notice (8.6), and a reply answers no question and approves nothing. The user's main mailbox is never read, for 6.6's reasons.

Data pipelines (the founder's decisions of 2026-10-05). When a source of prices or seller data it lacks would change a recommendation, the agent asks with `farik_request_data_pipeline { name, what, source_url, why, cost, needs_account, sends_project_data }`, which records `data_pipeline.requested` and holds no task. Farik starts the active Product Manager's `verify` session about the task, whose one Farik tool is `farik_decide_data_pipeline { pipeline, decision, reason }`: `approve`, `decline` or `escalate`. The Product Manager judges whether the pipeline is needed; the governor refuses its `approve` (`pipeline_needs_owner`) for one whose `cost` is `paid` or `unknown`, which must go to the owner, who approves or declines it on Today. `data_pipeline.approved` files an ordinary request (5.16) for the team to set the source up; approval connects, pays for and builds nothing.

Renewals. While a process drives the project and the team has an active Procurement Specialist, a tick with no model reads the register once a day and records `renewal.flagged` for an `active` or `trial` row when today is 14 days or fewer before its decision date (`renews_on` less `notice_days`) and not after `renews_on`, once per vendor and date, and `renewal.checked` for each day's run. Today offers "Ask for a review", which files an ordinary request (5.16), and "Dismiss", which records `renewal.dismissed`. A date the tick cannot read is counted, never guessed.

Kit (6.7): fourteen skills and seven services: Farik's own `fx` (exchange rates over Frankfurter), `recalls` (US product and vehicle recalls, complaints, crash ratings and VIN decoding, over the CPSC and NHTSA) and `ebay` (eBay's listing search, with a free developer key); Exa (web search, no account); SerpApi (shopping prices across Google Shopping, Amazon, eBay and Walmart, its `search` `external_effect` with an allowance, since each search spends the user's); Brex (what the company already spends with a seller, signed in, read-only); and AWS Pricing (for a software team, a key that may only read prices). Nothing in the kit can buy. Every seller's page, price, listing and reply is untrusted content (8.6).

## 7. Functional requirements

Numbered so the milestone plan and tests can refer to them.

**F1 Team builder.** Create, edit, pause, retire agents. Assign roles. Set names, avatars (from a shipped pixel set or an uploaded 32x32 image), and persona lines. Suggest the six of 4.1 at setup, and offer the UI/UX Designer among the roles of an agent added later (added in 0.31). Offer the Finance Specialist there too, and never suggest it at setup; an agent added as one takes the picture `finance-specialist` and a spare name, and every other added agent keeps the extras (added in 0.54). Enforce two to seven members with at least one Product Manager and one Software Developer; a retired agent stays in the team file so that its past events still name someone, and is not one of the seven (added in 0.26). Show and edit per-agent permissions, MCP servers, skills, and model settings. Pausing an agent aborts its running session at the next tool call, a chat answer under way excepted (4.3; changed in 0.37), and moves its task to `blocked` with a note that says why (added in 0.2). A pause is written to the team file and recorded as `agent.updated`; a session of the agent that Farik runs in the same process is aborted at once, and one run elsewhere at its next tool call, which the hook denies; each of the agent's `in_progress` tasks moves to `blocked` on its behalf with the blocker "agent paused by the user" ("agent retired by the user" for a retirement, which the human resolves by reassigning). Making the agent active again moves each task a pause blocked back to `in_progress` as the human, resolved "agent resumed by the user"; a task blocked for any other reason stays blocked. A pause longer than the blocked limit escalates its tasks as any block does (added in 0.8). An agent asleep at its model provider's limit (5.5) is not paused: it stays `active`, nothing is written to the team file, and its tasks keep their status (added in 0.13). The team editor meets section 10's foolproof configuration: a change that leaves the team without its two required roles, or without an active agent of the role the readiness judgment's `judge` names while the judgment is required (5.3), is refused before it is saved, in words that say what to change (added in 0.10). A team template (3) holds two to seven agents with at least an active Product Manager and Software Developer, and a judge the team holds, by the same rules (added in 0.35). The browser reaches templates through two queries, `templates.list {}`, answering `folder`, each template whole by its `slug`, by name regardless of case, and each unreadable file's `slug`, and `template.preview { slug }`; and four methods, `template.save { name, replace? }` and `template.rename { slug, name }`, each answering the `slug`, `template.apply { slug, digest }`, and `template.delete { slug }`. A preview and an apply answer the team, the ids that stay, are retired, are removed and join, the change in words, the team's refusals (`errors`, the codes the team editor uses, such as `needs_developer`), and the `digest`, the sha256 of the template file's bytes as they were read; an apply whose result is refused is refused with those errors and writes nothing, and an apply given a digest the file no longer has is refused with `template_changed` at `/digest` and writes nothing (added in the 0.35 fix wave). A template refusal carries its `code` and `path`: `template_exists` at `/name`; `template_name` at `/name`, a name with no letter or digit; `template_unreadable` at `/slug`, a file that is not a template, which can be deleted but not previewed, used or renamed; and `no_state_folder` at `/`; a slug nothing is saved under is not found. The page words each refusal from its code, never from the daemon's message.

**F2 Projects.** Open an existing git repository or create a new one. Produce and store the project scan. Initialize `.farik/`. Detect the project's test and build commands and add them to the criterion library (F16).

**F3 Board.** Kanban view of the task lifecycle, with tasks grouped under their epics and standalone tasks on their own. Filter by agent, sprint, risk, epic. Open a task to see its contract, events, diff, notes, and cost. Create a request by hand (it enters as `draft` and is triaged; added in 0.3) and overrule a triage. On the command line (added in 0.8), `farik task show` gives each event a summary of what it said (a move's statuses, who asked and why; a triage's size and reason; a question and its answer; an escalation's reason; a criterion's result; a note's first line; a session's purpose; a cost), cut at 200 characters; what the task has cost against its budget, in dollars, sessions, and tokens; an epic's tasks; and with `--diff` its branch's diff against the integration branch, against the merge commit's first parent once it is integrated, which an epic, having no branch, and a task not yet assigned refuse. `farik task create <file> --parent <epic>` files a task under an epic in progress, as the human, triaged small at filing; it is judged against the Definition of Ready and assigned by the epic's assignee as the breakdown's tasks are. In the web app (added in 0.28), the board has six lanes, in order Planning (`draft`, `refining`, and an `escalated` task awaiting the human's approval of its plan), To do (`ready`, `assigned`), In progress (`in_progress`, `rejected`), Stuck (`blocked`, and any other `escalated` task), Review (`verifying`), and Done (`accepted`, and `cancelled` only when "Show cancelled" is on); a phone shows one lane at a time. Each row carries its assignee, its title linking to the task page, its id, and at most one mark: "Needs your help" or "Waiting on you" when the human is waited on, else what the working agent is doing (Writing, Building, Reviewing), else its status when it is accepted, cancelled, being reworked, or escalated; a row the lane already describes has none. The filters are by agent, "Only what is waiting on me", by epic (the epic and its tasks), by sprint (all, this sprint, no sprint), by risk, and "Show cancelled". Above the lanes the open sprint's line ("Sprint N is running: D of T tasks done") links to its page, with "End the sprint early"; with none open, "Start a sprint" asks for an optional spending limit and says who will plan it. The task page has five tabs (summary and checks, history in plain words with each event's kind, leaving out the tool calls a session makes but keeping one Farik refused, the plan, the code changes, and the notes, which are the latest note of each kind in the log, signed by its writer, falling back to the contract's own), its cost split into Planning, Building, Checking, and Meetings and talk against its limit, whether it was added to the project, and Stop and Cancel while they apply. A sprint's page shows who started and planned it, its tasks, its meetings (planning, standups, review, and the look back, each with its number of posts), and what it has spent against its limit. When the team plans its work in sprints (section 3; added in 0.36), the board has a seventh lane, Backlog, between Planning and To do, holding every row the daemon marks as in the Backlog, whatever its status, and shown only while the policy is on; on a phone it is one more lane. Its note reads "Ready work waits here until you start a sprint.", and while a sprint runs "Waits here for the next sprint.", which holds alike for work that became ready during the sprint, work an early end left, and work the sprint's planning passed over (the founder, 2026-10-01). A card in it reads "Ready", "Ready. Waits for the next sprint" while a sprint runs, or, for an epic with tasks, "Ready, broken into <n> tasks" ("1 task" for one), and the tasks of an epic in the Backlog show under their epic, not on their own. With no sprint open the sprint line reads "No sprint is running. Ready work waits in the Backlog until you start one.", and while one runs it adds "1 more waits in the Backlog for the next sprint." or "<n> more wait in the Backlog for the next sprint." "Start a sprint" lists the Backlog's rows with no parent under "Waiting in the Backlog", each with its id, title and "Task" or "Epic, <n> tasks" ("Epic, 1 task" for one), its planner line adding "Work that becomes ready later waits for the next sprint."; `/board?start=sprint` opens it. "End the sprint early", on the board and the sprint's page, says "<n> tasks are not finished. They leave the sprint and wait in the Backlog for the next one. A session already running finishes. <name> will still run the review and the look back." Today's team band counts the Backlog where the sprint line is: with no sprint open, "<n> pieces of work are ready and wait in the Backlog. Start a sprint to begin them." ("1 piece of work is ready and waits in the Backlog. Start a sprint to begin it."), "Start a sprint" linking to `/board?start=sprint`; while a sprint runs, the sprint line, which links to its page, then "<n> more wait in the Backlog for the next sprint."; and nothing when the Backlog is empty. The daemon works the Backlog out, never the page: `tasks.list`'s and `task.get`'s rows carry `backlog`, `false` whenever the policy is off, and the query `backlog.summary {}` answers `{ plan_in_sprints, count }`, `count` the Backlog's rows with no parent, so an epic counts once, `0` with the policy off; the pages learn the policy from it. `farik board` does not show the Backlog yet.

**F4 Contracts.** Editor for the contract schema with validation against the JSON schema. PM authoring flow. Definition of Ready results displayed inline. The web UI's plan editor adds and removes a requirement or an exit criterion, but never the last of either, and a removed requirement leaves every criterion's `satisfies` (changed in 0.37).

**F5 Governor.** Everything in section 5, exposed as a library with no UI dependency, with a test suite that covers every transition in the table and every budget.

**F6 Runtime.** Start, resume, and end agent sessions. Inject identity, memory, contract, and tools. Stream events to the UI. Compute cost per session from usage.

**F7 Channel.** Team chat with agent posts, user posts, mentions, ceremony threads, and a link from any message to the task or event it refers to. On the command line (added in 0.14), the user posts with `farik say <text>`, whose `@<id>` mentions start the agent's conversation session, and reads the channel with `farik channel [--last <n>] [--json]` (section 3); each message's JSON carries its `seq` and `task_id`, which are its link to the event and the task. A ceremony's messages are shown with their thread, `<time> <author> [<thread>] <text>` (5.9; changed in 0.15), and the channel's view comes with the web app (phase 6; changed in 0.18 from the desktop app in phase 5). In the web app (added in 0.29), the Channel page, between Board and Team on the rail (the rail's "Chats" from 0.34, where the channel is the chat list's first chat, "Team", at `/channel`, F8), reads the newest 100 messages through the query `channel.messages { before_seq?, limit }` (1 to 200, default 100; the newest page, oldest first within it), pages back with "Show earlier messages" (a page that cannot be read is said in plain words, and the button stays), and appends each new `message.posted` as it is recorded, without asking again, keeping it however many other events follow. An agent's post shows its avatar, name, role, and time; Farik's system lines are small and muted; the user's own posts are labelled "You". `@human` is shown as "@you" and an agent's mention as "@<Name>", highlighted; `FRK-n` in a text, and a message's `task_id`, link to the task, followed by "waiting on you" when the task waits on the user; a reply says "Replying to <name>". Ceremony messages sharing a thread and a UTC day form one block, "<Planning | Standup | Review | Looking back>, <weekday>: N people posted" (N counts authors), collapsed except today's standup, with the anchor `#thread-<thread>-<date>` that the sprint page's "Read it" links and the side panel "Meetings in this sprint" open, paging back as "Show earlier messages" does while the pages read are no older than the meeting's day; a meeting the channel no longer holds is said in plain words. "Post to the team" sends `message_post { text }`, and Enter sends while Shift+Enter makes a new line (added in 0.34); typing `@` offers the team's agents, and a text over 2000 characters, counted in code points, is refused before it is sent. Today's "In the channel" shows, from the newest 20 messages, the last two that are not Farik's own lines, then "Open the channel".

**F8 One-on-one.** Direct conversation with an agent, read-only with respect to the project, with an offer to file a request (5.16). In the web app (added in 0.34; ADR 0026), "Chats" on the rail opens a list, `chats.list {}`: Team first, the channel (F7), then each agent in team order with its avatar, name, role and its chat's last line, and the agents that have retired under "Past teammates", their chats kept to read and with no box; on a phone the list is one row of faces above the open chat, Team first and past teammates last. `/channel/<agent_id>` opens that agent's chat, "Talking with <Name>" with a line saying the agent answers from memory and from reading the project and changes nothing, and "Back to Team"; an id that names no agent shows "No such teammate". The chat is read through `chat.messages { agent_id, before_seq?, limit }` (1 to 200, default 100; oldest first within the page), the user's lines labelled "You", line breaks kept, and the agent's text shown as text. "Message <Name>" sends `chat_message_post { agent_id, text }`, which has no author: Farik writes `human`. Enter sends and Shift+Enter makes a new line, and a text over 4,000 characters, counted in code points, is refused before it is sent. A reply that proposes a request shows it in an editable box, its title and text, with "Send as a request", which calls `request.file { text, from_chat_message }`: the link must name an agent's reply that proposes a request not already sent, checked and filed under the one lock every filing takes, so two tabs cannot send one proposal twice; otherwise the call is refused with a sentence. The reply then shows "Sent as FRK-n", linked, from `chat.messages`' `sent_as` or from the `task.created` that names it. A new message of the open chat is appended as it is recorded, and the chat asks again on its agent's `chat` session's start and on any end of its agent's sessions. `chat.messages`' `waiting` says why the user's newest message has no answer yet, the first that applies: `retired`; `key_refused`, the team paused for the key ("<Name> cannot answer: your AI provider refused the key.", linking to Settings); `day_spent` ("Today's spending limit is reached, so <Name> will answer tomorrow. Your message is kept.", linking to Costs); `asleep`, with `until`; `answering`, pending or a session running ("<Name> is thinking…"); and `no_answer`, a session that ended without a reply ("<Name> could not answer. Ask again."). No event is written for a chat that did not run. An agent the user has chatted with is retired from the Team page, not removed, so its chat stays under "Past teammates".

**F9 MCP and skills.** Per-agent configuration of MCP servers (stdio and remote), tool tagging (5.6; changed in 0.38, from by tier), credential storage in the OS keychain, or in a private file on a computer without one (changed in 0.38). Per-agent, per-role, and team-wide skill folders (changed in 0.40; 6.7, ADR 0034). Skills are given through three commands, `skill_save`, `skill_remove` and `skill_confirm` (6.7), which the browser sends as `command`, and read through two queries: `skills.list { agent }`, which answers `{ skills: [{ level, name, description, state, bytes }] }`, the agent's skills as 6.7 computes them, `level` `role`, `team` or `agent` and `state` `in_use`, `replaced`, `review` or `missing`, role rows read-only, and an agent the team does not have is `not_found`; and `skill.get { level, agent?, role?, name }`, which answers `{ files, sha256, ignored_fields }`, `files` mapping each path to its text as the folder holds it (a file that is not UTF-8 text is refused with `skill_file_not_text`, as `skill_save`, `skill_confirm` and the checks refuse it, so the text shown is the text hashed; changed in 0.43), `sha256` the folder's `skill_sha256` that `skill_confirm` is given, and `ignored_fields` the frontmatter keys Farik drops, a folder that does not exist being `not_found` and one the checks refuse being refused with that refusal's `skill_*` code at the start of its message. A `role` level, `{ level: "role", role, name }`, answers the `SKILL.md` Farik ships for that role as `{ files: { "SKILL.md": text }, ignored_fields: [] }` with no `sha256`, since a shipped skill is trusted and pinned in the program; `role` and `name` are checked before anything is looked up, a role Farik does not ship or a bad name being refused and a name the role does not ship `not_found` (added in 0.43). `farik skill list [--agent <id>]` prints one line per skill, `<level>  <name>  <state>`, the states as `in use`, `replaced`, `review` and `missing`, the team's alone without `--agent`; `farik skill show <name> (--team | --agent <id>)` prints every file, escaped, and the hash; `farik skill add <folder> (--team | --agent <id>) [--yes] [--replace]` reads and checks the folder in its own process, prints what `show` prints, and on a terminal asks "Add <name> for <whom>? [y/N]", while without a terminal it needs `--yes`, a shipped name needing `--replace`; `farik skill remove <name> (--team | --agent <id>)`; and `farik skill confirm <name> (--team | --agent <id>) <hash> [--replace]`, the hash all of what `show` printed or its first 12 hex digits. The agent page's Skills section (added in 0.42) lists the rows of `skills.list` in three groups, "Comes with <role>" (read-only, each with its description), "For the whole team" and "Just for <Name>", each team or own row with "Edit" and "Remove". A `review` row says "Changed in the project. Review before <Name> uses it" with "Review"; a `replaced` team row says, muted, "<Name>'s own <skill> replaces this one for <Name>", and a `replaced` role row "Your team's <skill> replaces this one", or "<Name>'s own <skill> replaces this one" when the agent's own skill is the one in use; a `missing` row says "<skill> is in the team file but its folder is gone" with "Remove". "Remove" asks "Remove <skill>?", saying the agent (or the whole team) stops using it and its folder is deleted from the project, with "Keep it" and "Remove <skill>", and sends `skill_remove`. After any skill command the page reads `skills.list` and the team again, so a later Save cannot write back pins it did not know of. "Add a skill" and "Edit" open `SkillEdit`: who it is for ("Just <Name>" or "Everyone on the team"), a name, "When should <Name> use it?" and the instructions, or "Upload a SKILL.md instead", which replaces them with the file's text in one field. Edit reads the folder with `skill.get`; a skill whose `SKILL.md` holds anything but its `name` and `description` (any `ignored_fields`, or a frontmatter the dialog would not write back byte for byte) opens as its whole file, so nothing the person did not see is dropped, and a skill's other files are listed by name and size and sent back as they are. The dialog writes `SKILL.md` as `---`, `name: <name>`, `description: <a JSON string>`, `---`, then the instructions. A changed name, or a changed "Who is it for?", saves a new skill and leaves the old one, which the dialog says. "Next" shows every file of the skill, each in its own `untrusted` frame under its path, with "Farik will follow these instructions and use these files. Read them before adding." and, when there are any, "Farik ignores: <fields>"; "Add skill" is only there and sends `skill_save` with every file. When the name is one the role ships it adds "This replaces <role>'s own <skill> for <whom>." and the button reads "Replace and add skill", which alone sends `replace_shipped`. A name another role ships, which the page does not know until the daemon refuses it with `skill_name_taken`, stays on this step and adds "This replaces the skill Farik comes with of that name, <skill>, for <whom>." with the same button (changed in 0.43). A name quoted in an uploaded file is read without its quotes. A refusal returns to the first step, said under its field and in Farik's words, never the daemon's: `skill_runs_commands`, `skill_attaches_files`, `skill_too_large` and `skill_file_not_text` under the instructions, the name's and the description's under theirs. "Review" opens `SkillReview`, which reads the folder with `skill.get` and shows every file in an `untrusted` frame under its path, then the files with their sizes, with "Use this skill", which sends `skill_confirm` with the `sha256` it showed (and `replace_shipped` for a shipped name, with the same sentence, which for a name another role ships appears once the daemon has refused it with `skill_name_taken`), and "Remove"; a folder `skill.get` refuses shows "This skill can't be used: <reason>." with "Remove" alone. A role's own skill has "Read" (added in 0.43), which opens `skill.get`'s role level read-only: its text as shipped, with no `untrusted` frame, since Farik ships it, and "Close". In the web app (added in 0.33), the agent page's Connectors section switches the built-in Playwright connector on and off for the agent (5.6), showing the change before it is saved. A user's own servers (added in 0.38; 6.7, ADR 0030). The browser reaches them through three methods: `connector.tools { agent, server, keys }`, which lists the server's tools with the agent's keys and keeps nothing, answering `{ tools: [{ name, description, usable }] }`; `connector.connect { agent, server, keys, tags }`, which lists them again, labels each usable tool by `tags` or else `external_effect`, refusing a tag for any other name (`tag_unknown_tool`), keeps only the keys the entry names, and handles `connector_connect`, answering `{ stored_in: keychain | file, tools }`; and `connector.disconnect { agent, server }`, which handles `connector_disconnect` and then deletes that agent's keys, answering `{}`. `server` is the entry as 6.7 describes it, without `source` and `tools`; the keys travel to the daemon, which keeps them as `account.connect` does, and a refusal of either method that carries keys has no `data`. A refusal of the entry carries the team's errors, each at its field. `team.get` answers `connectors` beside `team`: each agent's custom server with its `state`, `connected` when the definition kept on this machine is the team file's and every key it names is kept, `connect_again` when none is kept or the team file changed since, and `store_unavailable` when the keychain or the private file could not be read the last time it was, and, whenever keys are kept for it, `stored_in`, `keychain` or `file`; the daemon reads the store once per connect and disconnect, at session setup, and the first time a server is asked about, so a keychain is not asked at every `team.get`. It also answers `sandboxed`, whether sessions run in Docker's sandbox (added in 0.38 after review): true when the project's sandbox setting is `docker` (8.3), whether or not Docker answers at that moment, since a session does not start without it, and false in no-sandbox mode. It is read from the setting and never by asking Docker, so that `team.get`, which Today asks first, is not held up behind `docker info` (changed in 0.63). The agent page lists them under "Added by you" ("connectors Farik has not checked; you labelled their tools"), each with "Started by a command on this computer" or "Reached at a web address", where its keys are kept when some are ("<Name>'s keys are in your keychain" or "in a private file on this computer"), and its tools counted by label. A `connect_again` row says "Connect again to use it", that its settings in the project changed since it was connected on this computer, and offers "Connect again", which opens `ConnectorAdd` filled in from the team file with the keys empty, every header on a line of its own, "Where the key goes" first, each one changeable and removable (changed in 0.38 after review, from the others sent unseen); a `store_unavailable` row says "Farik cannot read <Name>'s keys for <server> right now", with a note to choose "Always" when the computer asks whether Farik may use the keychain, and offers no Connect again. "Remove" asks "Remove <server> from <Name>?", saying Farik deletes the keys from the keychain or the private file that keeps them, with "Keep it" and "Remove <server>", and says a refusal in plain words. Under Advanced, "A custom connector" says Farik has not checked it, so the user labels each of its tools, that a connector started by a command runs on this computer with the user's rights, and that it runs in a folder Farik keeps for it, so a file of the user's is given by its full path, with "Add a custom connector". `ConnectorAdd` is a dialog of three steps. "How to start it": the name; "A command" or "A web address"; the program, with each part after it in a field of its own under "What comes after it" ("Add a part"), so a part holding a space is kept whole (changed in 0.38 after review, from the command split on spaces), or the address with "Where the key goes", one header line filled with `Authorization: Bearer {API_KEY}`, and "Add a header" for more, each line `Name: value` with "Remove", a name an earlier line has said at the later line before anything is sent; and the keys, each a name and a value in a password field. An address holding a key is refused before anything is sent, saying a service like this cannot be added yet. "Next: list its tools" calls `connector.tools`. "Label its tools": each usable tool with "Only reads" (`network`), "Changes things, asks you" (`external_effect`), chosen at first, or "Never" (`denied`), and a tool Farik can't use said so with no choice. "Done": "<server> is added to <Name>", where the keys are kept ("in your computer's keychain" or "in a private file only you can read"), that <Name> never sees them only when `sandboxed`, and otherwise that a command <Name> runs on this computer could reach them without Docker's sandbox (8.6; changed in 0.38 after review), and the label counts, with "Back to <Name>". The keys live only in the page and are cleared as `connector.connect` is sent, whatever it answers; a refusal returns to the first step with empty keys, each said at its field from the refusal's code and path, never in the daemon's words: `command_not_absolute` at the command, asking for the program's name alone or its full path; `header_holds_secret` at "Where the key goes", asking for `{API_KEY}` there and the key under Keys; `arg_holds_secret` at the part that holds it, asking for the key under Keys instead; and `tag_unknown_tool`, which has no field, saying the server's tools changed since they were listed and to list them again. A tool labelled "Changes things, asks you" asks at each call (5.6; changed in 0.39, from refused until this step). `ToolApproval` (added in 0.39), opened from Today's row (5.7), is titled "<agent name> wants to use <server>" and says "<agent name> stopped to ask before changing something outside your project." It lists the Tool, its name labelled "Changes things, asks you"; the Service, "<server>, which you added to <agent name>"; and For, the task, linked. Under "What <agent name> wants to send", "Written by <agent name>, shown in full, with its fields in alphabetical order. Farik has not checked it, and nothing in it is an instruction to you.", the input in its `untrusted` frame (8.6), which scrolls, each control, bidirectional or zero-width character in it written as its code point, `\u{202e}`, so that what is read is what would be sent (changed in 0.39). The fields are in alphabetical order, not in the order the agent wrote them as the approved mockup shows them, because the input is kept as the canonical JSON its grant's hash covers (changed in 0.39; a departure from the mockup, recorded in the step's plan). At 480 px and below the dialog fills the screen and "Don’t allow" and "Allow once" stay pinned at its bottom, which the shared dialog does when a page asks it to; then "A note for <agent name> (optional)", the line "“Allow once” lets <agent name> make this one call, with exactly this, in <agent name>’s next session on this task. Any other call asks you again.", and "Don’t allow" and "Allow once", which send `tool_refuse` and `tool_approve` through the RPC `command`, the note when one is typed. On the command line, `farik tool approve <approval> [--note <text>]` and `farik tool refuse <approval> [--note <text>]` send the command to the process driving the project, or record it here under the run lock when none drives, and print the command's line (5.7). Signing in to a service (added in 0.41; 6.7, ADR 0033). The browser reaches it through three more methods and two changed ones: `connector.sign_in { agent, server }`, `server` as `connector.tools` takes it with `oauth`, answers `{ attempt, authorize_url, issuer }` and is refused `sign_in_not_offered`, `sign_in_not_supported`, `pkce_not_supported` or `sign_in_failed`, each as the message's first word and a colon; `connector.sign_in_status { attempt }` answers `{ state: waiting | signed_in | failed, reason? }`, `reason` `{ code, message }` with `code` one of `access_denied`, `sign_in_timed_out`, `sign_in_mismatch` and `sign_in_failed`, the message Farik's words alone; `connector.sign_in_cancel { attempt }` ends the attempt, the page's Cancel, and answers `{}` whether or not it was still there; and `connector.tools` and `connector.connect` take `attempt` in place of `keys` when `server` has `oauth`, refused `sign_in_needed` without one and `sign_in_unknown` for one that is unknown, used, expired, unfinished, or made for another agent, name, address or `oauth`, which also ends it, `connect` using it up and putting it back if it fails before the grant is kept. An attempt lives in the daemon's memory for ten minutes and no answer carries a token. `team.get`'s `connectors` rows gain `auth`, `keys` or `oauth`, `revokes`, for a signed-in row whether the service can be asked to forget the grant, and the state `sign_in_again`. `connector.connect` answers as before and `connector.connected` carries `issuer`. A role's kit on the agent page (added in 0.44; 6.7, ADR 0036). `team.get` answers `kits`, each role on the team (not retired) whose kit has a `stdio` or `http` service, `{ role, connectors: [{ name, title, about, why, setup, key_page?, labels, auth, credential_keys }] }`, no `container` connector among them; each `connectors` row gains `source`, `custom` or `kit`, and its `state` may be `not_in_kit`. `connector.tools`, `connector.sign_in` and `connector.connect` take `server` as `{ name, source: "kit" }`, a form of its own with nothing else in it, so a browser cannot send fields the kit decides, and `connector.connect` then takes `tags: {}`. The agent page's Connectors section shows "From the <role>'s kit" ("services Farik checked; each tool is already labelled") above "Added by you": each service's title and why, with "Connect", or "Connected" and "Remove", or, when the kit changed since, "Farik updated this service. Connect it again to keep using it." with "Connect again" and "Remove" (or "Sign in again"), or, for a service the kit no longer has, "Farik no longer offers this service" with "Remove" alone. "Connect" opens `ConnectorAdd` from the kit, a dialog of two steps, "Connect" and "Done", with no labelling step: the service's about, "Why <Name> wants it" and "What to do" (its setup, which may quote the service's own label), "Get your key" opening the key page in a new tab, and one field per key, "Your <Service> key", "For <Name> only, and never shown again. Farik checks it with <Service> before keeping it.", or, for a service that signs in, "Sign in with <Service>", whose page is asked for when the dialog opens so the click opens it, and a finished sign-in connects at once. "Done" lists the tools by their kit label, or by their name with `_` and `-` read as spaces, in "<Name> can now" (`network`), "<Name> asks you first before" (`external_effect`) and "Farik never offers" (`denied`), where the key or the sign-in is kept, and "Only <Name> has <Service>. To give it to someone else, connect it from their page." Saving the agent keeps a kit connector connected meanwhile, as it keeps a custom one. A kit connector's spending tools carry allowances (6.7; ADR 0037). A service that takes a public client but registers none is signed in to with an app of Farik's own (6.7; added in 0.58): `connector.sign_in` then also answers `provider`, `user_code` and `install_url`, and `team.get`'s connector rows `provider` and `settings_url`.

**F10 Pixel office.** A single scene: desks, agents, a meeting table for ceremonies, a door for the user. Agents move between desk, table, and a whiteboard based on state. Clicking an agent opens its panel. The scene is decorative and informative, never the only way to do anything; every action is also reachable from the board.

**F11 Audit.** Event log viewer with filters. Export as JSON lines. Cost report per task, agent, sprint. Replay an exported log into a fresh set of projections, for debugging and for demonstrations (added in 0.2).

**F12 Notifications.** Notifications for escalations and sprint boundaries, from the desktop app, and from the web app when the user's browser allows them (changed in 0.18). Configurable quiet hours.

**F13 Premium hooks.** License check, hosted-run toggle, and cloud sync are stubs in the open-source build. They must be present so the premium build is the same codebase with features enabled, not a fork.

**F14 Contract authoring assistant (added in 0.2).** Write an epic or a standalone task contract alone, with the Product Manager, or from a brief or issue link, with the Product Manager's questions answered first, Definition of Ready results shown live, criteria from the library, a lock that makes the contract human-owned (5.11, 5.13), and the approval that lets it be broken down (5.16). Questions from agents are shown and answered in the same place (5.7).

**F15 Team rules (added in 0.2).** Edit the rules in 5.12 from the team editor and the command line; every refusal they cause names the rule. The editor also edits the readiness judgment's policy (5.3), and every setting it edits meets section 10's foolproof configuration (added in 0.10).

**F16 Criterion library (added in 0.2).** List, add, edit, and remove named criteria in `.farik/team/criteria.yaml`; seed it from the project scan; reference criteria by name when authoring.

**F17 Harness metrics (added in 0.2).** Compute from the log, per project and per sprint: first-pass acceptance rate, human interventions per accepted task, cost per accepted task split by session purpose (refine, implement, verify, ceremony, conversation), the share of exit criteria verified by command or test rather than by review or human, and active weeks. Shown on the command line and in the app; these are the five numbers `docs/PRODUCT_ANALYSIS.md` says to track from Milestone 0.
