The web UI (added in 0.18; changed in 0.23, ADR 0021). The daemon serves the web app on 127.0.0.1 alone. The browser never holds the daemon's token. `farik serve` prints, and opens, a link carrying a one-time code; the page trades the code for a browser session, set as an `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict` cookie, and a second use of the code is refused, so a link left in a terminal's scrollback or a browser's history gives nobody anything. A code whose session cannot be kept, because the sessions file cannot be written, is not spent, so the same link opens once the failure is fixed (changed in 0.37). A used link opened in a browser whose session is live opens the app rather than the connect page (added in 0.23). A browser session lasts 30 days, so a page reconnects by itself after Farik restarts; it is kept hashed in the user's Farik state folder, readable by its owner alone, and the user revokes it from Settings. The browser routes, `POST /connect` and the WebSocket's upgrade at `/rpc`, refuse a request whose `Origin` is not exactly `http://127.0.0.1:<port>` or whose `Host` is not exactly `127.0.0.1:<port>`, the daemon's own port in both, and a request with no `Origin`, so that a web page the user opens elsewhere, on another local port among them, or one that rebinds a name of its own to 127.0.0.1, cannot drive the team with the user's authority; every other route needs the daemon's token instead (changed in 0.23). The page and asset GETs (`/` and any other path, which falls back to `index.html`) are checked on `Host` alone, because a navigation sends no `Origin`, and they answer 404 without a web state, like the other browser routes (added in 0.23). Every page and asset carries `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'self'; connect-src 'self' ws://127.0.0.1:<port>; img-src 'self' data:; font-src 'self' data:; style-src 'self'; frame-ancestors 'none'` (`frame-ancestors 'none'` stops another page framing the app, clickjacking), `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, and `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` (added in 0.23). Every text file among them, the page included, names its charset, `charset=utf-8` (added in 0.23). `GET /session` answers 204 when the session cookie is live and 401 otherwise; it is checked on `Host` and refuses an `Origin` only when one is present and foreign, because a same-origin GET sends none (added in 0.23). `POST /disconnect` takes the full Origin and Host check, revokes the session the cookie carries, and clears the cookie (added in 0.23). The event stream closes with code 1011 after three failed reads of the event log in a row, so the page shows the failure and reconnects instead of stalling (added in 0.23). Two residuals remain: the browser sends the session cookie to any server on 127.0.0.1, whatever its port, because cookies are scoped by host; and a program that is not a browser can send any `Origin` and `Host` it likes. So the session cookie, like `daemon.json`, protects against web pages, not against other programs of the same user (added in 0.23). The folder browser lists folders, never files, and only under the user's home directory. The model credential the first run collects is kept in the OS keychain, as a connector's keys are, or in a private file where the computer has none (6.7, 8.6; changed in 0.38). Settings shows the AI account: which kind of credential is connected and where it is kept. Disconnect deletes it from every store that holds it, the keychain and the private file, and pauses the team if it was running, since the running driver keeps the key it already loaded; the user starts Farik again to connect another, except while the provider refuses the key, when the row takes a new one (below). A credential read from an environment variable cannot be removed by Farik, so the row names the variable instead of offering Disconnect (added in 0.26). While the team is paused because the provider refused the key (5.5), `account.status` says `key_refused`, and the row says the key did not work and asks for a new one: `account.connect`, which a project's daemon answers too, keeps it as setup does, puts it in place for the next session without a restart, so that `serve.status` names the kind now in use, and resumes the team; over a credential from an environment variable it is refused, naming the variable (added in 0.30). The browser words Farik's line about a move (5.9) as the task's history does, naming who moved which task, linked, to which status, with the reason when there is one; a line of any other shape is shown as written, and the log, `farik channel` and the line's format are unchanged (added in 0.30). A browser session carries the human's authority, as the command line does: from Advanced it may add a check to the criterion library, and a check whose method is a command is one Farik runs (added in 0.26).

The Designer's browser (added in 0.33; ADR 0026, the founder's decisions of 2026-09-30). The Designer's browsing is limited to the project's preview. `prepare` runs the project's install and build with the network on, as the founder decided, and the browser's confinement does not cover it. What the browser shows and what a page check reports are untrusted content: the check's problems reach the agent in an `untrusted` block, and the diff in the design review's message is untrusted too. axe runs in a script world of its own, which the page's scripts cannot reach, so a page that fakes axe or the DOM's own methods still has its problems found (changed in the step's fix wave). In a build with the `e2e` feature alone (8.3), `farik-e2e-serve --preview` also admits a browser whose `Host` is exactly `localhost:<port>` and whose `Origin`, when there is one, is exactly `http://localhost:<port>`, the server's own port in both, and `GET /` from it opens a browser session without a code, so that a Designer's browser in a preview can open Farik itself; any other host, `localhost` on another port among them, is still refused. `--preview` implies `--no-keychain`, a temporary configuration folder, and a temporary copy of a recorded team as the project, so that a browser with no code never shares a daemon with a real credential.

## 9. Open source and premium

The repository is one codebase under Apache 2.0, with premium code in an `ee/` directory under a separate commercial license, following the GitLab and PostHog pattern. Everything in section 5 is Apache 2.0 without exception; a governance layer that people cannot audit is not one they will trust with their repositories.

Free, forever: the full harness, all six roles and their kits (6.7), the pixel office, local execution with the user's own API key, MCP and skills, the audit log.

Premium, in rough priority order: hosted execution with included model credits so the user does not need a key; cloud sync so a team can be opened from more than one machine; cost analytics and history beyond thirty days; additional office themes and avatar packs; priority support; and, later, multi-user teams and SSO.

Farik Cloud, from the web launch, has a free tier that signs users in and does nothing else: it holds Farik's own sign-in apps, a GitHub App and a Google app, and their secrets, server side, and a user signs in to it with a Farik account, once per computer (6.7, 8.6; ADR 0044; changed in 0.61 from 0.60's "until the premium phase, every login is the user's", ADR 0043). No build carries a credential of Farik's. The premium phase is Farik Cloud's paid tier: hosted execution and the rest of the list above, with the same apps.

Nothing that makes the agents safer or more controllable is ever premium.

## 10. Non-functional requirements

- The governor evaluates a tool call in under five milliseconds; it is on the hot path of every action.
- The UI stays responsive with ten thousand events in a project. Projections, not raw log scans, back every view.
- The shipped models are chosen so that a first day of the suggested team of six running a full sprint costs, by estimate, about twenty-five dollars on the user's key at current list prices; this is a property of the defaults, not a cap (ADR 0015). This number must be re-derived when prices change and is stated, as an estimate, in the setup screen and on the Team page, as the suggested six's with "A smaller team costs less." after it, since a team started from a saved one or from scratch is often smaller (added in the 0.35 fix wave). (Changed in 0.31: the earlier figure, under twenty dollars for a team of five, dates from revision 0.1 and the repository records no derivation of it, so the six's figure is that one scaled by six agents to five, twenty-four dollars, rounded up; the UI/UX Designer runs on the Developer's model and adds an `explore` and a decision session to each of its tasks, so the scaling may understate it, and a measured first day should replace it. Since a scaled figure bounds nothing, the setup screen says "about", with "(an estimate)", not "under".)
- Everything works offline except model calls and MCP servers that need the network.
- All user-facing text is in English for the first release, with strings externalized.
- Configuring the harness is user-friendly and foolproof (added in 0.10). Every setting has a safe default and a one-line explanation in plain language. A change that would leave the team unable to work, such as a judge role no active agent holds, an empty rubric with the judgment required (5.3), or a team without a Product Manager and a Software Developer (F1), is refused before it is saved, in words that say what to change; the daemon names each refusal by a code that the page words for a person, so a schema's own text never reaches the screen (changed in 0.26). Every refusal the web app shows is a plain sentence of its own, worded from the refusal's code, or, for a setup refusal that carries none, from the daemon's sentence it knows, with one plain sentence for any other; the daemon's or a parser's own text never shows (changed in 0.37). Each change shows its effect before it is saved, and every setting can be put back to its default: the defaults are the values `farik init` writes, which the daemon answers to the page, and each page that changes a setting offers "Put back the default" beside it (changed in 0.26). Setup's questions stay changeable after setup, from Settings, the same controls saved the same way (added in 0.27). The web UI does this by asking the daemon to validate the change and name its effects, in plain sentences such as "Ari may now run commands", "Finished work waits for you to merge it" or "The team may spend up to $10 a day", which the page lists before Save (added in 0.26). The page works out none of the team's rules itself: the daemon answers, for the team as it is and for a change before it is saved, each agent's model and effort as its sessions run them, in words (a model older than its family's newest is marked older), the tiers it holds and those its role and the team's permission answers give it, and who checks plans under each choice of judge; the page shows what each agent may do from those tiers.
- Every screen meets WCAG 2.2 AA (added in 0.18).
- The web app is responsive from 360 px wide (added in 0.18). It is not a phone app: the phone apps are native (8.1).
- Every client is built from the brand's design tokens in `@farik/brand` (section 14), so no colour, type size, or spacing is written into a client by hand (added in 0.18).
- The pixel scene renders at 60 frames per second on a 2020 laptop with integrated graphics, and can be disabled entirely for users who want only the board.

## 11. Milestones

Milestone 0, the harness. Four to five weeks. `core`, `store`, `runtime` with one adapter, a command-line interface, and two roles: Product Manager and Developer, the latter instantiated twice so that one Developer reviews the other (5.1). Exit criterion: on Farik's own repository (private until the open-source launch; decided 2026-09-15, and moot since the repository was found public on 2026-09-27), the PM writes contracts for three real issues, one Developer implements them, the other verifies, the PM accepts, and a human reviews the diffs and the event log and agrees each task was done as contracted. No UI. This milestone exists to find out whether the governance loop works before anyone draws a pixel. Its live run was not held at the command line: the founder decided on 2026-09-24 that all live testing is done in the web UI once the product side is built, so Milestone 0's exit is run in the browser together with Milestone 1's team sprint (changed in 0.18).

Milestone 1, the team. Five to six weeks. All five roles, the channel, ceremonies, and then, once the brand is recorded (section 14), the web UI covering the whole working loop, the one-to-one chats among it (moved from Milestone 2 in 0.34; ADR 0026) (changed in 0.18; the desktop shell moved to Milestone 3 in 0.20). Exit criterion: a new user with no help can go from nothing to an accepted task on their own repository inside thirty minutes, measured with five test users, at least three of them non-technical. The test is run in the browser (changed in 0.18 and 0.20).

Milestone 2, the ecosystem and launch. Six to eight weeks (four to five until 0.22). Other providers and agent engines, with every engine under the same governor (phase 8; added in 0.24, ADR 0023; renumbered by ADR 0029); per-agent MCP and skills UI, the Finance Specialist (added in 0.21), the role kits that equip every role with its skills and connectors (6.7; added in 0.22), memory (the one-on-one conversations moved to Milestone 1 in 0.34), the audit viewer, notifications. Public open-source launch at the end of this milestone, on the web alone (changed in 0.18 and 0.20), once a pre-registered, blind benchmark shows a Farik team at least as good as one fully equipped agent on quality and clearly better on failure rates and cost control (phase 10; added by ADR 0025).

Milestone 3, desktop (added in 0.20). Starts only when the web release is complete and tested: the founder and at least three non-technical testers run the released feature set in the browser, and the founder signs off the record. Then the Finance Specialist's receipts intake, as the first release after that check (added in 0.21); then the Tauri shell with the daemon in-process, the office scene, native notifications, and a desktop release. Exit criterion: the Milestone 1 loop repeated in the desktop app.

Milestone 4, mobile (added in 0.18; Milestone 3 until 0.20). Native iOS and Android apps, each in the platform's own toolkit, after the desktop app has shipped. How a phone reaches a running Farik is open (section 12, question 8), and the answer may make this milestone follow premium rather than precede it.

Milestone 5, premium (Milestone 3 until 0.18, Milestone 4 until 0.20). Hosted execution on the same event protocol, license and billing, cloud sync. Timing depends on what the open-source launch teaches.

## 12. Open questions

Recorded here so they are decided on purpose.

1. Resolved 2026-09-15: human acceptance of contracts is a team policy, `human_accepts_contracts`, with values `high_risk` (the default) and `all`. A "training wheels" period is the user switching it to `all` for a while.
2. Resolved 2026-09-15: a sprint has no length; it ends when its tasks are done (section 3).
3. Resolved 2026-09-15: the board and task detail show cost; the scene does not.
4. Resolved 2026-09-15: no-sandbox mode ships, with a warning on every run and in the setup screen.
5. Resolved 2026-09-15: the register is kept minimal (5.9), and the channel is instrumented so the choice can be revisited with data.
6. A second model provider. Not before someone asks, but the adapter interface is there.
7. The brand (added in 0.18): the logo, the palette, the type, the slogans, the voice, and whether the office scene's pixel-art look extends beyond the scene. It is the founder's to supply, and phase 5 records it (section 14). No implementation after phase 4 starts until it is recorded.
8. How a phone app reaches a running Farik (added in 0.18), since a phone does not run agents: the user's own desktop Farik over a secure link, or the hosted tier. The mobile phase decides (phase 14).

## 13. Glossary

Epic: the contract that translates one request from the user, approved by the user before it is broken into tasks (5.16). Contract: the document that makes an epic or a task ready. Locked contract: a contract the human owns and agents cannot change (5.11). Team rule: a constraint the human sets once and the governor applies to every contract and tool call (5.12). Criterion library: named, reusable exit criteria (5.13). Worktree: a task's own checkout of the repository (5.14). Exit criterion: a check that must pass for a task to be accepted. Governor: the deterministic enforcement layer. Ceremony: a structured team conversation (planning, standup, review, retro). Tier: a permission capability. ADR: architecture decision record. MCP: Model Context Protocol, the open standard for connecting tools to models. Skill: a folder of instructions an agent can load, following the Agent Skills format. Human gate: a point where the work waits for the human, a contract's approval or a result's acceptance, shown with a plain-language summary, the diff, and Farik's check results (5.4; added in 0.18). Design token: a named value of the brand, a colour, a type size, a spacing, that every client reads from `@farik/brand` (section 14; added in 0.18).

## 14. Brand (added in 0.18)

The brand is recorded in `docs/brand/brand.md` (founder, 2026-09-26; palette changed 2026-09-28). This section says where it goes and what it governs.

The name is Farik.

The logo, the colour palette, the type, the slogans, and the voice are the founder's to supply. They are recorded in `docs/brand/` as the guidelines people read: logo usage and clear space, the palette and each colour's role, type, voice and tone, the slogans, and what not to do.

The assets and the design tokens live in `packages/brand` (`@farik/brand`): the logo, the app icons each client needs, and the tokens (colour, type scale, spacing, radius) in a light and a dark theme. Every client uses them, the web app, the desktop app, and later the iOS and Android apps, so that one brand holds from the first screen (section 10). Every colour pair the tokens use for text meets WCAG 2.2 AA contrast, and no two jobs share a colour.

The office scene (F10) is pixel art. Whether that look extends beyond the scene, to the rest of the web and desktop apps, is the brand's decision.

The provisional design system the project plan held (D13) is superseded by the brand (ADR 0016).
